    AWS Certified Generative AI Developer – Professional (AIP-C01)

    Domain 1: Foundation Model Integration, Data Management, and Compliance

    A read-through study guide for the largest exam domain, 31% of scored questions. The official exam guide lists what to know. This guide explains it, in the order you would build it.

    ChapterExam taskWhat you will be able to do
    OrientationPrerequisiteNavigate Bedrock, regions, IAM, model access, and the two ways to call a model
    11.1 Analyze requirements and design GenAI solutionsTurn a business need into an architecture, prove it with a PoC, standardize it
    21.2 Select and configure FMsPick a model, swap it without redeploying, survive outages, manage custom models
    Project 1Build after Chapters 1 and 2A resilient model gateway with config-driven routing, fallback, and cost attribution
    31.3 Data validation and processing pipelinesValidate, transform, and format data of any modality for model consumption
    41.4 Vector store solutionsChoose and design a vector store, model its metadata, keep it fresh
    51.5 Retrieval mechanismsChunk, embed, search, rerank, and expose retrieval to a model
    Project 2Build after Chapters 3 to 5A multimodal document platform: ingest, store, retrieve, rerank, and serve through MCP
    61.6 Prompt engineering and governanceManage prompts as versioned assets and chain them into flows
    Project 3Build after Chapter 6A governed prompt registry with testing, approval, audit, and a triage flow

    Three project workshops sit between the chapters, each with a problem statement, an architecture, the services to use, and a build path. They are written for when you have finished the chapters before them, and the last section maps every exam skill to the project that practices it.

    Every lab uses the AWS console, and every chapter that touches an API shows a short Boto3 sketch. Do the labs in the Region your project is assigned, and delete what you create when you finish. Bedrock features and console layouts change often, so treat step wording as a guide to where things live, not a script.

    Chapter 0 · Prerequisite

    Orientation: Bedrock, Regions, IAM, and Your First Call

    The six chapters after this one assume you can read a Bedrock architecture diagram, name the right API client, and explain why a call returned AccessDenied. This chapter builds that vocabulary. You already know what a prompt, a token, and an embedding are. What follows is the AWS layer around them.

    Concepts covered

    • What Amazon Bedrock is and how a request reaches a model
    • Regions, model availability, and model access
    • Control plane versus data plane, and the four Boto3 clients
    • InvokeModel versus Converse, plus inference profiles and billing modes
    • IAM basics: identities, policies, roles, and service roles
    • Tokens, context windows, quotas, and throttling
    • A map of the Bedrock feature set and where each piece is covered

    What Amazon Bedrock actually is

    Amazon Bedrock is a managed service that gives you API access to foundation models (FMs) from several providers. The word "managed" matters. You do not provision GPUs, load weights, or size a cluster. You send an HTTPS request, AWS routes it to a fleet that serves the model, and you pay for what you used. The term AWS uses for this is serverless: capacity exists, but it is not yours to operate.

    The catalog is a mix of providers behind one set of APIs. Amazon's own models (the Nova family for text, image, and video, and the older Titan models, including the Titan embedding models you will meet in Chapter 5) sit alongside Anthropic's Claude, Meta's Llama, Mistral, Cohere, and others. The exact list changes every few months, and the exam cares about the kinds of models and how to choose between them (Chapter 2), not about memorizing the catalog. Treat any model name in this guide as an example and confirm what exists in your Region with the console model catalog or aws bedrock list-foundation-models.

    Two properties of the service shape everything else. First, the models are shared infrastructure that AWS operates, not something deployed into your account. Your prompts and completions are not used to train the base models and are not shared with the model providers. That is a headline compliance fact, and Domain 3 returns to it. Second, Bedrock is more than a model endpoint. Around the raw invocation APIs sit managed features for retrieval (Knowledge Bases), safety (Guardrails), prompt versioning (Prompt Management), orchestration (Prompt Flows, Agents, AgentCore), and measurement (Model Evaluation). A later section maps them.

    Backend analogy

    Think of Bedrock as a managed database service for models. With RDS you do not install Postgres on a VM; you get an endpoint, credentials, and a bill. With Bedrock you do not host a model; you get an endpoint, an IAM permission, and a token bill. The same split applies to operations: AWS runs the engine, you own the schema (your prompts and data) and the access rules.

    How a request travels

    When your FastAPI service calls a model, the request is signed with AWS credentials, sent to a Regional endpoint, authorized against IAM, checked against quotas, optionally passed through a guardrail, and then handed to the model. The response comes back along the same path, and the usage numbers are recorded in CloudWatch metrics (and in invocation logs, if you enabled them).

    flowchart LR
  A["Your app (FastAPI, Lambda, ECS task)"] -->|"SigV4-signed HTTPS"| B["bedrock-runtime endpoint (one Region)"]
  B --> C{"IAM allows bedrock:InvokeModel?"}
  C -->|no| X["AccessDeniedException"]
  C -->|yes| D{"Within RPM / TPM quota?"}
  D -->|no| Y["ThrottlingException"]
  D -->|yes| E["Guardrail (optional)"]
  E --> F["Foundation model"]
  F --> G["Response + token usage"]
  G --> H["CloudWatch metrics, optional invocation logs"]

    Read the diagram as a list of places a call can fail. An AccessDeniedException is an IAM or model-access problem. A ThrottlingException is a quota problem. A ValidationException usually means the request body or the model ID is wrong. Knowing which box failed is most of troubleshooting, and Domain 5 builds on it.

    The credentials in step one deserve a sentence. AWS does not use API keys for this by default. Your code signs each request using temporary credentials (an access key, secret, and session token) that come from an IAM identity. The SDK finds them for you through a standard lookup: environment variables, a named profile, or the role attached to the compute that runs your code. You will configure this in the second lab.

    Regions, model availability, and model access

    An AWS Region is a geographic cluster of data centers, named like us-east-1 or eu-central-1. Almost everything in AWS is Regional: a Bedrock endpoint, a Knowledge Base, a guardrail, and a prompt you saved all exist in exactly one Region and are invisible from another. If you create something in the console and cannot find it in a script, the Region is the first thing to check.

    Regions matter more for FMs than for most services for two reasons. Model availability varies, because a new model usually launches in a few Regions and spreads over time. And data residency rules may force you to keep requests inside a geography. Your project is also pinned to one assigned Region, so every lab in this guide should be run in your selected Region, and resources should not be created anywhere else. The mechanism that lets you reach models beyond your Region, cross-Region inference, is introduced below and covered in depth in Chapter 2.

    Getting access to a model

    Older Bedrock tutorials tell you to open a "Model access" page and tick a box for each model. That flow has been simplified, and you may still see it described in exam prep material, so it helps to know both. Today most serverless models are available without a manual enablement step, and you control who can call which model with IAM policies and service control policies (SCPs, organization-level guardrails on what an account may do). A few cases still need an action:

    • Anthropic models are enabled by default but ask for a one-time use-case form before first use. You can submit it from the Bedrock console playground or through the PutUseCaseForModelAccess API. In an AWS Organization, submitting it through the API from the management account extends the approval to the member accounts.
    • Some third-party models are sold through AWS Marketplace. The subscription is created automatically on first invocation if the caller has the aws-marketplace:Subscribe permission, which is a permission people forget to grant to production roles.
    • Some models carry an end-user license agreement you must accept, shown on the model card in the catalog.

    When a first call fails with AccessDeniedException, the cause is rarely one thing. It can be a missing IAM permission, a model you have not been granted or subscribed to, an SCP that blocks the model or the Region, or credentials that expired. Work through them in that order.

    Exam tip

    Questions about "which team can call which model" are answered with IAM identity policies that name specific model or inference-profile ARNs, and with SCPs for organization-wide limits. Model access is not a setting you manage per developer in the Bedrock console. If an answer proposes a separate access-request process per user, it is probably a distractor.

    Control plane, data plane, and the Boto3 clients

    AWS services split their APIs in two. The control plane creates and configures resources: list models, create a Knowledge Base, create a guardrail, buy Provisioned Throughput. The data plane uses those resources at request time: invoke a model, query a Knowledge Base. They have different endpoints, different SDK clients, and different IAM actions, which means you can allow a service to run inference without letting it create or delete anything. That separation is the first piece of least privilege you will use.

    In Boto3 this shows up as separate client names. Picking the wrong one is the most common first error, and it surfaces as UnknownOperationException or an AttributeError on the client.

    Boto3 clientPlaneUse it for
    bedrockControlList and describe models, inference profiles, guardrails, evaluation jobs, Provisioned Throughput, batch jobs, invocation-logging config
    bedrock-runtimeDataconverse, converse_stream, invoke_model, invoke_model_with_response_stream, apply_guardrail
    bedrock-agentControlCreate and configure Knowledge Bases, data sources, prompts (Prompt Management), flows, and classic agents
    bedrock-agent-runtimeDataretrieve, retrieve_and_generate, invoke_agent, invoke_flow

    Two caveats. AgentCore is a separate service with its own clients (bedrock-agentcore-control and bedrock-agentcore), and Domain 2 covers it. Bedrock also has a fifth data plane endpoint, bedrock-mantle, which serves OpenAI-compatible APIs (Responses, Chat Completions, and Messages) and supports server-side tool use, so teams can reuse OpenAI-style client code. The older bedrock-runtime endpoint also accepts Chat Completions under an /openai/v1 path, but only with client-side tool use. It is useful to know both exist, but the exam material centers on the four clients above.

    Backend analogy

    bedrock and bedrock-agent are like the DDL and admin connection to a database: you run them from a deployment pipeline or a console. bedrock-runtime and bedrock-agent-runtime are the application connection that runs queries. Your running service should only ever hold permissions for the second pair.

    InvokeModel versus Converse

    Bedrock gives you two families of inference calls, and the choice between them is a recurring exam theme.

    InvokeModel is the original, low-level call. You pass a modelId and a body that is a JSON string, and the body format is whatever that provider defined. An Anthropic model wants anthropic_version, max_tokens, and a messages list. A Titan text model wants inputText and textGenerationConfig. A Llama model wants a single prompt string with that family's special tokens. The response body is also provider specific. Swapping models therefore means rewriting request and response handling.

    import json
import boto3

runtime = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime")

body = {
    "anthropic_version": "bedrock-2023-05-31",   # Anthropic-specific field
    "max_tokens": 512,
    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "Explain RAG in two sentences."}],
}
resp = runtime.invoke_model(
    modelId=MODEL_ID,                 # an inference profile ID or model ID
    body=json.dumps(body),
    contentType="application/json",
    accept="application/json",
)
payload = json.loads(resp["body"].read())     # provider-specific shape
print(payload["content"][0]["text"])

    Converse (and ConverseStream for streaming) is the newer, uniform call. The request has the same shape for every model that supports it: messages, an optional system list, inferenceConfig for maxTokens, temperature, topP and stopSequences, plus toolConfig for function calling and guardrailConfig for safety. The response also has one shape, including a normalized stopReason and a usage block with token counts. Provider-specific extras go in additionalModelRequestFields when you need them.

    from botocore.config import Config

runtime = boto3.client(
    "bedrock-runtime",
    config=Config(retries={"max_attempts": 5, "mode": "adaptive"}),
)

resp = runtime.converse(
    modelId=MODEL_ID,
    system=[{"text": "You are a concise technical assistant."}],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Explain RAG in two sentences."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 512, "temperature": 0.2},
)
print(resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"])
print(resp["stopReason"], resp["usage"])    # end_turn, {'inputTokens': ..., 'outputTokens': ...}

    For most application code the rule is simple: use Converse. It makes switching providers a one-line change of modelId, which is the foundation of the dynamic model selection patterns in Chapter 2. Reach for InvokeModel when you need a feature the Converse schema does not expose, or when the task is not text conversation at all. Image generation and embedding models are called with InvokeModel, because there is no "conversation" to model.

    Exam tip

    "Switch between model providers without rewriting request-formatting code" points to the Converse API. "Malformed input request" or schema violation errors on InvokeModel point to a request body written for a different provider. Streaming variants are ConverseStream and InvokeModelWithResponseStream. Know that the IAM actions are bedrock:InvokeModel and bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream, and that Converse is authorized by the same pair.

    Watch out

    Always set maxTokens explicitly. If you leave it unset, some models default to a very large maximum, and Bedrock reserves quota for that maximum at the start of every request. A service making modest traffic can then hit ThrottlingException while using a small fraction of its real allowance. Size maxTokens to the output you actually expect.

    Model IDs and inference profiles

    The modelId argument accepts four kinds of value, and the error you see tells you which one you got wrong.

    • A base model ID such as anthropic.claude-... invokes the model in the Region of your client. It only works for models that support in-Region on-demand use there.
    • A system-defined inference profile ID has a geography prefix such as us., eu., or apac., or the global. prefix. A geographic profile lets Bedrock route the request to any Region inside that geography. A global. profile can route to any commercial Region where the model runs. Both give you more capacity and better resilience, and many newer models can only be called this way.
    • An application inference profile is one you create yourself, so you can tag it for cost allocation and track usage per application or team.
    • A Provisioned Throughput ARN points at capacity you bought.

    If you see "on-demand throughput isn't supported" for a model ID, the fix is almost always to use the model's inference profile ID instead. Look it up with aws bedrock list-inference-profiles. The geography prefix is also a data residency decision: a us. profile keeps processing in the US, while global. may route anywhere. Chapter 2 treats cross-Region inference as a resilience technique, and Domain 3 revisits it for compliance.

    Three ways to consume a model

    How you pay and how capacity is guaranteed are separate choices from how you call the model. At a high level there are three modes.

    ModeHow it worksChoose it when
    On-demandPay per input and output token. Capacity is shared and subject to quotas.Default. Variable or unknown traffic, prototypes, most production apps.
    Batch inferenceYou submit a file of requests (in S3) as a job, results land in S3 later. Priced at a discount.Large offline workloads with no one waiting: summarizing a document archive, generating embeddings or labels in bulk.
    Provisioned ThroughputYou buy dedicated model units (MUs) at an hourly rate. You pick no commitment, one month, or six months, and a longer commitment lowers the hourly price. Required to use a model you customized in Bedrock.Steady, high-volume, latency-sensitive traffic, or a fine-tuned model that must be hosted.

    Within on-demand there are also service tiers, set with the serviceTier field on a Converse request: a standard tier (the default when you omit the field), a priority tier for latency-sensitive paths at a premium price, a flex tier that is discounted but may queue at peak, and a reserved tier that gives dedicated capacity for a one- or three-month commitment. Domain 4 covers these under cost and performance. Do not confuse the reserved tier with Provisioned Throughput, which is a separate purchase of model units. The trade-off to remember: on-demand is flexible but throttle-prone at scale, Provisioned Throughput is predictable but bills every hour whether or not you send traffic, and batch is cheap but not interactive.

    Watch out

    Provisioned Throughput bills by the hour from the moment it is created until you delete it. With a one-month or six-month commitment you cannot delete it until the term ends, so a mistake costs the full term. Never create it casually while learning. None of the labs in this guide need it.

    IAM for a Bedrock developer

    AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) decides who may do what to which resource. Every Bedrock call you make is evaluated by IAM before anything else happens. You need a working model of it more than a deep one, so here are the pieces.

    • A principal is whoever makes the call: a person, an application, or an AWS service acting on your behalf.
    • An IAM role is an identity with permissions but no password or long-lived keys. Something assumes the role and receives temporary credentials. Compute services (Lambda, ECS tasks, EC2) each take a role, and your code inherits it automatically. Prefer roles to IAM users with access keys everywhere.
    • A policy is a JSON document made of statements. Each statement has an Effect (Allow or Deny), a list of Actions (API operations such as bedrock:InvokeModel), a list of Resources (ARNs, the unique names of resources), and optionally a Condition.
    • An identity-based policy attaches to a user or role and says what it can do. A resource-based policy attaches to a resource (an S3 bucket, a Lambda function) and says who can use it. A service control policy sits above the account and sets the outer limit that no identity inside can exceed.

    Evaluation follows a few fixed rules. Everything is denied by default. An explicit Allow in an identity policy grants access. An explicit Deny anywhere wins over any Allow. An SCP that does not allow an action blocks it even if the role's own policy does. When a Bedrock call is denied, the question to ask is which of those layers said no.

    Least privilege applied to Bedrock

    Least privilege means granting only the actions and resources a workload needs. For a service that answers chat requests this is a short list: bedrock:InvokeModel and bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream, scoped to the model or inference-profile ARNs it actually uses. Avoid bedrock:* and broad managed policies for runtime roles. One detail trips up almost everyone the first time: when you call a model through an inference profile, the request may be served from any Region the profile covers, so the policy must allow both the inference profile ARN and the underlying foundation model ARN with a wildcard Region. The lab shows the exact shape.

    Service roles: when Bedrock acts as you

    Some Bedrock features need to do things in your account on their own: a Knowledge Base ingestion job reads documents from S3 and writes vectors to a store, a batch job reads input and writes output files, and an agent invokes a Lambda function. For these, you create an IAM role that Bedrock assumes. The role has two parts. The trust policy says who may assume it (the Bedrock service). The permissions policy says what it may do (read this bucket, call the embedding model, write to that index).

    {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "bedrock.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": { "aws:SourceAccount": "123456789012" },
      "ArnLike": { "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:123456789012:knowledge-base/*" }
    }
  }]
}

    The two Condition keys are protection against the confused deputy problem: without them, another AWS customer who learns your role ARN could trick the Bedrock service into acting with your role on their behalf. Restricting the trust policy to your account and to the specific Bedrock resource type closes that gap. Expect this pattern in security-flavored questions in every domain.

    Backend analogy

    A role is a service account whose token is minted on demand and expires. A trust policy is the rule that says which callers may mint that token. A service role for a Knowledge Base is the credential you would otherwise hard-code into an ingestion worker, handed to a managed worker instead.

    Note

    If your AWS project uses a managed access experience, human sign-in permissions may be handled for you, and you may not author policies for yourself at all. You still write policies for the roles your applications and Bedrock itself use, and the exam tests that skill regardless.

    Tokens, context windows, quotas, and throttling

    You know tokens and context windows from other platforms, so only the Bedrock specifics are here. Billing counts input tokens (everything you send, including system prompt, history, and retrieved context) and output tokens (what the model generates), usually at different prices per thousand. The context window is the model's limit on input plus output together, and it differs by model. Overflowing it is an error in some paths and silent truncation in others, a distinction that Chapter 5 and Domain 5 return to. Every Converse response includes a usage block with the exact counts, which is the cheapest cost telemetry you will ever get.

    Bedrock limits are expressed per model and per Region as requests per minute (RPM) and tokens per minute (TPM). Exceeding either returns HTTP 429, surfaced as ThrottlingException. TPM has a subtlety: at the start of each request Bedrock reserves quota for your input tokens plus the maxTokens you asked for, then releases the unused part as the response is generated. For some model families, output tokens also count at a multiple of their raw number when quota is settled: Anthropic Claude 3.7 and later models use a 5x burndown rate, so one output token consumes five tokens of quota, while other models use 1x. Tokens read from a prompt cache do not count toward the final settlement. The practical advice is the same as before: set maxTokens tightly and measure real output lengths.

    Not every error should be retried. Throttling, timeouts, and transient service errors are worth retrying with exponential backoff, and the SDK's adaptive retry mode does that for you. Validation errors and access denials will fail identically every time, so retrying them only wastes time. When retries are not enough, the levers are quota increase requests through Service Quotas, cross-Region inference profiles to spread load, batch inference for work that can wait, and Provisioned Throughput for steady heavy traffic. Domain 4 goes deeper on each.

    The Bedrock feature map

    Bedrock is a family of capabilities that share the same models and IAM, and the exam names many of them. The diagram groups them by the job they do, and the table says where each is taught.

    flowchart TB
  subgraph Models["Models and invocation"]
    M1["Model catalog and inference profiles"]
    M2["Converse / InvokeModel"]
    M3["Batch, Provisioned Throughput"]
  end
  subgraph Grounding["Grounding and prompts"]
    G1["Knowledge Bases"]
    G2["Prompt Management"]
    G3["Prompt Flows"]
  end
  subgraph Behavior["Behavior and safety"]
    B1["Guardrails"]
    B2["Agents / AgentCore"]
  end
  subgraph Quality["Measurement and data"]
    Q1["Model Evaluation"]
    Q2["Data Automation"]
  end
  Models --> Grounding --> Behavior
  Behavior --> Quality
    FeatureWhat it doesWhere it is covered
    Model catalog, Converse, inference profilesSelect and call models; route across RegionsThis chapter, Chapter 2 (Task 1.2), Domain 2 (Task 2.4)
    Knowledge BasesManaged RAG: ingest documents, embed, store vectors, retrieveChapter 4 (Task 1.4) and Chapter 5 (Task 1.5)
    Prompt ManagementVersioned, parameterized prompt templates with variantsChapter 6 (Task 1.6)
    Prompt FlowsVisual or API-defined chains of prompts, conditions, and other nodesChapter 6 (Task 1.6), Domain 2 (Task 2.5)
    GuardrailsContent filters, denied topics, PII handling, grounding checksTask 1.6 introduces them, Domain 3 covers them in depth
    Agents and AgentCoreModels that plan and call tools; managed runtime, gateway, memory, and identity for agentsDomain 2 (Task 2.1)
    Model EvaluationAutomatic, human, and LLM-as-judge evaluation jobsDomain 5 (Task 5.1)
    Data AutomationExtract structured data from documents, images, audio, and videoChapter 3 touches it, Domain 2 (Task 2.5)

    Note

    Classic Bedrock Agents (the bedrock-agent action-group service) is in maintenance mode and closed to new customers. AWS directs new agent work to AgentCore, including its Harness, a managed agent loop you configure instead of coding. The exam guide lists both Bedrock and Bedrock AgentCore as in scope, so understand the older agent concepts (action groups, session state) as vocabulary, but build new agent examples on AgentCore.

    Hands-on labs

    Do these two in order. The first shows you the request the console builds, and the second reproduces it from code so you see the same call from both sides.

    Hands-on lab

    Lab 0.1: Explore a model in the playground

    About 15 minutes · Cost: a few cents of on-demand tokens · Needs: console access in your selected Region

    1. Sign in to the AWS console and confirm the Region selector (top right) shows your project's assigned Region. Use only that Region for every lab.
    2. Open Amazon Bedrock. In the left navigation, open the Model catalog and filter by provider. Open a model card for one Anthropic model and one Amazon model, and note the supported input and output modalities, context window, and whether the model lists cross-Region inference.
    3. In the left navigation, open a Playground (Chat or Text). Choose Select model, pick a provider and model, and apply. If the model asks for a use-case form or license acceptance, complete it. That is the one-time access step from earlier in this chapter.
    4. Send a prompt such as "Summarize why serverless matters for ML inference in three bullets."
    5. Open the configuration panel and set Temperature to 0, run the same prompt twice, and compare. Then set it to 1 and compare again. Set Maximum length (max tokens) to a small number such as 50 and watch the answer get cut off. Note the stop reason or truncation.
    6. Find the option that shows the API request behind your chat (it has been labeled View API request, often under a three-dot menu, but check the console for the current label and location). Read the JSON. Identify modelId, the message structure, and inferenceConfig fields. This is the Converse request you will write in Lab 0.2.
    7. Look at the token counts or metrics shown after the response, if your playground displays them, and note input versus output tokens.

    Cleanup. Nothing persists. The playground creates no billable resources, only the token charges for your prompts.

    Hands-on lab

    Lab 0.2: Your first Converse call from Python

    About 25 minutes · Cost: a few cents · Needs: Python with uv, AWS CLI v2, credentials for your project

    1. Authenticate your terminal to AWS. Run aws login if your project uses browser sign-in, or configure a named profile (aws configure sso or aws configure). Verify with aws sts get-caller-identity, which prints the identity your calls will use. Export your Region: export AWS_REGION=<your-assigned-region>.
    2. Find a model you can call. List system-defined inference profiles: aws bedrock list-inference-profiles --region $AWS_REGION --query "inferenceProfileSummaries[].inferenceProfileId". Pick one for a small, cheap text model and keep its ID. If a base model ID works in your Region, aws bedrock list-foundation-models --by-output-modality TEXT lists them.
    3. Check permissions. If your sign-in already allows Bedrock invocation, continue. Otherwise attach a policy like the following to the role or identity you use, replacing the placeholders with your Region, account ID, profile ID, and underlying model ID. Note both resource lines. 
      {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel", "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"],
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:bedrock:<region>:<account-id>:inference-profile/<profile-id>",
      "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/<model-id>"
    ]
  }]
}
    4. Create a scratch project: mkdir -p labs && cd labs && uv init --bare lab0 && cd lab0 && uv add boto3 (or work in an existing lab folder).
    5. Create converse_hello.py: 
      import os
import sys

import boto3
from botocore.config import Config

MODEL_ID = os.environ["MODEL_ID"]          # the inference profile ID from step 2

runtime = boto3.client(
    "bedrock-runtime",
    region_name=os.environ["AWS_REGION"],
    config=Config(retries={"max_attempts": 5, "mode": "adaptive"}),
)


def ask(question: str) -> None:
    resp = runtime.converse(
        modelId=MODEL_ID,
        system=[{"text": "Answer in at most three sentences."}],
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": question}]}],
        inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 300, "temperature": 0.2},
    )
    print(resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"])
    usage = resp["usage"]
    print(f"\nstop={resp['stopReason']} in={usage['inputTokens']} out={usage['outputTokens']}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    ask(" ".join(sys.argv[1:]) or "What is a foundation model?")
    6. Run it: MODEL_ID=<profile-id> uv run python converse_hello.py "What is a foundation model?". You should see an answer and a token line.
    7. Break it on purpose to learn the errors. Change the client to boto3.client("bedrock") and note the failure. Restore it, then change the model ID to a base ID for a model that requires an inference profile and read the ValidationException text. Finally set AWS_REGION to a Region where you have no access and observe the error (this is a read-only check, do not create resources elsewhere). Restore your settings.
    8. Swap converse for converse_stream, iterate resp["stream"], and print each contentBlockDelta text. Notice that the final metadata event carries the usage numbers.

    Cleanup. No resources were created. If you attached a new IAM policy only for the lab, detach it when you are done. Delete the scratch folder if you do not want to keep it.

    Chapter recap

    • Bedrock is managed, serverless access to many providers' models behind shared APIs, IAM, and quotas. Your prompts are not used to train base models.
    • Everything is Regional. Model availability, quotas, and resources you create all live in one Region. Cross-Region inference profiles extend reach and capacity.
    • Four clients matter: bedrock and bedrock-agent are control plane, bedrock-runtime and bedrock-agent-runtime are data plane.
    • Use Converse for a uniform request across models. Use InvokeModel for provider-specific bodies, image generation, and embeddings.
    • Always set maxTokens, use adaptive retries, and retry only throttling and transient errors.
    • Runtime roles get bedrock:InvokeModel scoped to specific ARNs. Inference profiles need the profile ARN plus the foundation model ARN with a wildcard Region.
    • Services that act for you use service roles with a trust policy and confused-deputy conditions (aws:SourceAccount, aws:SourceArn).
    • Three consumption modes: on-demand (flexible), batch (cheap, offline), Provisioned Throughput (reserved, billed hourly).

    Knowledge check

    1. A team's Lambda function calls boto3.client("bedrock").converse(...) and gets AttributeError or UnknownOperationException. What is wrong, and what is the fix?

      Answer

      They used the control plane client for a data plane operation. Inference lives on the bedrock-runtime client. The fix is boto3.client("bedrock-runtime"). The same applies to bedrock-agent versus bedrock-agent-runtime for retrieval and agent invocation.

    2. An application must be able to move from one provider's model to another by changing configuration only, with no changes to request-building code. Which API should it use, and why not the alternative?

      Answer

      The Converse API (or ConverseStream). It uses one request and response schema across supporting models, so only modelId changes. InvokeModel requires a different JSON body per provider, so swapping providers means rewriting the formatting and parsing code.

    3. A production role has bedrock:InvokeModel on a foundation model ARN for one Region. Calls using a us. inference profile ID fail with AccessDeniedException. What is the least-privilege fix?

      Answer

      Allow bedrock:InvokeModel on both the inference profile ARN (arn:aws:bedrock:<region>:<account-id>:inference-profile/<profile-id>) and the foundation model ARN with a wildcard Region (arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/<model-id>), because the profile can route to any Region it covers. Granting bedrock:* on * would work but violates least privilege.

    4. A service handles modest traffic but intermittently receives ThrottlingException. Its code does not set maxTokens. Explain the likely cause and the first fix.

      Answer

      Bedrock reserves tokens-per-minute quota at the start of each request based on input tokens plus the maximum output allowed. With no maxTokens, that can be the model's large default, so each request reserves far more quota than it uses and the allowance is exhausted early. Set maxTokens close to the expected output length, use adaptive retries, and then consider quota increases or a cross-Region profile if throttling continues.

    5. You are creating an IAM role that Bedrock will assume to read documents from S3 during Knowledge Base ingestion. Which two Condition keys should the trust policy include, and what attack do they prevent?

      Answer

      aws:SourceAccount and aws:SourceArn (scoped to your Knowledge Base resources). They prevent the confused deputy problem, where another customer causes the Bedrock service to use your role on their behalf. Without them, anyone who can name your role ARN could attempt it.

    Chapter 1 · Task 1.1

    Analyze Requirements and Design GenAI Solutions

    Most failed GenAI projects fail before anyone writes a prompt. The team picks a model first, discovers the data cannot leave a Region, or finds out each request costs more than the value it creates. This chapter is about the decisions that come first: what the business needs, which architecture fits, how to prove it cheaply, and how to make the result repeatable across teams.

    Exam skills covered

    • 1.1.1 Create architectural designs that fit business needs and technical constraints, including the choice of FMs, integration patterns, and deployment strategies
    • 1.1.2 Build proof-of-concept implementations to validate feasibility, performance, and business value before full-scale deployment
    • 1.1.3 Create standardized technical components for consistent implementation across deployments, using the AWS Well-Architected Framework and the Generative AI Lens

    How this task is examined

    Task 1.1 is the "architect" task of the exam. You will rarely be asked for an API parameter. You will be given a paragraph describing a company, a workload, and three or four constraints, and asked to pick the best design. The constraints do the real work: "must keep data in the EU", "traffic is spiky and unpredictable", "the team has no ML engineers", "answers must cite internal documents". Each constraint eliminates some options, and usually only one option survives all of them.

    So the skill to build is a habit: read the scenario, extract the constraints, and map each one to a design consequence. The rest of this chapter gives you the vocabulary and the maps. Later chapters go deep on individual components (models in Chapter 2, data pipelines in Chapter 3, vector stores in Chapter 4, retrieval in Chapter 5, prompts in Chapter 6). Here you learn how those parts get chosen and assembled.

    Turning a business request into requirements

    A request like "add an AI assistant to our support portal" is not a requirement. It is a wish with a technology attached. Requirements analysis converts it into statements you can design against and later test. You already know how to do this for a normal backend service. For GenAI there are a few extra axes, because the component at the center is probabilistic, priced per token, and trained on data you do not control.

    Functional requirements: what the system must do

    Start by naming the task in terms of what goes in and what comes out. Most GenAI use cases reduce to a small set of task types, and the type often hints at the architecture.

    Task typeExampleArchitecture hint
    GenerateDraft a product description from a spec sheetDirect model call with a good prompt template
    Summarize or transformCondense a 40-page contract into key termsDirect call; watch the context window for long inputs
    Extract or classifyPull invoice fields, route tickets by intentDirect call with structured output; consider a smaller model
    Answer from private knowledge"What is our refund policy for EU customers?"Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)
    ConverseMulti-turn support chatDirect call or RAG, plus conversation memory
    ActLook up an order, then issue a refundAgent with tools, often with a human approval step
    Process at volumeTag 2 million archived documents overnightAsynchronous batch, not an interactive API

    Non-functional requirements: how well, how fast, how safely

    These are where GenAI designs diverge. Capture each one as a number or a yes/no, not an adjective.

    • Quality. What does "good" mean? Factual accuracy against a reference, adherence to a tone guide, correct JSON, or citation of a source. If nobody can define it, you cannot evaluate it, and you will argue about opinions later. Define a measurable bar early, for example "at least 90% of answers judged correct by a reviewer on a 100-question test set".
    • Latency. Two numbers matter for text. Time to first token is how long before the user sees anything, which matters for chat. Total generation time is how long until the answer is complete, which matters for API consumers. A chat UI that streams can feel fast even when the full answer takes ten seconds. A back-office job does not care about either.
    • Throughput and concurrency. Peak requests per second, and how bursty. Model quotas are expressed in requests per minute and tokens per minute, so a steady 10 requests per second is a very different problem from a Monday-morning spike of 200.
    • Cost. Model usage is billed per token, so cost scales with how much text you send and receive. Estimate cost per request and per month before you commit. A design that costs a dollar per interaction cannot serve a free consumer feature.
    • Data sensitivity, residency, and compliance. Does the prompt contain personal data or regulated data? Must it stay in a country or Region? Which audits apply? These are covered next because they are the most frequent deciding factors on the exam.
    • Availability and resilience. What happens to the business if the model endpoint is unavailable for an hour? The answer decides whether you need fallbacks and multi-Region patterns (Chapter 2).
    • Operability. Who runs this at 2 a.m.? A team with no ML or platform engineers should lean toward managed services, even if they cost a little more.

    Backend analogy

    Think of non-functional requirements as the SLO sheet for a service, with two additions. Quality is a first-class SLO because correct HTTP 200 responses can still contain wrong answers. And cost is a per-request variable, like paying a third-party API per call, rather than a fixed server bill.

    Is GenAI even the right tool?

    A good architect asks this before anything else. Foundation models are expensive, slower than ordinary code, and non-deterministic. Use them when the problem involves unstructured language or media, open-ended output, or fuzzy judgment. Do not use them when a simpler tool is exact and cheaper.

    If the problem is...A better first choice may be...
    Exact rules with known inputs (tax calculation, eligibility)Plain code or a rules engine
    Find documents matching keywords or filtersSearch (for example, OpenSearch keyword queries)
    Predict a number or label from structured columnsClassical ML on SageMaker AI
    Read text from a scanned form, or detect a faceA task-specific AI service such as Amazon Textract or Amazon Rekognition
    Detect sentiment, entities, or PII in text at scaleAmazon Comprehend
    Open-ended language: summarize, draft, reason over text, converseA foundation model on Amazon Bedrock

    Many real solutions combine them. A document pipeline might use Textract to read a scan, plain code to validate fields, and a foundation model only for the free-text portion. The exam rewards candidates who do not reach for a large model when a purpose-built service is a better fit.

    Exam tip

    If a scenario describes a narrow, well-defined task (extract text from scanned forms, detect PII, translate, transcribe audio) and asks for the lowest operational effort, the answer is usually the task-specific managed service (Textract, Comprehend, Translate, Transcribe), not a foundation model. If the task is open-ended language generation or reasoning, the answer is Bedrock.

    Constraints that decide the design

    Four constraint families show up again and again in scenarios. Learn what each one forces.

    Data residency and cross-Region inference

    An AWS Region is a physical location with its own set of data centers. Customer data stays in the Region where you put it unless you move it. Foundation models complicate this because not every model is hosted in every Region, and a Region may not have enough capacity at peak.

    Bedrock offers cross-Region inference to deal with this. Instead of calling a model by its base ID, you call an inference profile, a named routing rule that lets Bedrock send your request to any of several Regions that host the model. This gives higher throughput and resilience. The catch is that the prompt and response may be processed in a Region other than the one you called. Profiles come in two scopes. A geographic profile (for example, a US or EU profile) only routes within that geography. A global profile can route to any supported commercial Region. Choosing the profile prefix is therefore a data residency decision, not just a performance one. If a scenario says "customer data must remain in the EU", an EU geographic profile can satisfy it and a global profile cannot.

    Note what cross-Region inference does not do. Your stored data (documents in S3, vectors in a store, logs) remains in the Region where you created it. Only the inference request is routed.

    Compliance and privacy posture

    Regulated workloads (health, finance, public sector) need answers to a predictable list of questions. Is the service eligible for the relevant compliance program? Bedrock lists the programs it supports in AWS Artifact and the compliance documentation, and you should confirm the specific model and feature you use. Is customer data used to train models? On Bedrock, your prompts and completions are not used to train the base foundation models and are not shared with model providers. Is data encrypted at rest and in transit? Bedrock encrypts both, and you can supply your own AWS KMS key for resources you create, such as custom models and knowledge bases. Can traffic stay off the public internet? Yes, with VPC interface endpoints through AWS PrivateLink. Domain 3 of the exam goes deep on these, but at design time you need to recognize which constraint a scenario is hinting at.

    Latency budgets

    Work backward from the user. If a chat answer must begin within two seconds, you have a budget to split across network hops, retrieval, guardrail checks, and the model's time to first token. A design that does three sequential model calls (rewrite the query, retrieve, answer) may blow that budget, and you would respond by streaming, using a smaller model for the rewrite step, or running steps in parallel. Chapter 2 covers model choice for latency, and Domain 4 covers tuning.

    Cost per request

    Cost is driven by tokens in and tokens out, multiplied by the model's price, multiplied by volume. Output tokens usually cost several times more than input tokens. A RAG design that stuffs ten retrieved passages into every prompt multiplies input cost. An agent that loops five times multiplies it again. Estimate on paper first. You will do a worked example in the PoC section below.

    Watch out

    Candidates often treat "use the most capable model" as the safe answer. On this exam it is frequently the wrong one. When a scenario stresses cost or latency for a simple task, a smaller, cheaper model is the better answer. When it stresses quality on hard reasoning, the larger model wins. Read which constraint the question emphasizes.

    The build-versus-buy ladder

    AWS offers GenAI capability at several levels of abstraction. Higher rungs mean less code, less control, and less to operate. Lower rungs mean more control and more engineering. The architect's job is to stand on the highest rung that still meets the requirements.

    RungWhat you getChoose it whenOperational effort
    Finished applications
    (Amazon Q Business, Amazon Q Developer, Amazon Connect with AI)    		A ready assistant for employees, developers, or a contact centerThe need is a standard capability and you want users productive without building anythingLowest
    Managed building blocks on Bedrock
    (Knowledge Bases, Guardrails, Prompt Management, Prompt Flows, agent services)    		RAG, safety filters, prompt versioning, and orchestration as configurationYou need a custom application but not custom plumbing for each pieceLow to moderate
    Bedrock model APIs with your own code
    (Converse API, your retrieval, your orchestration)    		Direct access to many models behind one API; you write the restYou need control over retrieval, prompts, routing, or caching that managed blocks do not exposeModerate
    Customized models on Bedrock
    (fine-tuning, continued pre-training, distillation, custom model import)    		A model adapted to your domain, still served by BedrockPrompting and RAG cannot reach the quality, tone, or cost targetModerate to high
    SageMaker AI
    (JumpStart models, your own endpoints, training jobs)    		Full control of the model, container, hardware, and scalingYou need a model Bedrock does not offer, special hardware or serving control, or heavy custom trainingHighest

    A useful rule of thumb is to try each rung in order and move down only when you can name the specific requirement that the higher rung failed. Prompting beats RAG for simplicity. RAG beats fine-tuning for knowledge that changes, because updating a document is easier than retraining a model. Fine-tuning earns its place for style, format, and domain behavior that prompting cannot teach, not for facts that change weekly.

    Amazon Q Business deserves a specific mention because it appears in scenarios. It is a managed enterprise assistant that connects to company data sources, respects the existing permissions of each user, and answers questions without you building a retrieval pipeline. If a scenario asks for an internal question-answering assistant over SharePoint, Confluence, and S3 with minimal development and per-user access control, Q Business is a strong candidate. If instead the scenario describes a customer-facing application with custom logic and a specific user experience, you are building on Bedrock.

    Backend analogy

    The ladder is the same decision as hosted SaaS versus managed Postgres versus Postgres on your own EC2 instance. You pick the rung where the control you gain is worth the pager duty you accept.

    Exam tip

    "Least development effort", "fully managed", and "no ML expertise" point up the ladder. "Needs a custom model architecture", "must run on specific GPU instances", or "model not available in Bedrock" point to SageMaker AI. Between those extremes, Bedrock APIs plus Knowledge Bases is the most common right answer.

    Reference architectures and when each fits

    Almost every GenAI solution on AWS is a variation of six patterns. Knowing them by shape lets you recognize a scenario quickly. Each pattern below shows the core flow, the AWS services that usually fill each box, and the situations where it is the right answer.

    Pattern 1: Direct model call

    The simplest design. An application sends a prompt to a model and returns the result. There is no retrieval, no tools, and no orchestration beyond prompt assembly. It fits generation, summarization, classification, and extraction where the model already knows enough, or where the needed context fits in the request itself (for example, the user pastes the document).

    flowchart LR
  U["Client app"] --> AG["Amazon API Gateway"]
  AG --> L["AWS Lambda"]
  L --> G["Bedrock Guardrails"]
  L --> B["Amazon Bedrock Converse API"]
  L --> CW["CloudWatch Logs and metrics"]

    API Gateway gives you authentication, throttling, and request validation. Lambda holds your prompt template and calls bedrock-runtime. A guardrail, if you attach one, screens input and output. This is the baseline against which every more complex pattern should justify itself.

    Pattern 2: Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)

    Use RAG when answers must come from your own, changing, or private content, and when you want citations. The model is not retrained. Instead you retrieve relevant passages at question time and place them in the prompt. You know the idea. What matters here is how AWS divides it into two independent paths: an ingestion path that prepares knowledge ahead of time, and a query path that runs per request.

    flowchart LR
  subgraph Ingest["Ingestion path (offline)"]
    S3["S3 documents"] --> CH["Parse and chunk"]
    CH --> EM["Embedding model"]
    EM --> VS["Vector store"]
  end
  subgraph Query["Query path (per request)"]
    Q["User question"] --> QE["Embed question"]
    QE --> VS
    VS --> CTX["Top-k passages"]
    CTX --> P["Prompt with context"]
    P --> M["Foundation model"]
    M --> A["Answer with citations"]
  end

    On AWS you can fill these boxes in three ways. Bedrock Knowledge Bases manages the whole ingestion path and exposes a retrieval API, which is the lowest-effort route. A custom pipeline uses Lambda or Glue for chunking, the Bedrock embedding API, and a vector store you operate, such as OpenSearch Service or Aurora PostgreSQL with the pgvector extension. A managed assistant such as Amazon Q Business hides everything. Chapters 4 and 5 cover the store and retrieval details. For design purposes, remember the trade: more control means more to build and operate.

    Pattern 3: Agentic

    Use an agent when the system must decide what to do next, call external tools, and loop until a goal is met: check an order, query a database, then issue a refund. The model plans, a runtime executes tool calls, and the results feed back into the model.

    flowchart LR
  U["User goal"] --> AGT["Agent runtime"]
  AGT --> FM["Foundation model reasons and plans"]
  FM --> TC{"Tool call needed?"}
  TC -->|"yes"| T["Tools: Lambda, APIs, MCP servers, knowledge base"]
  T --> FM
  TC -->|"no"| R["Final answer"]
  AGT --> MEM["Memory and state"]
  AGT --> HITL["Human approval for risky actions"]

    On AWS, agents can be built with Bedrock agent services, with the open-source Strands Agents SDK running on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, or with Step Functions orchestrating the loop yourself. The original (classic) Bedrock Agents service is in maintenance mode and closed to new customers, and AWS directs new agent workloads to AgentCore. Domain 2 covers agents in depth. For Task 1.1, the design question is whether you need one at all. Agents add cost (several model calls per task), latency, and unpredictability. If the steps are fixed and known, a workflow (pattern 5) is cheaper and safer.

    Pattern 4: Asynchronous and batch

    Not every workload has a user waiting. Classifying a backlog of documents, generating embeddings for a corpus, or producing nightly reports do not need a sub-second answer, and they benefit from queues and retries. Decoupling the request from the response also protects you from model throttling, because a queue absorbs bursts.

    flowchart LR
  IN["S3 upload or API request"] --> EV["EventBridge or SQS queue"]
  EV --> W["Lambda or ECS worker"]
  W --> BR["Bedrock model call"]
  W --> DLQ["Dead-letter queue for failures"]
  W --> OUT["S3 results or DynamoDB"]
  OUT --> N["SNS notification or callback"]

    Bedrock also offers a batch inference mode. You place many prompts in a file in S3, submit a job, and receive outputs in S3 later, typically at a lower price per token than on-demand calls for the models that support it. It suits large offline jobs where you can wait. It does not suit anything interactive.

    Pattern 5: Workflow-orchestrated

    When the sequence of steps is known, use a workflow engine rather than letting a model decide the steps. AWS Step Functions is a managed state machine service. It has built-in integration with Bedrock, so a state can invoke a model without a Lambda in between. Workflows give you retries, timeouts, parallel branches, error handling, and human approval pauses, all visible in an execution history. Typical examples are document processing (extract, validate, summarize, route for review) and multi-step content generation.

    Bedrock Prompt Flows is a related, lower-code option: a visual builder for chaining prompts, knowledge base lookups, and Lambda calls into a flow. Chapter 6 covers it. A rule of thumb is that Prompt Flows suits prompt-centric chains owned by a small team, and Step Functions suits workflows that cross many services and need enterprise-grade error handling.

    Backend analogy

    Step Functions is a durable workflow engine, similar to Temporal or Airflow's control flow, where the state lives in the service. An agent is the opposite: the control flow is decided at runtime by the model. Prefer the workflow whenever you can write the steps down in advance.

    Pattern 6: Multimodal

    Inputs and outputs are not only text. Examples are reading charts in PDFs, describing images, transcribing and summarizing calls, or extracting fields from forms. There are two broad approaches. You can convert to text first: Amazon Transcribe turns audio into text, Amazon Textract extracts text and tables from documents, and then a text model reasons over the result. Or you can use a multimodal foundation model that accepts images (and in some models, video or audio) directly in the Converse API. Bedrock Data Automation is a managed capability for extracting structured output from documents, images, audio, and video at scale. Task 1.3 covers these pipelines. The design rule is to use the converted-text route when exact text fidelity matters or cost is tight, and a multimodal model when layout, charts, or visual context carry the meaning.

    Choosing between patterns

    Scenario signalPattern
    Draft, summarize, classify, or extract from supplied text1. Direct call
    Answers must reflect internal, current, or large document sets; citations needed2. RAG
    The system must choose tools and decide the next step; open-ended tasks3. Agentic
    Millions of items, overnight, no user waiting, bursty load4. Async or batch
    Fixed multi-step process with retries, approvals, and audit trail5. Workflow
    Images, scans, audio, or video are the input6. Multimodal (often combined with 5)

    Patterns combine. A claims-processing system might use a workflow (5) that runs multimodal extraction (6), a RAG lookup against policy documents (2), and a human review step. Design the simplest combination that satisfies the constraints, and no more.

    Exam tip

    A frequent trap is offering an agent where a workflow or RAG would do. If the process is deterministic ("extract, then validate, then route"), Step Functions beats an agent on cost, predictability, and auditability. If the need is "answer from our documents", RAG beats fine-tuning and beats an agent.

    Integration patterns

    Once you know the shape of the solution, you decide how the model call is wired into the rest of the system. Three questions drive this: does the caller wait, does the response stream, and who triggers the work.

    Synchronous, streaming, and asynchronous

    In a synchronous call, the client sends a request and waits for the complete answer. The Converse API works this way. It is simple and fits short generations and server-to-server calls. Its risk is timeouts. Long generations can exceed the limits of the components in front of the model, such as API Gateway's integration timeout (a REST API defaults to 29 seconds and the limit can be raised for Regional and private REST APIs, while an HTTP API has a hard maximum of 30 seconds that cannot be raised) and, for Lambda, the function timeout (up to 15 minutes). REST APIs can also stream responses in proxy integrations, which sidesteps the wait for a complete answer.

    In a streaming call, the model returns tokens as they are produced. Bedrock offers ConverseStream and InvokeModelWithResponseStream for this. Streaming does not make the total generation faster, but it makes the experience feel immediate because the user starts reading at the first token. It is the default choice for chat. You then need a delivery path that can stream to the browser: server-sent events or WebSockets, a Lambda function with response streaming, or a long-lived container service. Task 2.4 covers the mechanics.

    In an asynchronous pattern, the caller submits work and gets an acknowledgment, and the result arrives later through polling, a callback, or a notification. Use it when generation takes long, when the caller is another system, or when you need to smooth bursts. A queue (Amazon SQS) between the caller and the worker gives you buffering, retry with backoff, and a dead-letter queue for poison messages.

    Event-driven integration

    Many GenAI features are triggered by something happening, not by a user clicking. A new file lands in S3 and needs summarizing. A support ticket is created and needs triage. A database row changes and a vector needs refreshing. Amazon EventBridge is an event bus that routes events from AWS services and your own applications to targets using rules. It lets the GenAI component stay loosely coupled: the ticketing system emits "ticket created" and does not need to know that a model is involved. This is also how you add GenAI to a legacy system with minimal change, by consuming its existing events or webhooks rather than modifying its code. Task 2.3 develops this.

    Where the model call lives

    OptionStrengthsLimitsPick it when
    AWS LambdaNo servers, scales to zero, pay per use, easy event triggers15-minute cap, cold starts, per-request pricing can exceed containers at sustained high loadSpiky or low traffic, event-driven jobs, short interactions
    Containers on Amazon ECS with AWS FargateNo servers to patch, long-lived connections, any framework (your FastAPI app unchanged), steady-load economicsPay for idle capacity unless you scale down, you manage the image and scaling policyStreaming chat backends, sustained traffic, existing containerized services
    Amazon EKSKubernetes ecosystem, portabilityHighest operational overhead of the compute optionsThe organization already standardizes on Kubernetes
    AWS App RunnerSimplest container deployment from an image or repoLess control over networking and scaling than ECSSmall teams, simple web APIs
    Step FunctionsOrchestration without code, direct Bedrock integrationNot a general runtime for custom logicMulti-step workflows (pattern 5)

    Given your FastAPI background, the practical default for an interactive, streaming GenAI API is a container on ECS with Fargate behind an Application Load Balancer, and Lambda for event-driven and bursty background work. The two combine well in one system.

    Note

    API Gateway is the usual front door for serverless designs because it adds API keys, usage plans, throttling, request validation, and authorization (Amazon Cognito or Lambda authorizers). With containers, an Application Load Balancer fills the routing role, and you add authentication in the application or with API Gateway in front. Task 2.4 and 2.5 cover the details.

    Deployment strategies for model access

    "Deployment" in GenAI has two meanings, and the exam uses both. One is how you deploy your application code. The other, more specific to this field, is how you obtain model capacity. At design level you choose among five access modes, and the right one depends on traffic shape, model choice, and whether you customized the model.

    ModeHow it worksBillingChoose it when
    On-demand (Bedrock)Call the model by ID or inference profile; capacity is shared and subject to quotasPer input and output tokenVariable or unknown traffic, PoCs, most production workloads to start
    Cross-Region inference profileOn-demand calls routed across Regions for more throughput and resiliencePer token, same model pricing structureYou hit quotas or need resilience, and residency rules allow the routing scope
    Batch inference (Bedrock)Submit a file of prompts; results land in S3 laterPer token, at a discount versus on-demandLarge offline jobs with no interactive latency requirement
    Provisioned Throughput (Bedrock)You buy dedicated model units for a model, with a commitment term of none, one month, or six monthsPer hour per model unit, regardless of useSteady, high, predictable traffic; guaranteed throughput; and for models you customized in Bedrock, which must be served through it
    SageMaker AI real-time endpointYou choose the instance type and count, deploy a model container, and manage scalingPer instance hourModels not offered in Bedrock, custom serving stacks, or tight control over hardware

    Two refinements sit inside this table. Provisioned Throughput offers three commitment levels (none, one month, six months), and the longer the term, the lower the hourly price, but a committed purchase cannot be deleted until the term ends. Separately, on-demand calls accept a serviceTier setting: standard by default, priority for lower latency at a premium, flex for discounted best-effort work that may queue, and a reserved tier for dedicated capacity on a one- or three-month commitment. Reserved is a service tier, not Provisioned Throughput.

    Most designs start on-demand, because it needs no commitment and no capacity planning. You then move to provisioned capacity only when measurements justify it. The break-even is simple in principle. Provisioned Throughput costs a fixed amount per hour, so it wins when your sustained token volume would cost more on-demand than the hourly fee for the units you need. For spiky or low traffic it loses, because you pay for idle capacity.

    A hybrid deployment is common and tested. For example, serve baseline traffic from Provisioned Throughput for a predictable cost and latency floor, and overflow to on-demand through application logic when demand spikes. Or serve a fine-tuned model from SageMaker AI for one task and use Bedrock on-demand models for everything else, behind one internal API so that callers do not care. Task 2.2 develops these deployment decisions.

    Watch out

    Provisioned Throughput bills every hour it exists, whether or not you send a request. It is easy to leave one running after an experiment, and a one-month or six-month commitment cannot be cancelled early. In a learning project, avoid it unless you are deliberately testing it, and delete it right away. Also set maxTokens explicitly on every call. Leaving it unset can reserve far more of your token quota than the request needs and cause throttling that looks unexplained.

    Application deployment strategy

    The second meaning is how you ship the system. Treat prompts, guardrail configurations, and model identifiers as deployable artifacts, not as hard-coded strings, because they change as often as code and can break behavior just as badly. Infrastructure as code (AWS CDK or CloudFormation) defines the resources. A pipeline (CodePipeline and CodeBuild) runs tests, including prompt regression tests against a golden dataset, before promotion. Staged rollout (canary or blue/green) limits the blast radius of a prompt or model change. Task 2.3 covers the pipeline, and Task 1.2 shows how to switch models without redeploying code.

    Exam tip

    Match the access mode to the traffic pattern. "Unpredictable traffic, minimize cost" means on-demand. "Consistent high throughput, predictable latency" means Provisioned Throughput. "Process 500,000 records overnight at lowest cost" means batch inference. "Needs a model not available in Bedrock, or custom container control" means SageMaker AI. "Hit throughput limits in one Region" means cross-Region inference, subject to residency.

    Proof of concept: validate before you build

    A design on paper is a hypothesis. A proof of concept (PoC) tests the riskiest parts of that hypothesis cheaply. For GenAI the risks are different from ordinary software. Rarely is it unclear whether you can call a model. The unknowns are whether the quality is good enough on your data, whether the latency fits the experience, and whether the cost per request makes business sense. A PoC answers those three questions with numbers.

    Step 1: State the hypothesis and the exit criteria

    Write down, before building, what would make you proceed and what would make you stop. For example: "A RAG assistant over our 500 support articles answers at least 85% of a 50-question test set correctly, responds with first token in under 2 seconds at the 95th percentile, and costs under two cents per question." Exit criteria stop the PoC from drifting into an open-ended experiment, and they give the business a clear go or no-go.

    Step 2: Build a golden dataset

    A golden dataset is a fixed set of realistic inputs, each with the expected answer or a clear grading rule. It is the most valuable PoC artifact because you reuse it for every later model comparison, prompt change, and regression test (Domain 5). Start with 30 to 100 items. Include a mix: typical questions, hard edge cases, questions whose answer is not in your data (to check the system declines rather than invents), and a few adversarial inputs. Ask real users or support staff for questions instead of inventing them, because invented questions are cleaner than real ones and make results look better than they will be.

    Step 3: Establish a baseline, then measure three things

    First, run the simplest possible version, often just a direct call with a decent prompt. Anything more elaborate must beat it. Then measure:

    • Quality. Score each answer against the golden set with a small rubric (for example, 0 wrong, 1 partly right, 2 right). Humans are the gold standard for a PoC of this size. You can add an LLM-as-a-judge, where a stronger model grades outputs against a rubric, to scale up. Amazon Bedrock Model Evaluation supports automatic metrics, LLM-as-a-judge, and human review workflows, which Chapter 5 and Domain 5 cover.
    • Latency. Record time to first token and total time, and report percentiles (p50 and p95), not averages, because the slow tail is what users remember.
    • Cost. Record input and output tokens for every call. The Converse response includes a usage block with these counts, so you never need to estimate.

    A tiny harness is enough. It sends each golden question to each candidate model and records the answer, tokens, and latency, ready to paste into a spreadsheet for scoring.

    import csv, time
import boto3

client = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime")  # use your project's selected Region

def ask(model_id: str, question: str) -> dict:
    start = time.perf_counter()
    resp = client.converse(
        modelId=model_id,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": question}]}],
        inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 512, "temperature": 0.0},
    )
    return {
        "answer": resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"],
        "in_tokens": resp["usage"]["inputTokens"],
        "out_tokens": resp["usage"]["outputTokens"],
        "wall_ms": round((time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000),
        "server_ms": resp["metrics"]["latencyMs"],
    }

def run(models: list[str], questions: list[str], out_path: str) -> None:
    with open(out_path, "w", newline="") as f:
        w = csv.writer(f)
        w.writerow(["question", "model", "answer", "in_tokens", "out_tokens", "wall_ms", "server_ms"])
        for q in questions:
            for m in models:
                r = ask(m, q)
                w.writerow([q, m, r["answer"], r["in_tokens"], r["out_tokens"], r["wall_ms"], r["server_ms"]])

    The code is deliberately plain. It uses the Converse API, which gives one request and response shape across model families, so swapping the candidate is a one-string change. Setting temperature to 0 makes runs more repeatable, which helps comparison. Note that maxTokens is set explicitly.

    A worked cost estimate

    Cost per request is (input tokens × input price) + (output tokens × output price). The prices below are illustrative round numbers, not current list prices, so always check the Bedrock pricing page for the model you use.

    Larger modelSmaller model
    Illustrative input price$3.00 per million tokens$0.25 per million tokens
    Illustrative output price$15.00 per million tokens$1.25 per million tokens
    Request: 2,000 input + 400 output tokens$0.006 + $0.006 = $0.012$0.0005 + $0.0005 = $0.001
    At 100,000 requests per month$1,200$100

    A twelvefold gap is typical between model tiers. If your golden set shows the smaller model is good enough for the task, you have just saved more than a thousand dollars a month before writing any optimization code. This is why PoC measurement belongs before architecture commitment. Chapter 2 and Domain 4 build on this idea with tiered model use and model cascading.

    Step 4: Time-box and decide

    Give the PoC a fixed budget, commonly one to three weeks. At the end, compare results with the exit criteria and decide: proceed, change approach (for example, add RAG or move to a different model), or stop. Stopping is a successful outcome if the numbers show the idea does not pay off. The PoC's whole purpose is to make that call cheap.

    AWS tools that speed up a PoC

    • Bedrock playgrounds in the console let you chat with a model, tune parameters, and compare two models side by side with no code. They are the fastest way to form a first impression.
    • Bedrock Prompt Management lets you save prompt versions and variants so you can compare them (Chapter 6).
    • Bedrock Knowledge Bases stand up a working RAG pipeline from an S3 bucket in minutes, which is ideal for testing whether RAG helps at all.
    • Bedrock Model Evaluation runs your dataset against chosen models and scores the results.
    • Amazon Q Developer or Kiro, the AI coding assistants, speed up writing the PoC harness itself.

    PoC versus production: the gap checklist

    A PoC that passes its exit criteria is not production-ready. Naming the gaps early prevents the common failure of shipping a demo. Use this checklist when moving from PoC to design for full deployment.

    AreaPoC typically doesProduction must add
    DataA small, hand-cleaned sampleValidation, ongoing ingestion, refresh, access control by user
    QualityOne-time scoring on a golden setContinuous evaluation, regression tests, human feedback loop
    SafetyNone, or ad hocGuardrails, prompt injection defenses, PII handling
    ReliabilitySingle call, no retriesRetries with backoff, fallbacks, circuit breakers, quotas planned
    SecurityAdmin credentials, public endpointLeast-privilege IAM roles, VPC endpoints, encryption keys, audit logging
    OperationsPrint statementsMetrics, traces, token and cost dashboards, alarms
    CostIgnoredBudgets, per-team tagging, caching, model tiering
    DeliveryNotebook or console clicksInfrastructure as code, CI/CD, staged rollout

    Exam tip

    For "validate feasibility with the least effort and cost" scenarios, the answer is usually the Bedrock playground, Knowledge Bases quick-create, or Model Evaluation on a small dataset, not training a model or building a custom pipeline. For "compare candidate models objectively", look for a golden dataset plus Bedrock Model Evaluation or a scripted comparison, not anecdotal chat sessions.

    Hands-on lab

    Compare two models on a 10-question golden set

    About 30 minutes · Cost: a few cents of on-demand token usage · Needs: Bedrock model access in your selected Region, a spreadsheet

    1. Pick a small, real task you care about, for example answering questions about a product return policy or summarizing support emails. Write 10 questions in a spreadsheet with columns ID, Question, and Expected answer or grading rule. Include 6 typical items, 2 edge cases, 1 question whose answer is not available, and 1 tricky or adversarial one.
    2. Open the AWS console, switch to your project's selected Region, and open Amazon Bedrock.
    3. In the left navigation, open the Model catalog and confirm you can use two models of different sizes from the same family, such as a larger and a smaller one. Most models need no enablement step, but Anthropic models ask for a one-time use-case form before first use, and some third-party models need a Marketplace subscription or license acceptance.
    4. Open Playgrounds and choose the Chat playground. Select the larger model. Under configurations, set temperature to 0 and a maximum output length such as 512.
    5. Paste a short system prompt that matches your task (for example, "Answer only from the policy text provided. If the answer is not in the text, say you do not know."), then ask question 1. Read the answer.
    6. Turn on Compare mode in the playground and select the smaller model as the second model. Send the same question to both. If your console version has no compare toggle, open a second playground tab with the smaller model and paste the same question. Where the console shows metrics under a response, note latency and input and output token counts, and check the console for the current metric labels.
    7. Repeat for all 10 questions. In your spreadsheet, add columns for each model: Answer, Score (0, 1, 2), Latency, Tokens in, Tokens out.
    8. Total each model's score and compute average tokens. Using the pricing page for the two models, calculate cost per request and a monthly cost at a volume you choose.
    9. Decide: is the cheaper model within an acceptable quality gap? Which questions did it fail, and could a better prompt fix them? Write two sentences of conclusion. This is a PoC exit report in miniature.
    10. Optional: run the same questions through the Python harness above to collect exact token counts and latencies automatically, and compare with your manual numbers.

    Cleanup. Playground usage creates no persistent resources. Nothing to delete. Keep the spreadsheet; you will reuse it as a golden dataset in later chapters.

    The Well-Architected Framework applied to GenAI

    The AWS Well-Architected Framework is AWS's structured checklist for judging whether a workload is built well. It organizes advice into six pillars: Operational Excellence, Security, Reliability, Performance Efficiency, Cost Optimization, and Sustainability. Each pillar poses questions ("How do you manage access to your workload?") and lists best practices that answer them. You do not memorize the questions. You use the pillars as a lens to make sure a design has not forgotten a whole category of concern.

    For GenAI, every pillar picks up concerns that ordinary applications do not have.

    PillarGenAI-specific questions to ask in design
    Operational ExcellenceAre prompts, guardrails, and model IDs versioned and deployed through a pipeline? Can you trace a bad answer back to the prompt version, model, and retrieved passages that produced it? Do you run prompt regression tests before release?
    SecurityIs access to models and data least-privilege? Is traffic kept private with VPC endpoints? Are prompt injection and data leakage handled? Is sensitive data masked before it reaches a model or a log?
    ReliabilityWhat happens when the model is throttled, slow, or unavailable? Are there retries with backoff, a fallback model, or a graceful degraded mode? Are quotas planned for peak load?
    Performance EfficiencyIs the model sized to the task? Do you stream responses? Is retrieval tuned? Have you benchmarked models on your own data and not only on public leaderboards?
    Cost OptimizationDo you track tokens per request and cost per team or feature? Do you use caching, smaller models for simple tasks, and batch mode for offline work? Is context kept lean?
    SustainabilityAre you using the smallest model that meets the need, avoiding unnecessary calls, and preferring managed or shared capacity over idle dedicated hardware?

    The Generative AI Lens
