    A lens is an extension of the Framework for a specific kind of workload. AWS publishes lenses for areas such as serverless, SaaS, and machine learning. The Generative AI Lens adds guidance specific to building on foundation models, whether they run on Amazon Bedrock or are customer-managed on SageMaker AI. It keeps the six pillars but adds best practices about topics the general Framework does not cover: choosing and evaluating models, customizing them, prompt engineering, RAG and data architecture, agents, responsible AI, and operating a probabilistic system. The lens also walks through six lifecycle stages of a GenAI workload: scoping, model selection, customization, development, deployment, and continuous improvement. It highlights responsible AI practices and the shared responsibility between model producers, providers, and consumers.

    A related lens is the Machine Learning Lens, which covers traditional ML applications built on SageMaker AI, and the Generative AI Lens itself points to it for that case. For this exam, the Generative AI Lens is the one named in the exam guide. It appears in the Well-Architected Tool's Lens Catalog under the name Generative AI.

    Note

    The lens organizes its focus areas under the six pillars. Operational excellence covers consistent output quality, traceability, lifecycle automation, and deciding when to customize a model. Security covers protecting endpoints, harmful outputs and excessive agency, securing prompts, and model poisoning. Reliability covers throughput, graceful failure, versioning artifacts, and distributing inference. Performance efficiency covers capturing and improving model performance and retrieval performance. Cost optimization covers cost-optimized models, prompt engineering for cost, and optimizing vector stores and agent workflows. Sustainability covers minimizing compute for customization, hosting, data processing, and storage. The lens is updated periodically (its latest revision at the time of writing is dated November 19, 2025), so skim the current version once. The exam tests applying the Framework and lens to a GenAI workload to find risks and standardize practice, and it is unlikely to ask you to recite lens questions.

    The AWS Well-Architected Tool

    The Well-Architected Tool is a free console service that turns the Framework and lenses into an interactive review. The workflow is short.

    1. Define a workload. Give it a name, description, environment (production or pre-production), and the Regions it runs in.
    2. Choose lenses. The Framework is always applied. You add the Generative AI Lens, or any other, from the lens catalog.
    3. Answer questions. For each pillar and lens you select which best practices you follow. You can mark a question not applicable and add notes.
    4. Review the findings. The tool classifies gaps as high-risk issues or medium-risk issues and generates an improvement plan that links to guidance.
    5. Save a milestone. A milestone is a snapshot, so you can show improvement over time and re-review after major changes.

    You can also define custom lenses to encode your own organization's rules, which is relevant to standardization in the next section.

    Hands-on lab

    Review a GenAI workload with the Generative AI Lens

    About 25 minutes · Cost: free · Needs: access to the AWS Well-Architected Tool in your project

    1. In the AWS console, switch to your selected Region and search for AWS Well-Architected Tool. If the service is not available in your project, read the Generative AI Lens guide in the AWS documentation instead and answer the questions in a notes file.
    2. Choose Define workload. Enter a name such as customer-chatbot, a short description, the review owner, the environment (Pre-production), and your Region.
    3. On the lens step, confirm that AWS Well-Architected Framework is selected and add the Generative AI lens from the Lens Catalog. Choose Define workload.
    4. Open the workload, select the Generative AI Lens, and choose Start reviewing.
    5. Read the first pillar's questions without answering yet. Note which ones relate to topics in this chapter: model selection, prompt management, data and retrieval, guardrails, observability, and cost tracking.
    6. Answer ten questions honestly for a project you have in mind or a planned one. Choose None of these where you have not done the practice. Use the notes box to record why.
    7. Open the Improvement plan for the lens and read the high-risk items the tool raised from your answers. Pick the two you would fix first and write the reason.
    8. Choose Save milestone and name it something like initial-design.

    Cleanup. The tool has no charge. If you do not want to keep the review, delete the workload from the workload list.

    Standardized components for consistent delivery

    One team building one GenAI application can get by with ad hoc choices. Ten teams doing it will produce ten ways to call a model, ten logging formats, and ten different answers to "do we filter personal data?" Skill 1.1.3 is about avoiding that by providing a shared, tested foundation that each team starts from. The idea is the same as an internal platform or paved road in a normal engineering organization.

    Reusable infrastructure

    Package your approved architecture as infrastructure as code. With the AWS Cloud Development Kit (CDK), you write infrastructure in a programming language, and you can wrap a whole pattern (API Gateway, Lambda, guardrail, log groups, alarms, KMS key) into a single reusable construct that teams instantiate with a few parameters. CloudFormation modules and templates serve the same purpose in YAML. AWS Service Catalog sits on top: platform teams publish approved products (for example, "RAG application baseline") into a portfolio, and application teams launch them self-service, with constraints that prevent them from changing the parts that security cares about. The point is that the secure and compliant way is also the easiest way.

    Note

    Service Catalog is on the exam's in-scope list, so learn it. If your own AWS project was created through the newer sign-up experience, some services such as Service Catalog may not be available to you. Check the supported-services list for your plan before planning a lab around one.

    A shared client library and gateway

    Most inconsistency happens in the calling code. A small internal library that wraps the Bedrock client removes repeated decisions: the retry policy, the timeout, mandatory maxTokens, the default guardrail, and structured logging of token usage. Teams import it rather than calling boto3 directly.

    import json
import logging
import time

import boto3
from botocore.config import Config

log = logging.getLogger("genai")


class GenAIClient:
    def __init__(self, model_id, guardrail_id=None, guardrail_version=None, region=None):
        self._model_id = model_id
        self._guardrail = (
            {"guardrailIdentifier": guardrail_id, "guardrailVersion": guardrail_version}
            if guardrail_id else None
        )
        self._rt = boto3.client(
            "bedrock-runtime",
            region_name=region,
            config=Config(retries={"max_attempts": 5, "mode": "adaptive"}, read_timeout=60),
        )

    def converse(self, messages, *, max_tokens, system=None, temperature=0.2, tags=None):
        kwargs = {
            "modelId": self._model_id,
            "messages": messages,
            "inferenceConfig": {"maxTokens": max_tokens, "temperature": temperature},
        }
        if system:
            kwargs["system"] = [{"text": system}]
        if self._guardrail:
            kwargs["guardrailConfig"] = self._guardrail
        if tags:
            kwargs["requestMetadata"] = tags  # string key/value pairs for cost and audit attribution

        start = time.perf_counter()
        resp = self._rt.converse(**kwargs)
        log.info(json.dumps({
            "model": self._model_id,
            "in_tokens": resp["usage"]["inputTokens"],
            "out_tokens": resp["usage"]["outputTokens"],
            "ms": round((time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000),
            "tags": tags or {},
        }))
        return resp

    Notice that max_tokens is a required keyword argument, so no team can forget it. Notice too that the model ID is injected rather than hard-coded, which sets up the dynamic model selection pattern in Chapter 2.

    The next step up is a GenAI gateway: a central service that all applications call instead of calling Bedrock directly. It can enforce authentication, apply guardrails, route among models, cache responses, meter usage per team, and write a uniform audit log in one place. A library standardizes behavior inside each application, while a gateway standardizes it at the network edge. The gateway costs more to build and becomes a critical shared dependency, so organizations usually adopt it once several teams are in production. Task 2.3 covers gateway architectures.

    Shared baselines

    Beyond code, standardize the policies that every GenAI workload should inherit.

    • Prompt baseline. A template skeleton with a role definition, refusal rules, citation format, and output format, stored and versioned in Bedrock Prompt Management or a repository. Teams extend it and do not start from a blank page.
    • Guardrail baseline. A shared Bedrock guardrail with organization-wide content filters, denied topics, and PII handling, referenced by every application. Teams may add stricter guardrails but not remove the baseline.
    • Logging baseline. Model invocation logging to CloudWatch Logs or S3, encrypted with a KMS key, with defined retention and restricted access. Remember that masking applied by a guardrail affects the response, while logs may still capture the original text, so the log store itself must be protected.
    • Identity baseline. Standard IAM role patterns and permission boundaries that grant invocation rights for approved models only.
    • Tagging and cost baseline. Required tags and application inference profiles so spend can be attributed by team and feature.
    • Evaluation baseline. A common golden-dataset format and a pipeline stage that runs it before promotion.

    Backend analogy

    This is the same move as a company-wide service template, a shared logging middleware, and an internal API gateway. Nothing about it is exotic. The GenAI-specific part is the content of the baselines: guardrails, prompt templates, token metering, and evaluation sets.

    Exam tip

    When a scenario describes many teams that need consistent, compliant GenAI implementations, look for a combination of: approved IaC (CDK or CloudFormation) published through AWS Service Catalog, a shared guardrail and prompt baseline, and a Well-Architected review with the Generative AI Lens. An answer that lets each team configure its own controls is almost always wrong.

    Chapter recap

    • Turn requests into measurable functional and non-functional requirements. For GenAI, quality, token cost, time to first token, and data residency are the ones that usually decide the design.
    • Ask whether GenAI is needed. Narrow tasks often fit Textract, Comprehend, search, classical ML, or plain code better.
    • Climb the build-versus-buy ladder only as far as needed: finished applications such as Q Business, then Bedrock managed blocks, then Bedrock APIs with your code, then customized models, then SageMaker AI.
    • Six patterns cover most solutions: direct call, RAG, agentic, async or batch, workflow-orchestrated, and multimodal. Prefer a workflow over an agent when the steps are known.
    • Match model access to traffic: on-demand for variable load, cross-Region profiles for throughput (mind the residency scope), batch for offline volume, Provisioned Throughput for steady high load and most customized models, SageMaker AI for models or control Bedrock does not offer.
    • A PoC needs a hypothesis, exit criteria, a golden dataset, a baseline, and measured quality, latency (p50 and p95), and cost per request. Then check the gap list before production.
    • Apply the six Well-Architected pillars and the Generative AI Lens, using the Well-Architected Tool to record and track findings.
    • Standardize through reusable IaC, Service Catalog, a shared client library or gateway, and baselines for prompts, guardrails, logging, identity, tagging, and evaluation.

    Knowledge check

    1. A company wants an internal assistant that answers employee questions using content in SharePoint, Confluence, and S3. Answers must respect each employee's existing document permissions. The company has no ML engineers and wants the least development effort. Which solution fits best?

      A. A custom RAG application with OpenSearch and Lambda. B. Amazon Q Business with the data source connectors. C. A fine-tuned model on SageMaker AI. D. A Bedrock agent with a custom action group per data source.

      Answer

      B. Q Business is a managed assistant with connectors and user-level permission awareness, which matches "least development effort" and "no ML engineers". A and D require building and operating retrieval and permission handling yourself. C is the highest-effort option, and fine-tuning does not solve per-user document access.

    2. A media company must classify 3 million archived articles over the weekend. No user is waiting, and cost must be minimized. Which approach is best?

      A. Provision throughput for the model for one month. B. Call the model synchronously from a single large Lambda function. C. Use Bedrock batch inference with prompts and results stored in S3. D. Deploy a SageMaker AI real-time endpoint sized for peak.

      Answer

      C. Batch inference is built for large offline jobs and is typically priced lower per token than on-demand. A commits to a month of hourly billing for a weekend job. B will hit timeouts and throttling. D pays for always-on instances for a one-off workload.

    3. A European bank is building a chatbot on Bedrock. Regulations require that prompts and responses are processed only within the EU. The team is hitting on-demand throughput limits in a single Region. What should they do?

      A. Use a global cross-Region inference profile. B. Use an EU geographic cross-Region inference profile for the model. C. Copy documents to a US Region with more capacity. D. Disable the guardrail to reduce latency.

      Answer

      B. A geographic profile routes only among Regions in the EU, which raises throughput while respecting residency. A can route outside the EU. C moves data out of the permitted area. D is unrelated to throughput and removes a safety control.

    4. An insurer needs a process that extracts fields from a claim form, validates them against rules, summarizes the claim, and routes it for human approval when the amount exceeds a threshold. Every step must be auditable, with retries on failure. Which design fits best?

      A. An autonomous agent that decides which tools to call. B. An AWS Step Functions workflow with Bedrock and Lambda steps and a human approval state. C. A single large prompt that performs all the steps. D. A fine-tuned model that outputs the final decision.

      Answer

      B. The steps are fixed and known, and the requirements stress auditability, retries, and human approval, which are native to Step Functions. A adds unpredictability and cost without need. C gives no per-step control or audit trail. D does not provide approval or validation steps.

    5. A team must choose between three candidate models for a summarization feature. They want objective evidence before committing. What should they do first?

      A. Pick the largest model, since it is the most capable. B. Chat with each model for a few minutes and choose the one that feels best. C. Build a golden dataset of representative inputs, run all three models on it, and compare quality, latency percentiles, and cost per request. D. Fine-tune all three models and compare them.

      Answer

      C. A golden dataset gives repeatable, comparable measurements on the team's own data. A ignores cost and latency, and often over-pays for a simple task. B is anecdotal. D is expensive and premature before a baseline exists.

    6. Twelve product teams in an enterprise are building GenAI applications. The security team requires every application to use approved guardrails, structured invocation logging, and standard IAM patterns. Which approach best achieves consistency with the least friction for teams?

      A. Publish a written guideline and ask teams to follow it. B. Provide approved CDK or CloudFormation products through AWS Service Catalog that include the shared guardrail, logging, and IAM baseline, plus a shared client library, and review workloads with the Generative AI Lens. C. Let each team design its own controls and audit them annually. D. Route all teams through one engineer who builds every application.

      Answer

      B. It makes the compliant path the easy path through reusable infrastructure and shared code, and it adds a structured review. A depends on voluntary compliance. C detects problems late. D does not scale and creates a bottleneck.

    Chapter 2 · Task 1.2

    Select and Configure Foundation Models

    Choosing a model is the first decision in a GenAI system, and the one you should expect to revisit. Models get cheaper, get retired, hit capacity limits, and get replaced by better ones. This chapter covers how to choose well, how to build the application so the choice stays reversible, how to keep it running when a model or Region has a bad day, and how to manage models you customize yourself.

    Exam skills covered

    • 1.2.1 Assess and choose FMs against business use cases and technical requirements (benchmarks, capability analysis, limitations)
    • 1.2.2 Build architecture patterns that switch models and providers without code changes (Lambda, API Gateway, AppConfig)
    • 1.2.3 Design resilient systems (Step Functions circuit breakers, cross-Region inference, fallbacks, graceful degradation)
    • 1.2.4 Deploy and manage customized models (SageMaker AI, LoRA and adapters, Model Registry, rollback, retirement)

    Choosing a foundation model

    A foundation model (FM) is a large pretrained model that you use through an API or host yourself. On Amazon Bedrock, AWS runs the models for you and exposes them through a common set of APIs. You pick a model by passing its ID, and AWS handles the GPUs, scaling, and patching. The catalog covers several providers: Anthropic (the Claude family), Amazon (Nova models and the older Titan models), Meta (Llama), Mistral AI, Cohere, AI21 Labs, DeepSeek, Stability AI, and others. The exact list changes every few months, so never memorize it. Learn the selection method instead, because that is what the exam tests.

    The exam almost never asks "which model is best." It asks which model, or which kind of model, fits a scenario with constraints. Those constraints fall into a small set of dimensions, and it helps to run through them in the same order each time.

    The selection dimensions

    Modality. What goes in and what comes out. Text-in, text-out is the common case. Some models also accept images, documents such as PDFs, audio, or video as input. Others generate images or video, and a separate class produces embeddings (vectors), which you will meet in Chapter 5. If the use case is "extract fields from scanned invoices," you need a multimodal input model, or a pipeline that runs OCR first. If it is "generate product images," you need an image generation model, and no amount of prompting will make a text-only model do it.

    Context window. The context window is the maximum number of tokens the model can handle in one request, input and output combined. A token is a chunk of text, roughly three quarters of an English word on average. A model with a 200,000 token window can take a long contract in one request, while a model with 8,000 tokens cannot. Do not treat a big window as free. You pay per token, long inputs raise latency, and many models recall details less reliably from the middle of a very long prompt. For RAG systems the window sets how many retrieved chunks you can afford to include.

    Maximum output length. This is separate from the window and usually much smaller. A model that can read 200,000 tokens may be limited to a few thousand or tens of thousands of output tokens. A task like "rewrite this 60-page document" fails if the output cap is below what the rewrite needs.

    Latency. Two numbers matter. Time to first token decides how responsive a chat UI feels. Total generation time decides how long a batch job or an agent loop takes. Smaller models in a family are much faster. Bedrock also offers latency-optimized inference for some models, and service tiers (priority, standard, flex, and reserved) that trade price against speed or guaranteed capacity.

    Cost. Bedrock on-demand pricing is per token, with separate prices for input and output tokens. Output tokens usually cost several times more than input tokens. Larger models in a family cost many times more than smaller ones. Compute cost per request as (input tokens × input price) + (output tokens × output price), multiply by expected traffic, and compare. A model that costs ten times more per token can still be cheaper if it solves the task in one call where a smaller model needs three attempts, but you only find that out by measuring on your own task.

    Capabilities and features. Does the model support tool use (function calling), structured JSON output, system prompts, extended reasoning, prompt caching, streaming, and guardrail integration? Not every model supports every feature through the Converse API. An agent design that depends on tool calling rules out any model without it.

    Language and domain. A model that is strong in English may be weaker in Hindi or Japanese. Code, legal, and medical text have their own failure modes. General benchmarks rarely tell you about your language or domain.

    Region availability. Not every model is offered in every AWS Region, and new models usually launch in a few Regions first. Your data residency rules may restrict you to one Region or one geography. This single constraint eliminates more candidate models in real projects than any quality difference does. Cross-Region inference, covered later in this chapter, partly relaxes it.

    Licensing and data terms. Bedrock states that your prompts and outputs are not used to train the underlying provider models and are not shared with model providers. That is a major reason enterprises use Bedrock over calling a provider's public API. Some third-party models carry their own end-user license agreements or require an AWS Marketplace subscription. Read the model card in the Bedrock model catalog before you commit.

    DimensionQuestion to askTypical exam signal
    ModalityWhat inputs and outputs are required?"scanned documents," "images," "audio calls"
    Context windowHow much text must fit in one request?"entire contract," "hundreds of pages"
    LatencyIs a human waiting for the answer?"real-time chat," "sub-second"
    CostWhat is the price per request at expected volume?"minimize cost," "high-volume simple queries"
    FeaturesAre tools, JSON output, or reasoning needed?"call internal APIs," "structured output"
    RegionIs the model offered where the data must stay?"data must remain in the EU"
    CustomizationCan it be fine-tuned if prompting is not enough?"domain-specific terminology"

    Exam tip

    When a question describes a simple, high-volume task such as classification, routing, or short summarization, the best answer is almost always a smaller, cheaper model in a family. When it describes complex multi-step reasoning, long documents, or code, a larger model is justified. Answers that say "use the largest model to be safe" are distractors.

    Reading benchmarks without being fooled

    Providers publish benchmark scores for reasoning, coding, math, and knowledge tests. These are useful for narrowing a field of twenty models to three. They are poor at picking the winner among those three, for several reasons. Public benchmarks leak into training data, so scores can reflect memorization. Benchmarks measure averages across tasks, and your task may sit in a weak corner. Prompts used in benchmark runs are tuned by the vendor, while your prompt is not. And benchmarks say nothing about cost, latency, or your tone requirements.

    So the working method is a funnel. Use public benchmarks and the dimensions above to cut the catalog to a short list. Then build a small evaluation set from your own data, and run every candidate against it. That set can be as small as 50 to 200 representative prompts with reference answers or a scoring rubric. Compare quality, latency, and cost together. This is what the exam means by "capability analysis" and "limitation evaluation."

    Backend analogy

    Choosing an FM is like choosing a database engine. Vendor benchmarks narrow the field, but you load your own schema and query mix into the final candidates and measure. You also check licensing, available Regions, and operational limits before anything else.

    Amazon Bedrock Model Evaluation

    Bedrock includes a built-in evaluation service so you do not have to write the harness yourself. You can run it in three styles. Automatic evaluation uses built-in or custom datasets and programmatic metrics such as accuracy, robustness, and toxicity for tasks like summarization, question answering, classification, and open-ended generation. LLM-as-a-judge evaluation uses a second model to score outputs on dimensions such as correctness, completeness, helpfulness, and responsible AI measures, and it is far cheaper than human review. Human evaluation sends outputs to your own workforce or an AWS-managed team to rate against criteria you define. Bedrock also supports evaluating RAG pipelines, covering both retrieval and generated answers. The evaluation job reads a dataset from Amazon S3, calls the models you selected, and writes a report back to S3. Chapter scope for Domain 1 is the idea of using it to pick a model. Domain 5 returns to evaluation in depth.

    Exam tip

    If a question asks how to compare several FMs on a company's own task with the least custom code, the answer is Bedrock Model Evaluation with a custom prompt dataset. If it asks for subjective quality such as brand tone, add human evaluation or LLM-as-a-judge. Building a custom evaluation harness on Lambda is usually the over-engineered distractor.

    Model families in practice

    Rather than listing every model, think in tiers. Most providers sell a family with a small, fast, cheap model, a mid-size balanced model, and a large, slow, expensive model. Anthropic names these Haiku, Sonnet, and Opus. Amazon names its Nova text models Micro, Lite, Pro, and Premier. Meta's Llama models come in several parameter sizes. The same pattern applies across vendors, which is why a good architecture can treat "small," "balanced," and "large" as roles and map them to specific model IDs through configuration.

    Family (example)Typical strengthsTypical use
    Anthropic ClaudeReasoning, long context, coding, tool use, careful instruction followingAgents, analysis of long documents, code, regulated-domain assistants
    Amazon NovaPrice-performance, multimodal input (text, image, video) on larger tiers, tight AWS integrationHigh-volume summarization, classification, document and video understanding
    Meta LlamaOpen-weight, customizable, available on Bedrock and SageMakerFine-tuning, workloads that want open-weight portability
    Mistral, Cohere, AI21, othersSpecific strengths such as multilingual text, retrieval, or rerankingMultilingual chat, enterprise search, embeddings and rerankers
    Amazon Titan Embeddings and Cohere EmbedProduce vectors, not textRAG indexing and semantic search (Chapters 4 and 5)
    Image and video models (Nova Canvas and Reel, Stability AI)Generate media from promptsMarketing assets, design drafts

    Note

    Treat the examples in this table as illustrations that were true when this guide was written. Providers release new versions often, and older versions move to a "legacy" state and are eventually retired. Check the Bedrock model catalog and the supported-models page in the AWS documentation for the current list in your Region.

    Inference parameters

    Once you have chosen a model, you configure how it generates. These are the sampling and length controls that appear in the inferenceConfig block of the Converse API. You already know the concepts from other platforms, so this section focuses on how Bedrock exposes them and where the exam draws its distinctions.

    Temperature rescales the probability distribution over the next token. Near 0 the model picks the most likely token almost every time, which gives consistent and repeatable output. Higher values flatten the distribution and produce more varied, creative, and less predictable text. Use low temperature for extraction, classification, SQL generation, and anything that must be consistent. Use higher temperature for brainstorming and creative writing. Even at 0 outputs are not guaranteed to be identical across calls.

    Top-p (nucleus sampling) restricts sampling to the smallest set of tokens whose cumulative probability reaches p. A top-p of 0.9 means the model samples only from the tokens that together cover 90 percent of the probability mass. Top-k restricts sampling to the k most likely tokens. Both trim the unlikely tail. A common rule is to adjust temperature or top-p, but not both at once, because their effects overlap.

    Max tokens caps the length of the generated response. It is the most important parameter to set deliberately on Bedrock, for two reasons. First, it bounds cost and latency, because generation stops at the cap. Second, Bedrock reserves capacity against your token-per-minute quota at the start of each request using the input size plus this value. If you leave it unset, the request may default to the model's maximum output length and reserve far more quota than it will use. That can trigger throttling even when your real usage is low. Set it to a value slightly above the longest answer you expect.

    Stop sequences are strings that end generation when the model produces them. They are useful for structured outputs, such as stopping at a closing delimiter, and for stopping role-play style continuations. When a response ends because of a stop sequence, the Converse response reports a stop reason of stop_sequence. When it ends because it hit the cap, the stop reason is max_tokens, and the answer may be cut off mid-sentence. Always check the stop reason in production code.

    import boto3

bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime")

resp = bedrock.converse(
    modelId="us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6",   # example ID, verify in your Region
    system=[{"text": "You extract fields from invoices and reply with JSON only."}],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": invoice_text}]}],
    inferenceConfig={
        "maxTokens": 600,          # always set explicitly
        "temperature": 0.0,        # extraction: favor consistency
        "topP": 0.9,
        "stopSequences": ["</json>"],
    },
    # topK and other provider-specific knobs go here, not in inferenceConfig
    additionalModelRequestFields={"top_k": 40},
)

print(resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"])
print(resp["stopReason"], resp["usage"])   # inputTokens, outputTokens, totalTokens

    Notice where top_k lives. The Converse API standardizes maxTokens, temperature, topP, and stopSequences across models. Parameters that only some providers support, such as top-k, go in additionalModelRequestFields using that provider's own field names. This is the main place where "unified" Converse still leaks model-specific details, and it comes up again in the next section.

    Exam tip

    Match parameter to goal. Deterministic extraction or classification means low temperature. Creative variety means higher temperature or top-p. Responses cut off mid-sentence mean max tokens is too low (check stopReason). Unexpected throttling with light traffic points to an unset or oversized max tokens. Know that these settings change behavior and cost but never change what the model knows.

    Hands-on lab

    Compare two models and sweep temperature in the playground

    About 20 minutes · Cost: a few cents of on-demand tokens · Needs: Bedrock access to at least two text models in your selected Region

    1. Sign in to the AWS console and confirm you are in the Region assigned to your project (shown at the top right).
    2. Open Amazon Bedrock. In the left navigation, find the Playgrounds section and open Chat. If you are asked to choose between a text and a chat playground, choose chat.
    3. Click Select model. Choose a provider (for example Anthropic) and a small model such as a Haiku tier, then Apply. If the model requires a one-time use-case form, complete it.
    4. Paste this prompt: Extract vendor, date, and total from: "Acme Corp invoice dated 3 March 2025, amount due $1,284.50 including tax." Reply with JSON only. and run it. Note the answer, the latency, and the token counts shown after the response.
    5. Turn on Compare mode (in current console layouts this is a toggle near the top of the chat playground; if you do not see it, open the same prompt in two sessions and pick a different model in each). A second pane appears. In the second pane, select a different model, such as a Nova tier or a larger Claude tier. Run the same prompt in both panes.
    6. Open the Configurations panel on one side. Set Temperature to 0, run the prompt three times, and note whether the output changes. Set temperature to 1.0, run it three times again, and note the variation.
    7. Lower Response length (max tokens) to a very small number, such as 20, and run again. Observe the truncated answer and, if displayed, the stop reason.
    8. Now try a harder prompt, such as summarizing a long paragraph you paste in, and compare the two models on quality and speed. Record which model you would pick for each prompt and why, using the dimensions from the earlier section.

    Cleanup. Nothing persists. Playground requests are billed per token only. You can view the API request behind a run using View API request, which is a good way to copy settings into Boto3 code.

    Switching models without changing code

    Skill 1.2.2 asks you to build architectures where the model, and even the provider, can change without a code deployment. The motivation is practical. A model you chose in March may be superseded in June, may become unavailable in your Region, or may turn out too costly at real traffic. If the model ID is a string literal in a Python file, every change is a build, test, and release cycle. If it is configuration, a change is a controlled rollout that takes minutes and can be reversed just as fast.

    Three pieces make this work: a uniform way to call models, a place to keep configuration, and a facade that hides model details from callers.

    The Converse API as the uniform interface

    Bedrock offers two ways to call a model synchronously. InvokeModel sends a provider-specific JSON body. Anthropic expects an anthropic_version and a messages list, Titan expects inputText, Llama expects a prompt string with max_gen_len, and so on. Swapping providers therefore means rewriting the request and response parsing. Converse (and ConverseStream for streaming) uses one request and response shape for all models that support it: messages, system, inferenceConfig, toolConfig, and a standard output and usage in the response. This is the foundation of provider switching, and the reason AWS documentation recommends Converse by default.

    Converse is uniform, not identical. Some models do not support system prompts, tool use, or document inputs. Provider-specific parameters still go in additionalModelRequestFields. And a prompt written and tuned for one model often behaves differently on another, even when the API call succeeds. Swapping models is therefore a configuration change plus an evaluation step, never a configuration change alone. This is why Bedrock Prompt Management, covered in Task 1.6, lets you keep prompt variants per model.

    Exam tip

    "Switch between models from different providers with minimal code changes" points to the Converse API. "Use a provider-specific feature that Converse does not expose" points to InvokeModel. If both appear in the options, choose Converse unless the scenario names a feature gap.

    Where to keep the model choice

    Once calls are uniform, the model ID, inference parameters, and perhaps the prompt template name become data. The exam lists AWS AppConfig explicitly, so it deserves a careful look, but it helps to see the alternatives first.

    • Environment variables on a Lambda function. Simple, but changing one means updating the function configuration, which creates a new version of the config and restarts execution environments. There is no gradual rollout and no validation.
    • AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store. A hierarchical key-value store with versioning, IAM access control, and encryption through KMS. Reading a parameter is trivial, and standard parameters have no additional charge. It has no deployment strategy, so a change applies instantly to everyone, and it has no built-in validation or automatic rollback.
    • Amazon DynamoDB. Flexible and useful when the configuration is per tenant or per user, but you build the caching, versioning, and rollout logic yourself.
    • AWS AppConfig. A capability of Systems Manager built for exactly this problem: deploying configuration changes safely to running applications.

    AWS AppConfig in detail

    AppConfig separates configuration from code and adds the production safety features that a plain key-value store lacks. Its vocabulary is worth learning, because exam questions use it precisely.

    ConceptWhat it is
    ApplicationA logical grouping, such as one per GenAI service.
    EnvironmentA deployment target such as dev, staging, or prod, with optional CloudWatch alarms attached as monitors.
    Configuration profileThe definition of a configuration: where it lives and its type. Two types exist. Feature flags are structured booleans and attributes with built-in schema. Freeform is any content, such as JSON or YAML.
    Configuration sourceThe AppConfig hosted store, or an external source such as Parameter Store, S3, or Secrets Manager.
    ValidatorsA JSON Schema, or a Lambda function, that checks content before it can be deployed.
    Deployment strategyHow fast a new version spreads: all at once, linear (for example 20 percent every few minutes), or canary (a small percentage first, then the rest). It also sets a bake time, during which AppConfig watches the environment's alarms.
    DeploymentThe act of rolling a specific configuration version to an environment using a strategy.

    The features that matter for GenAI are the safety ones. Validation stops a typo such as a misspelled model ID from reaching production, if you encode the allowed values in a JSON Schema. Gradual deployment means a risky change, like moving 100 percent of traffic to a new model, reaches 10 percent of callers first. And if a CloudWatch alarm you attached to the environment fires during the deployment or bake time, for example because error rate or latency spiked, AppConfig rolls the change back automatically. That makes AppConfig a good fit for exam scenarios that mention "gradually roll out," "validate before deploying," or "automatic rollback" for model or prompt settings.

    Applications read configuration through the AppConfig Data API, using a session token and a polling interval, or through the AppConfig Lambda extension, which runs as a Lambda layer, caches the configuration locally, and serves it over a localhost HTTP endpoint. The extension is the usual choice in Lambda because it avoids network calls on every invocation and reduces cost.

    Backend analogy

    AppConfig is a managed remote-config and feature-flag service, closer to LaunchDarkly or Unleash than to a secrets vault. Parameter Store is closer to etcd or Consul KV: reliable storage with no rollout logic. If a scenario needs percentage rollouts or alarm-based rollback, think AppConfig.

    Exam tip

    Parameter Store versus AppConfig is a classic pairing. Choose Parameter Store for simple, static, or secret values that rarely change. Choose AppConfig when the change must be validated, rolled out gradually, monitored, and rolled back automatically. Feature flags inside AppConfig fit "enable the new model for 10 percent of users" or "turn on the new feature for a beta group."

    Reading the model ID from AppConfig

    Here is a minimal Lambda-style handler. The configuration is a small JSON document such as {"model_id": "us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6", "max_tokens": 800, "temperature": 0.2}. The code uses the AppConfig Data API and caches the result until the next poll.

    import json, time, boto3

appconfig = boto3.client("appconfigdata")
bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime")

APP, ENV, PROFILE = "genai-service", "prod", "model-config"
_token, _config, _next_poll = None, {}, 0.0

def get_config():
    global _token, _config, _next_poll
    if time.time() < _next_poll:
        return _config
    if _token is None:
        s = appconfig.start_configuration_session(
            ApplicationIdentifier=APP,
            EnvironmentIdentifier=ENV,
            ConfigurationProfileIdentifier=PROFILE,
            RequiredMinimumPollIntervalInSeconds=60,
        )
        _token = s["InitialConfigurationToken"]
    r = appconfig.get_latest_configuration(ConfigurationToken=_token)
    _token = r["NextPollConfigurationToken"]
    _next_poll = time.time() + r["NextPollIntervalInSeconds"]
    body = r["Configuration"].read()
    if body:                       # empty body means "unchanged since last poll"
        _config = json.loads(body)
    return _config

def handler(event, context):
    cfg = get_config()
    resp = bedrock.converse(
        modelId=cfg["model_id"],
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": event["prompt"]}]}],
        inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": cfg["max_tokens"],
                         "temperature": cfg["temperature"]},
    )
    return resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"]

    Two details are easy to miss. First, an empty response body from get_latest_configuration means nothing changed, so you keep the cached copy. Second, the IAM role of the function needs permission for appconfig:StartConfigurationSession and appconfig:GetLatestConfiguration on that profile, in addition to bedrock:InvokeModel for the models it may call. If you let configuration choose any model, scope the Bedrock permission to the set of models you actually allow, so a typo or a malicious change cannot select a model outside your approved list.

    The facade: Lambda and API Gateway

    The last piece is where this code lives. Clients such as a web app or another service should not know which model answers. Put an abstraction layer in front. Amazon API Gateway is a managed service for exposing HTTP APIs, with authentication, throttling, request validation, and usage plans. Behind it, AWS Lambda is a function-as-a-service runtime that runs your handler on demand. Callers send POST /generate with a task name and input. The Lambda reads configuration, maps the task to a model and prompt, calls Bedrock through Converse, normalizes the response, and returns it. Because every caller goes through this one contract, you can swap models, add a fallback, or change providers, and no client code changes.

    flowchart LR
  C["Client app"] --> AG["API Gateway"]
  AG --> L["Lambda facade"]
  L -->|"read config (cached)"| AC["AppConfig"]
  L -->|"Converse with configured model ID"| B["Amazon Bedrock"]
  AC -.->|"gradual deployment + alarm rollback"| L

    For containerized services, for example a FastAPI service on ECS, the same pattern applies, with the AppConfig agent running as a sidecar or the Data API called from your own code. The exam tends to describe the Lambda and API Gateway version, so know it well.

    Note

    Bedrock also supports application inference profiles. These are profiles you create that wrap a model, or a system-defined cross-Region profile, and give it a stable ARN you can tag for cost allocation. Applications can call the application profile's ARN as their model ID. That gives you a per-team or per-application handle for cost tracking and usage metrics, which complements AppConfig rather than replacing it.

    Hands-on lab

    Store and deploy a model configuration with AWS AppConfig

    About 25 minutes · Cost: effectively free at lab volumes (AppConfig bills per configuration request and per configuration received) · Needs: console access in your selected Region

    1. In the AWS console search bar, type AppConfig and open AWS AppConfig (it sits under AWS Systems Manager). Confirm your Region at the top right.
    2. Choose Create application. Name it genai-service and create it.
    3. On the application page, open the Environments tab and choose Create environment. Name it dev. Skip monitors for now. Create it.
    4. Open the Configuration profiles and feature flags tab and choose Create configuration. Select Freeform configuration. Name it model-config. For the source, keep AWS AppConfig hosted configuration.
    5. In the content editor, choose JSON and enter {"model_id": "us.anthropic.claude-haiku-4-5-20251001-v1:0", "max_tokens": 500, "temperature": 0.2}. Use a model ID that appears in your own Bedrock model catalog or inference profile list (run aws bedrock list-inference-profiles --region <your-region> to see them). The ID shown follows the pattern geography-prefix.provider.model-date-version. Some newer models cannot be called with the plain base model ID and fail with "on-demand throughput isn't supported", so the profile ID is the one to use. This lab only stores and deploys the string, so it works whichever ID you choose, but the ID must be one your project can actually invoke before you wire it to code. Save and create.
    6. Optional validator: on the profile, add a JSON Schema validator that requires model_id to be a string from an enumerated list of approved IDs, then save a new version. Try saving a version with an ID that is not in the list and observe that AppConfig rejects it.
    7. Choose Start deployment. Select environment dev, the hosted configuration version, and a deployment strategy. Pick a predefined strategy such as AppConfig.Linear20PercentEvery6Minutes to see how gradual rollout is expressed, or AppConfig.AllAtOnce if you want it finished quickly. Start the deployment and watch the status move through deploying, baking, and complete.
    8. Create a second version with a different model_id and deploy it. Note that the deployment history lists each version and that you can stop a deployment in progress, which rolls back to the previous version.
    9. Optional code step: open CloudShell (or your terminal with credentials), paste the get_config() function from this section into a Python file, and run it to read the deployed document. Your credentials need the two AppConfig data permissions described above.

    Cleanup. In AppConfig, delete the environment, then the configuration profile (delete hosted versions as prompted), then the application. Nothing here bills when idle, but removing it keeps the account tidy.

    Designing for failure

    A GenAI application depends on a remote, shared, capacity-constrained service that you do not control. Calls get throttled when you exceed your quota. Models time out on long generations. A Region can have an incident. A provider can retire the model you rely on. Skill 1.2.3 is about keeping the product working through all of this. The design toolbox has five layers, and a mature system uses several of them together: retry the call, route around a failing dependency, fail over to another model or Region, degrade the feature, and reserve capacity so you are less likely to hit limits at all.

    Classify the failure before you react

    Not every error deserves a retry. Retrying the wrong error wastes quota and hides bugs. The Bedrock runtime errors fall into two groups.

    ErrorMeaningRetry?
    ThrottlingException (HTTP 429)You exceeded requests-per-minute or tokens-per-minuteYes, with backoff
    ModelTimeoutException (408)The model took too long to respondYes, limited attempts
    ServiceUnavailableException (503)The service or model is temporarily unavailableYes, then fail over
    InternalServerException (500)Server-side failureYes, limited attempts
    ValidationException (400)Bad request: malformed body, unsupported parameter, wrong model ID typeNo, fix the request
    AccessDeniedException (403)IAM, model access, or an SCP is blocking the callNo, fix permissions
    ResourceNotFoundException (404)Model or profile not found in this RegionNo, fix the identifier

    Throttling comes in two forms and they need different fixes. A requests-per-minute limit is about call count and clears when the minute window rolls over. A tokens-per-minute limit is about volume, and Bedrock estimates it at the start of every request from the input size plus your maxTokens. Oversized maxTokens values therefore cause throttling that looks mysterious until you check how much quota each call reserves. Fix this at the source first, then add retries.

    Retries with exponential backoff and jitter

    The standard retry strategy waits longer after each failure, so a struggling service gets time to recover: wait 1 second, then 2, then 4, up to a cap. That is exponential backoff. Jitter adds randomness to each wait so that many clients that failed together do not all retry at the same instant and cause the next spike. AWS recommends "full jitter," where each wait is a random value between zero and the exponential limit.

    You rarely write this by hand. The AWS SDKs have built-in retry modes. In Boto3 you configure them through botocore.config.Config. The standard mode retries throttling and transient errors with backoff and jitter. The adaptive mode adds client-side rate limiting that slows the client down when it sees throttling, which suits Bedrock workloads that share a quota across many workers.

    import boto3
from botocore.config import Config

cfg = Config(
    retries={"max_attempts": 5, "mode": "adaptive"},
    read_timeout=120,        # long generations need more than the 60 s default
    connect_timeout=5,
)
bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", config=cfg)

    Two cautions. Retries multiply load, so cap the attempts and the total time budget, and make sure callers further up the chain do not also retry blindly, or one failure becomes nine requests. And streaming calls cannot be retried transparently after tokens have already been sent to the user, so decide whether a mid-stream failure restarts the response or surfaces an error.

    Exam tip

    For "intermittent throttling errors from Bedrock," the first-line answer is SDK retries with exponential backoff and jitter, together with right-sizing maxTokens. For sustained throttling at predictable high volume, the answer escalates to a quota increase, cross-Region inference, or provisioned throughput. API Gateway usage plans and throttling protect your own backend from clients, which is a different problem from handling Bedrock throttling.

    The circuit breaker pattern

    Retries help with brief hiccups. When a dependency is truly down, retrying every request makes things worse: each user waits through the full retry sequence, your workers pile up, and you add load to a service that is already failing. A circuit breaker tracks recent failures and, once a threshold is crossed, stops sending traffic to the failing dependency for a cooling-off period. Requests fail fast or go straight to a fallback. It has three states.

    stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Closed
  Closed --> Open: failures reach threshold
  Open --> HalfOpen: cooldown timer expires
  HalfOpen --> Closed: trial request succeeds
  HalfOpen --> Open: trial request fails
  Closed: Closed (normal traffic)
  Open: Open (fail fast, use fallback)
  HalfOpen: Half-open (allow a probe)

    In AWS, the exam favors AWS Step Functions for this. Step Functions is a managed workflow service where you define a state machine in JSON (Amazon States Language) made of states such as Task, Choice, Retry, Catch, and Parallel. The circuit state has to live outside the state machine, because each execution is independent, so a common design stores it in Amazon DynamoDB: a row per dependency with fields like state, failureCount, and openedAt.

    A workflow looks like this. The first state reads the circuit row from DynamoDB. A Choice state checks it. If the circuit is open and the cooldown has not expired, go directly to the fallback branch. Otherwise, call the model through a Task state using the Step Functions Bedrock integration (arn:aws:states:::bedrock:invokeModel, or the AWS SDK integration for other Bedrock actions). On success, reset the failure count. A Catch on the task records the failure, and if the count reaches the threshold, flips the circuit to open and stamps the time. After the cooldown, one execution is allowed through as the half-open probe.

    Step Functions also gives you the lower-level pieces natively. A Retry block on any Task supports MaxAttempts, IntervalSeconds, BackoffRate, and a jitter strategy, and a Catch block routes specific errors to another state. So simple backoff needs no custom code, and the circuit breaker is what you add on top when failures persist.

    Backend analogy

    If you have used Resilience4j, Polly, or a service mesh with outlier detection, this is the same pattern. The AWS twist is that the breaker state lives in a table because Lambda functions and Step Functions executions share no memory.

    Fallback chains and graceful degradation

    A circuit breaker needs somewhere to send traffic when it opens. A fallback chain is an ordered list of progressively simpler options. The first entry is your preferred model. The next might be a different model in the same family, then a model from another provider, then a smaller or cached option, and the last resort is a static response. Each step trades quality or freshness for availability.

    flowchart TD
  R["Request"] --> P["Primary model (large)"]
  P -->|"success"| OK["Return answer"]
  P -->|"throttled or down"| S["Secondary model (other family or Region)"]
  S -->|"success"| OK
  S -->|"fails"| T["Small fast model with trimmed context"]
  T -->|"success"| OK
  T -->|"fails"| C["Cached answer or canned response"]
  C --> OK2["Return degraded answer + notice"]

    Graceful degradation is the product-side counterpart. Decide in advance what the feature does when it cannot give a full answer. A support assistant could drop to retrieval-only mode and show the top knowledge base articles without generation. A summarizer could return the first paragraph. A long job could be moved to a queue, such as Amazon SQS, and the user told it will arrive by email. The right choice depends on the business cost of a wrong or missing answer, and a good exam answer usually favors a defined degraded behavior over a hard failure.

    Watch out

    A fallback model is not a drop-in replacement. Prompts tuned for one model may produce different formats on another, and tool definitions may behave differently. Test every link of the chain with your evaluation set, and keep per-model prompt variants for the ones that differ. Also confirm the fallback model is allowed by your IAM policy and your data-handling rules before an outage forces the switch.

    Cross-Region inference

    A Region is a separate geographic cluster of AWS data centers with its own capacity. Bedrock quotas are enforced per Region and per model, and a given model may have limited capacity in your Region. Cross-Region inference is a Bedrock feature that lets one request be served from capacity in several Regions, chosen automatically at invocation time, so you get higher effective throughput and resilience against traffic bursts without writing routing logic.

    You use it through an inference profile, a resource that stands in for a model and lists the Regions it may route to. Instead of a base model ID such as anthropic.claude-..., you pass the profile's ID, which carries a prefix. There are two kinds of system-defined profile.

    TypePrefix (example)Routing scopeChoose when
    Geographicus., eu., apac.Only Regions inside that geographyData residency rules limit where inference may run
    Globalglobal.Any supported commercial RegionMaximum throughput and availability, with no geographic constraint

    How it behaves matters for compliance questions. You call your own source Region. Bedrock may serve the request from another Region in the profile, and the request travels over the AWS network rather than the public internet. Data at rest such as logs, knowledge bases, and stored configuration stays in the source Region. Prompts and outputs are processed in the destination Region, though. For that reason, the profile prefix is a data residency decision. If your policy says customer data must not leave the EU, use an eu. profile and verify the Regions it lists. A global. profile would break that rule. There is no extra routing charge for cross-Region inference, and the price is calculated from the Region you call. Geographic profiles are billed at standard pricing. AWS describes global profiles as costing roughly 10% less, which makes them the cost-optimization choice when residency does not matter. Confirm the exact rate for your model on the Bedrock pricing page, because pricing changes. To see where a request actually ran, find the call in CloudTrail in your source Region and read the additionalEventData.inferenceRegion field.

    Two operational points trip people up. First, IAM permissions need to allow the inference profile ARN and the foundation model in every destination Region, so a least-privilege policy that names only your own Region fails with an access error when a request is routed elsewhere. Second, service control policies (SCPs, organization-level guardrails that cap what an account may do) that deny Regions can block a profile whose routing includes a denied Region. A geographic profile needs every destination Region in its list to be allowed. A global profile needs the policy to allow the special aws:RequestedRegion value unspecified. Many models are now offered only through inference profiles, so you will see the error "on-demand throughput isn't supported" when you pass a base model ID, and the fix is to use the profile ID.

    Note

    If your AWS project is restricted to a single assigned Region, as projects in the new AWS sign-up experience are, check before you rely on cross-Region inference. AWS's supported-services page for that experience says multi-Region and cross-Region features are not supported for any service. Expect that a call through a us., eu., or global. profile may be denied there, and treat any test call as an experiment. Browsing profiles in the lab below is read-only.

    Cross-Region model deployment, and provisioned throughput

    Cross-Region inference covers Bedrock's serverless models. For models you host yourself, for example a fine-tuned model on SageMaker AI endpoints, you handle multi-Region yourself. You deploy the same model package to endpoints in two Regions, put a health-aware routing layer in front, such as Amazon Route 53 failover or latency records, or application-level routing, and replicate the model artifacts across Regions with S3 Cross-Region Replication (which is itself unavailable in a single-Region project). The cost is paying for idle capacity in the standby Region, and that cost is the price of the recovery objective you chose.

    Provisioned Throughput is Bedrock's way of reserving dedicated model capacity. You buy model units for a model, with an hourly charge, optionally under a one- or six-month commitment that lowers the rate. In return you get a guaranteed token throughput that does not compete with other customers. You buy it for a base model or a custom model, and inference profiles currently do not support it, so you cannot combine cross-Region routing with Provisioned Throughput. Use it when traffic is high and steady and latency must be stable, or when you serve a custom model that you do not want to deploy on demand. Do not use it for spiky or experimental traffic, because it bills every hour whether or not you send requests. Newer options such as the reserved service tier address similar needs, so read the current documentation when choosing.

    Exam tip

    Match the problem to the tool. Brief spikes in throttling mean retries with backoff. A model that is persistently failing means a Step Functions circuit breaker plus a fallback. A model that is not available in your Region, or throughput that exceeds single-Region quota, means a cross-Region inference profile. Strict data residency means a geographic profile, not a global one. Steady high volume with strict latency means Provisioned Throughput.

    Hands-on lab

    Inspect cross-Region inference profiles

    About 15 minutes · Cost: free to inspect, cents if you make a test call · Needs: Bedrock console access in your selected Region

    1. Open Amazon Bedrock in the console and confirm your Region. In the left navigation, look under the section for inference or deployment settings for Cross-region inference (the item may also be labeled Inference profiles). Open it.
    2. Browse the list of system-defined profiles. For one model, note the profile ID prefix (for example us. or global.), its type, and the list of destination Regions in its details. Compare a geographic profile with a global profile for the same model.
    3. Open the model catalog and find the same model. Check the Regions where it is available in-Region, then compare that list with the profile's destination Regions. This shows how a profile widens availability.
    4. Optional, from CloudShell or your terminal: run aws bedrock list-inference-profiles --region <your-region> and read the inferenceProfileId and models fields in the output.
    5. Optional test call: in the chat playground, pick the model and notice that the console uses the profile ID for models that need it. Send one short prompt and view the API request. In a single-Region project this call may be denied, which is itself a useful finding. If it succeeds, find the call in CloudTrail afterwards and read additionalEventData.inferenceRegion.
    6. Think through a design question and write your answer down: your policy says prompts may be processed only inside the EU. Which profile prefix do you allow, and which IAM and SCP statements do you need so that only that profile is callable?

    Cleanup. Nothing was created. If you tried creating an application inference profile with the CLI for cost tagging, delete it with aws bedrock delete-inference-profile.

    Customizing models: when and how

    Sometimes no off-the-shelf model does the job. The vocabulary of your industry is unusual, the output style must match a house format exactly, or a small model needs to match a big model's quality at a fraction of the cost. Skill 1.2.4 covers what to do then, and the exam expects you to know both the decision and the operations that follow it. Customization creates an asset you own. It has a version, it must be deployed, monitored, and updated, and eventually it must be retired. That lifecycle is where the exam concentrates.

    The decision ladder

    Start at the cheapest rung and move up only when evidence says you must. Each rung costs more in data, money, and operational burden.

    RungWhat changesUse whenCost and effort
    1. Prompt engineeringInstructions, examples, output formatThe model can do the task if told clearlyMinutes, near zero
    2. RAGContext retrieved at request timeThe model lacks your private or fast-changing factsDays; vector store to run (Chapters 4 and 5)
    3. Fine-tuningModel weights, from labeled examplesYou need consistent style, format, or task behavior that prompts cannot reachWeeks; hundreds to thousands of curated examples
    4. Continued pre-trainingModel weights, from large unlabeled domain textThe domain language is far from the model's training dataLarge corpus, higher cost
    5. DistillationA small student model learns from a large teacher's outputsYou want near-teacher quality at lower latency and priceNeeds a teacher model and prompts; moderate

    The most useful rule for the exam is the difference between knowledge and behavior. Fine-tuning is good at teaching behavior: tone, format, a classification scheme, how to call your tools. It is a poor and expensive way to inject facts, because facts change, the model can still hallucinate them, and you cannot easily trace an answer to a source. For facts that change or must be cited, use RAG. For behavior that prompts cannot control, fine-tune. Many production systems combine both: a lightly fine-tuned model that follows the house format, fed with retrieved context.

    Exam tip

    "Up-to-date company knowledge" or "must cite sources" points to RAG. "Consistent output structure or brand voice across thousands of calls, with labeled examples available" points to fine-tuning. "Large amounts of unlabeled domain text, such as medical or legal corpora" points to continued pre-training. "Reduce cost and latency while keeping quality" points to distillation or a smaller model. "Fastest and cheapest first step" is always prompt engineering.

    Customization inside Amazon Bedrock

    Bedrock lets you customize supported models without managing training infrastructure. You supply data in Amazon S3 as JSON Lines files, where each line is one training example. For fine-tuning, a line holds a prompt and the desired completion (or a conversation for chat-style models). For continued pre-training, the data is raw text with no labels. You create a model customization job in the console, SDK, or CLI, choose the base model, point at the S3 data and an output location, set hyperparameters such as epochs, batch size, and learning rate, and provide an IAM service role that lets Bedrock read your data and write the result. The result is a custom model that appears in your account. Bedrock also offers model distillation, where it generates training data from a large teacher model's responses and uses it to fine-tune a smaller student model.

    Only some models support customization, and the supported set differs by Region and changes over time, so check the current documentation before designing around a specific one. Customization data stays in your account. The customized model is private to you and is encrypted with a KMS key if you specify one. To invoke a custom model you have two options. You can purchase Provisioned Throughput for it, which gives dedicated capacity with guaranteed throughput but bills by the hour whether or not you send traffic. Or, for supported models, you can create a custom model deployment for on-demand inference. You then call the deployment's ARN and pay only for the tokens you use, with no provisioned compute to manage. On-demand deployment is the cheaper starting point for a small or spiky workload. Check the current documentation to see which options your chosen model supports. The console playgrounds do not support custom models, so you test them through API calls.

    Bedrock also supports Custom Model Import. If you trained or fine-tuned a model elsewhere, for example an open-weight Llama or Mistral variant tuned on SageMaker or on your own GPUs, you can import the weights from S3 and invoke the model through Bedrock APIs. This brings serverless-style invocation to a model you built, though only for supported architectures.

    Hosting and customizing on Amazon SageMaker AI

    Amazon SageMaker AI is AWS's general machine learning platform. Where Bedrock gives you a managed catalog behind an API, SageMaker gives you control over the model, container, instance type, and scaling. You choose it when you need a model Bedrock does not offer, an open-weight model with full control of training, a custom inference container, or specific GPU hardware. SageMaker JumpStart is a hub of pretrained and open-weight models that you can deploy or fine-tune in a few steps. Training runs as a training job on instances you choose. Hosting runs as a real-time endpoint, a fleet of instances behind an HTTPS URL that you call with the SageMaker runtime API, with autoscaling and monitoring built in.

    LLM hosting differs from classical ML hosting in ways the exam highlights. The model weights are large and must fit in GPU memory, so instance choice follows memory first. Loading takes minutes, so scale-out is slow. Throughput depends on tokens and batching rather than request count. Purpose-built inference containers, such as the Large Model Inference (LMI) containers with engines like vLLM, handle tensor parallelism across GPUs and continuous batching. You will return to deployment trade-offs in Domain 2.

    Exam tip

    Choose Bedrock when a supported model and managed customization are enough and you want no infrastructure. Choose SageMaker AI when you need an unsupported or open-weight model, full control of the training recipe, custom containers, or a specific hardware profile. A hybrid is common: SageMaker for the special model, Bedrock for everything else, with the facade from the previous section routing between them.

    Parameter-efficient fine-tuning: LoRA and adapters

    Full fine-tuning updates every weight in a model that may have tens or hundreds of billions of them. It needs large GPU clusters, and each variant is a complete copy of the model, hundreds of gigabytes each. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) avoids that by freezing the base model and training only a small number of added parameters.

    LoRA (low-rank adaptation) works from the observation that the weight change needed for a new task has low intrinsic rank. For a large weight matrix, LoRA trains two small matrices whose product approximates the update. The size of those matrices is set by the rank, a hyperparameter, and the trained pair is typically well under one percent of the base model's parameter count. Adapters are a related technique that inserts small trainable layers into the network while the original weights stay frozen. QLoRA combines LoRA with a quantized (lower-precision) base model to cut memory further and make fine-tuning fit on smaller GPUs.

    The operational consequences matter more than the mathematics for this exam.

    • Cheaper training. Fewer trainable parameters means less GPU memory, fewer GPU hours, and smaller checkpoints.
    • Tiny artifacts. An adapter is megabytes to a few hundred megabytes, so storing, versioning, and moving it is cheap.
    • One base, many adapters. You can load a single base model on a GPU and attach different adapters per tenant, language, or domain, switching per request. Serving ten domain variants no longer costs ten endpoints. On SageMaker AI, inference components let you deploy a base model component and then adapter components that share it.
    • Lower forgetting risk. Because the base weights are untouched, the model keeps its general abilities, and you can roll back by detaching the adapter.

    Backend analogy

    A LoRA adapter is like a patch or plugin on top of a shared base image. You ship the small diff and keep one large runtime, instead of baking a separate large image for every customer.

    Versioning and approving models with SageMaker Model Registry

    Once models are being produced regularly, "which model is in production" becomes a real question. SageMaker Model Registry is the catalog that answers it. It organizes models into model package groups, one group per logical model such as "support-assistant-lora". Each training run that you want to keep is registered as a new numbered model package version in that group. A version records the model artifacts location in S3, the inference container image, the metrics from evaluation, the training data lineage, and optional documentation such as a model card.

    Each version has an approval status: pending manual approval, approved, or rejected. This status is the control point for the whole lifecycle. A reviewer, who might be a data scientist, a compliance owner, or an automated gate, reads the metrics and sets the status. Changing the status emits an event on Amazon EventBridge, and a rule on that event can start a deployment pipeline. In that design nobody deploys by hand. Approval is the deployment trigger, and the registry is the audit trail of what was approved, by whom, and when.

    For LLM adapters, you register the adapter artifact as the versioned unit and keep the base model reference in the metadata. Roll-forward and rollback then mean choosing a different approved version of a small file.

    Exam tip

    "Track versions of customized models, require approval before production, and trigger deployment on approval" maps to SageMaker Model Registry with approval status changes feeding EventBridge and a pipeline. S3 object versioning alone gives you file history but not approval workflow, metrics lineage, or deployment hooks, which makes it the common distractor.

    Automated deployment pipelines and safe rollout

    A repeatable path from new training data to a serving model has four stages, and each is a managed AWS service or feature you should be able to name.

    1. Build the model. SageMaker Pipelines is a workflow service for ML steps: process data, train, evaluate, and conditionally register. A condition step compares the evaluation metrics to a threshold, and only a model that beats the current one is registered.
    2. Gate and trigger. Approval in Model Registry, as above, generates an EventBridge event.
    3. Deploy. AWS CodePipeline orchestrates the release stages, and AWS CodeBuild runs the build, test, and deployment commands. The pipeline updates a SageMaker endpoint, or for Bedrock-based customization updates the model ID in AppConfig.
    4. Verify and roll back. The deployment watches alarms and reverts automatically on failure.
    flowchart LR
  D["New training data"] --> SP["SageMaker Pipelines: train + evaluate"]
  SP --> REG["Model Registry: new version, pending approval"]
  REG -->|"approved"| EB["EventBridge event"]
  EB --> CP["CodePipeline + CodeBuild"]
  CP --> EP["Endpoint update with traffic shifting"]
  EP -->|"alarm fires"| RB["Automatic rollback to previous version"]

    The rollout step deserves the most attention. SageMaker endpoint deployment guardrails support blue/green deployment. The platform stands up a new fleet (green) next to the live one (blue), then shifts traffic using one of three modes. All at once moves everything in one step. Canary sends a small slice of traffic first, waits, and then moves the rest. Linear moves traffic in equal steps. During a configurable baking period, SageMaker watches the CloudWatch alarms you specify, and if one fires, for instance on a rise in invocation errors or model latency, it shifts traffic back to the blue fleet automatically. Because the old fleet stays up until the new one passes, rollback is fast and the endpoint never goes down during an update.

    StrategyHow it worksBest forCost during rollout
    All at once (blue/green)Full switch after the green fleet is healthyLow-risk changes, fast cutoverRoughly double capacity briefly
    CanarySmall percentage first, then the restRisky changes where early signal mattersDouble capacity briefly, small exposure
    LinearEqual increments on a scheduleLarge fleets that need gradual warm-upCapacity grows with traffic share
    Shadow variantNew model gets copies of live traffic, responses are discardedCompare outputs and latency with zero user riskCost of running both
    Weighted production variantsSeveral models on one endpoint with traffic weightsLong-running A/B testsBoth variants running

    For models served through Bedrock, the same ideas apply with different tools. A custom model or a new base model version becomes just another model ID, so you roll it out gradually with AppConfig, using a canary or linear strategy and alarm monitors, exactly as in the previous section.

    Watch out

    Generic alarms such as 5xx rate or latency catch crashes and slowdowns but not quality regressions. A new model can respond quickly with confident nonsense. Pair the rollout gates with a quality check, such as a synthetic evaluation set scored automatically, and with business signals like thumbs-down rate. Task 5.1 builds on this idea.

    Model lifecycle: retiring and replacing

    Models do not last forever. Providers release newer generations and then retire older ones, and you need a plan for both the managed models you call and the custom models you built.

    Bedrock publishes a lifecycle for each foundation model. A model begins as active. When a successor is available it moves to legacy, which still works but signals that an end-of-life date is coming. After that date the model is end of life and calls fail. AWS announces dates ahead of time, and the status is visible in the console and in the modelLifecycle field returned by ListFoundationModels and GetFoundationModel. Treat that field as a monitoring input. A scheduled job can list the models your configuration references, flag any that are legacy, and open a ticket well before the cutoff.

    The retirement plan follows from everything earlier in this chapter. Because the model ID lives in AppConfig, the cutover is a deployment, not a release. Because you have an evaluation set, you can score the replacement before you touch production. Because you can canary the rollout, a regression affects a small share of traffic. Custom models need extra care, since a fine-tune is built on a specific base model version. When that base is retired, the customized model goes with it, so you must retrain on the successor base. Keep training data, hyperparameters, and evaluation sets versioned and reproducible so that retraining is a pipeline run and not a research project.

    For models you host on SageMaker, retirement is your decision. Mark the old Model Registry version as rejected or otherwise out of service, update the endpoint to the replacement, and delete the unused endpoint, endpoint configuration, and model resources. Idle endpoints are billed per instance hour, so leaving an old fleet running is a classic cost leak.

    Chapter recap

    • Select models by modality, context window, output limit, latency, cost, features, language, Region availability, and licensing, then confirm on your own evaluation data. Public benchmarks only narrow the list.
    • Bedrock Model Evaluation compares models using automatic metrics, LLM-as-a-judge, or human reviewers. It is the low-code way to run a bake-off.
