    • Always set maxTokens. Use low temperature for deterministic tasks, and check stopReason for truncation. Provider-specific parameters such as top-k go in additionalModelRequestFields.
    • The Converse API gives one request shape across models. Keep the model ID and parameters in AWS AppConfig for validated, gradual, alarm-protected changes. Parameter Store is simpler but has no rollout or rollback.
    • Hide models behind a facade of API Gateway and Lambda so clients never depend on a provider. Re-evaluate prompts whenever you switch models.
    • Retry throttling, timeout, and server errors with exponential backoff and jitter. Do not retry validation or access errors. Add a Step Functions circuit breaker with DynamoDB state when failures persist, and a fallback chain ending in a degraded but defined response.
    • Cross-Region inference profiles raise throughput and availability. Geographic profiles keep processing inside a geography, and global profiles do not. Provisioned Throughput reserves capacity for steady high volume.
    • Climb the customization ladder only as needed: prompting, RAG, fine-tuning, continued pre-training, distillation. Fine-tune for behavior, use RAG for facts.
    • LoRA and adapters train a small add-on to a frozen base, which cuts training cost and lets one hosted base serve many domains.
    • SageMaker Model Registry versions models and gates them with approval status. EventBridge, CodePipeline, and deployment guardrails automate canary or linear rollout with automatic rollback. Watch Bedrock lifecycle status and plan base-model replacement, including retraining custom models.

    Knowledge check

    1. A retail company wants to classify millions of short support tickets per day into twelve categories at the lowest possible cost. Accuracy requirements are moderate, and answers are one word. Which approach is best?

      Answer

      Use a small, low-cost model from the family, with low temperature and a small maxTokens, and validate it against a labeled sample. A large reasoning model adds cost and latency with no need for deep reasoning. Fine-tuning or Provisioned Throughput is premature until you show that prompting a small model falls short.

    2. A team hard-coded a Bedrock model ID in their Lambda function. Leadership wants to move 10 percent of traffic to a newer model, watch error rates, and automatically revert if latency alarms fire. What should the team do?

      Answer

      Store the model ID in an AWS AppConfig configuration profile, read it in Lambda through the AppConfig Data API or Lambda extension, and deploy changes with a canary or linear strategy with CloudWatch alarms attached as monitors. Parameter Store changes apply instantly with no rollout or rollback. Lambda environment variables require a function update and cannot shift traffic gradually.

    3. An application receives intermittent ThrottlingException responses from Bedrock during the day, although average usage is well below the account's quota. Developers did not set maxTokens. What are the best two actions?

      Answer

      Set maxTokens to a value matched to the real response size, since Bedrock reserves token quota using that value, and configure SDK retries with exponential backoff and jitter (adaptive mode). Buying Provisioned Throughput or increasing quota would treat a symptom that a parameter fix removes, and retrying ValidationException or access errors would not help.

    4. A European bank must ensure that prompts are processed only inside EU Regions, but wants higher throughput than a single Region provides. Which design meets both requirements?

      Answer

      Use a geographic cross-Region inference profile for the EU (an eu. prefixed profile), and write IAM and SCP rules that allow only that profile and EU Regions. A global. profile may route outside the EU. A single-Region deployment meets residency but not the throughput goal.

    5. The primary FM in a Step Functions workflow has been failing for several minutes. Every execution spends 30 seconds retrying before it errors out, and users are complaining. The team wants to fail fast and send requests to a cheaper fallback model until the primary recovers. What pattern should they add?

      Answer

      A circuit breaker. Store the breaker state (closed, open, half-open), failure count, and open time in DynamoDB. A Choice state at the start of the workflow routes to the fallback branch while the circuit is open, and lets one probe request through after a cooldown. More retries would make latency worse, and a static timeout alone does not stop the flow of traffic to a failing dependency.

    6. A company fine-tunes a Llama model with LoRA for twelve business units, each with its own adapter, and wants to avoid paying for twelve GPU endpoints. They also need to require sign-off before any adapter reaches production, and to roll back quickly. Which combination fits?

      Answer

      Host one base model on a SageMaker AI endpoint with the adapters attached as inference components so they share the base model's GPU memory. Register each adapter as a version in SageMaker Model Registry with approval status controlling promotion, and use deployment guardrails or a prior approved version for rollback. Separate full fine-tuned copies would multiply cost, and storing adapters only as S3 objects would provide no approval workflow.

    Project workshop 1 · Build after Chapters 1 and 2

    Project 1: A resilient model gateway

    Chapters 1 and 2 were about choosing a design and then choosing, configuring, and protecting a model. This workshop turns both into one small service: a gateway that every application calls instead of calling Bedrock directly. You will practice requirements, a proof of concept, model comparison, config-driven switching, and failure handling in one codebase.

    Exam skills practiced

    • 1.1.1 to 1.1.3 architecture, proof of concept, standardized components
    • 1.2.1 choosing a model with evidence
    • 1.2.2 switching models without code changes
    • 1.2.3 resilience: retries, fallback, circuit breaker

    Problem statement

    The scenario

    A company has three internal tools that use generative AI: a ticket summarizer, an email drafter, and an FAQ bot. Each team calls Bedrock directly with a model ID hard-coded in its own repository. Three problems keep coming back.

    • On busy Monday mornings one model is throttled, and the ticket summarizer returns errors to agents until the spike passes.
    • A cheaper model was released last month. Moving to it needs a code change, review, and deployment in three repositories.
    • Finance sees one Bedrock bill and cannot tell which tool, or which feature, caused it.

    Build one internal HTTP service that all three tools call. Tools say what they need ("summarize", "draft", "answer"), and the gateway decides which model serves it, retries or falls back when something fails, and records usage per tool.

    The exam describes this scenario in many forms: "the company wants to change models without redeploying", "the application must keep working when a model is unavailable", "teams need consistent controls". The reference answers use API Gateway, Lambda, and AWS AppConfig. You will build the same pattern with FastAPI on a container, because that is the stack you already know. The architecture is identical, so what you learn transfers directly to an exam answer written in terms of Lambda.

    Solution architecture

    flowchart LR
  A["Client apps (summarizer, drafter, FAQ bot)"] --> B["Application Load Balancer"]
  B --> C["FastAPI gateway on ECS Fargate"]
  C --> D["AWS AppConfig: routing profile"]
  C --> E["Circuit breaker state in DynamoDB"]
  C --> F["Bedrock Runtime: Converse API"]
  F --> G["Primary model"]
  F --> H["Fallback model"]
  C --> I["CloudWatch: logs and metrics"]
  C --> J["Application inference profiles for cost tags"]
  J --> F

    A request carries a task name and the messages. The gateway reads the routing profile from AppConfig, which maps each task to an ordered list of models plus inference settings. It checks the circuit breaker for the first model, calls converse, and on a retryable failure moves down the list. Every call emits a structured log line and custom metrics for input tokens, output tokens, latency, and which model finally served the request. Because calls go through application inference profiles, the Bedrock bill itself is also split by tool.

    A second, offline path covers model choice. It is a script rather than a service.

    flowchart LR
  A["Golden dataset in S3 (50 to 100 cases)"] --> B["Eval runner script"]
  B --> C["Candidate model 1"]
  B --> D["Candidate model 2"]
  B --> E["Candidate model 3"]
  C --> F["Judge model scores answers"]
  D --> F
  E --> F
  F --> G["Report: quality, p50 and p95 latency, cost per request"]
  G --> H["Update AppConfig routing profile"]

    The loop closes at the last step. The evidence from the evaluation decides what you write into the routing profile, and a deployment of that profile is the only thing needed to change which model serves a task. That is skill 1.2.2 in practice.

    Technology and services

    PieceWhat you useWhy this choiceRead first
    API layerFastAPI, container on ECS Fargate behind an ALBYour usual stack. A Lambda function behind API Gateway is the exam's version of the same box.Deployment strategies
    Model callsBoto3 bedrock-runtime, converse and converse_streamOne request shape for every model, so switching needs no code change.Switching models
    Routing configAWS AppConfig with a JSON profile and a JSON Schema validatorVersioned, validated, gradually rolled out, with automatic rollback on alarm.Switching models
    ResilienceBoto3 retry config, fallback list, circuit breaker with state in DynamoDBShared breaker state keeps several container copies consistent.Resilience
    Cost attributionApplication inference profiles with tags, plus requestMetadataSplits spend by tool and feature in Cost Explorer and logs.Registry and cost
    ObservabilityCloudWatch Logs, custom metrics, one dashboard and two alarmsMeasures latency, error rate, fallback rate, and token use.Proof of concept
    InfrastructureTerraform or CDK in the project's infra/ folderRepeatable, and a practice run for the standardized-components idea.Standard components

    Build path

    Suggested folder name: genai-model-gateway, laid out as in the repository's project template (src/, tests/, infra/, Dockerfile). Work in this order. Each step leaves a working service.

    1. Write the requirements and design noteOne page with measurable targets: p95 latency, maximum cost per request, availability goal, and the three tasks the gateway serves. Pick the integration pattern and say why. This is skill 1.1.1 and costs nothing.
    2. Direct proof of conceptA POST /v1/generate endpoint that takes task and messages and calls one hard-coded model through converse. Log token usage and latency on every call. Add maxTokens as a required setting.
    3. Golden dataset and model comparisonCreate 50 or more realistic cases per task. Write the eval runner. Run three candidate models and produce a table of quality score, p50 and p95 latency, and cost per request. Keep the table, because the exam asks you to justify a choice with exactly this kind of data.
    4. Config-driven routingMove the task-to-model mapping and inference settings into an AppConfig profile with a schema validator. Fetch it with the AppConfig data client and cache it for a short time. Prove it works by changing the primary model for one task and watching new requests use it with no redeployment.
    5. Failure handlingAdd retries with backoff for throttling, a timeout, an ordered fallback list, and a circuit breaker per model with state in DynamoDB. Write tests that fake a throttled model and assert the fallback serves the request. Return a clearly marked degraded response when every model fails.
    6. Cost attribution and dashboardCreate one application inference profile per tool, tag it, and route calls through it. Emit metrics for tokens, fallback rate, and breaker state. Build a dashboard and an alarm on error rate.
    7. Container and infrastructure codeWrite the Dockerfile and the Terraform or CDK for the service, its task role (invoke access to approved models only), the AppConfig application, the DynamoDB table, and the alarms. Deploy, send traffic, and confirm the dashboard fills.
    8. Well-Architected reviewCreate a workload in the Well-Architected Tool with the Generative AI Lens, answer the questions honestly, and fix the two worst findings. Save a milestone before and after.

    Definition of done

    • Changing a task's model is an AppConfig deployment, with no container rebuild and no code change.
    • With the primary model blocked by a deny policy or a test double, requests are still served by the fallback and the fallback metric rises.
    • After a configured number of consecutive failures the breaker opens, the failing model stops receiving traffic, and it is tried again after the cool-down.
    • You can answer "which tool cost the most last week, and on which model" from the console without reading code.
    • The evaluation table, not opinion, explains why each task uses the model it uses.

    Cost, Region, and account notes

    Watch out

    Your project is limited to one assigned Region, and multi-Region and cross-Region features may be unavailable. Build the fallback list from different models in that Region, which already teaches the pattern. Treat a cross-Region inference profile as an optional experiment, and first check whether your project's managed policies allow it. A task role that can invoke every model also defeats the purpose, so restrict it to the models in your routing profile.

    Expect cents in model charges if the golden dataset is small and you cap output tokens. The ALB and Fargate task are the costs that continue while idle, so stop the service when you are not testing. If ECS or load balancers are not on the supported-services list for your plan, a container on Lambda with a function URL runs the same FastAPI app with an adapter such as Mangum, and the rest of the design is unchanged.

    Stretch ideas

    Add a Step Functions version of the circuit breaker for batch jobs, since that is the form the exam names. Add response caching keyed on the prompt, and measure the saving. Add a Bedrock guardrail to every call so safety is enforced in one place. Caching leads into the cost topics of Domain 4 and the guardrail into the safety topics of Domain 3, so you can come back to both after you finish this domain.

    Chapter 3 · Task 1.3

    Data validation and processing pipelines for FM consumption

    A foundation model can only work with what you hand it. This chapter covers the stages between raw company data and a well-formed model request: check that the data is fit to use, convert every modality into something a model can read, clean it, and format it into the exact shape the API expects.

    Exam skills covered

    • 1.3.1 Build validation workflows so data meets quality standards before a model sees it (AWS Glue Data Quality, SageMaker Data Wrangler, Lambda validators, CloudWatch metrics)
    • 1.3.2 Process text, image, audio, and tabular data with specialized steps (Bedrock multimodal models, SageMaker Processing, Amazon Transcribe, multimodal pipeline design)
    • 1.3.3 Format input for inference to match model-specific requirements (Bedrock JSON requests, SageMaker endpoint payloads, conversation formatting)
    • 1.3.4 Improve input quality so responses are better and more consistent (Bedrock to reformat text, Comprehend to extract entities, Lambda to normalize)

    The pipeline at a glance

    You already know that retrieval and prompting quality depend on the text you feed in. What is new on AWS is that each step of preparing that text has a managed service with its own name, its own limits, and its own place in exam questions. A document that arrives as a scanned PDF needs Amazon Textract. A recorded support call needs Amazon Transcribe. A table of orders needs Glue, Athena, or SageMaker Processing. Plain text can go straight to Amazon Comprehend for entity and PII detection. Whatever the source, the path to the model has the same four stages.

    flowchart LR
  A["Raw sources: S3, databases, uploads"] --> B["Validate (1.3.1)"]
  B -->|pass| C["Convert by modality (1.3.2)"]
  B -->|fail| Q["Quarantine and alert"]
  C --> D["Clean and enrich (1.3.4)"]
  D --> E["Format the request (1.3.3)"]
  E --> F["Bedrock or SageMaker endpoint"]

    The diagram shows the order in which things happen, which is different from the order of the exam skills. Validation comes first because it is cheapest to reject bad data before you spend money converting or embedding it. Formatting comes last because it depends on the model you picked. Keep this flow in mind. Most scenario questions in this task describe a symptom (answers are inconsistent, the model ignores half the document, the API returns a validation error) and ask which stage to fix.

    Backend analogy

    Think of this chapter as the boundary layer of a service. Validation is your Pydantic model at the edge, conversion is the parser that turns a multipart upload into domain objects, enrichment is the normalization step before persistence, and formatting is the serializer for the downstream API. The difference is scale (millions of files, not one request) and that "valid" includes fuzzy things such as "this OCR output is readable".

    What quality means for FM input

    In classic machine learning, data quality is mostly about numeric columns: nulls, ranges, distribution drift. For foundation models, the input is mostly unstructured, so quality has a wider meaning. A file can be perfectly valid UTF-8 and still be useless because the PDF was a photo of a whiteboard and the OCR returned noise. These are the dimensions worth checking, with the way each one shows up in a running application.

    DimensionExample failureSymptom in the GenAI appTypical check
    CompletenessEmpty body field, truncated OCR, missing author or date metadataModel answers "I don't have that information" for things you know you ingestedNot-null and minimum-length rules
    DuplicatesSame policy uploaded as three versions or copied across foldersTop-k retrieval returns three copies of one passage and crowds out other evidenceUniqueness rules, content hashing
    Encoding and formatLatin-1 file read as UTF-8, unsupported file type, file over the size limitGarbled characters in answers, or files silently missing from the indexFormat allow-list, size checks, decode test
    FreshnessLast year's price list still in the corpusConfident, outdated answersFreshness rule on a modified-date column
    Sensitive contentCustomer SSNs inside support ticketsPII appears in a response or in a logPII detection with Comprehend or Macie
    Schema conformanceColumn renamed upstream, JSON field changes typePrompt templates render with empty or wrong valuesSchema and type rules
    Size and token fitA 900-page manual passed as one documentContext window errors, truncated input, high costLength checks, token estimates

    Two rows deserve extra attention because they fail quietly. First, an Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base skips unsupported file types and oversized files during ingestion without raising an error, so the only symptom is a document that never gets retrieved. A validation step that checks type and size before ingestion turns that silent loss into a visible rejection. Second, freshness is a data problem that looks like a model problem. When users complain that the assistant "hallucinates" an old policy, the cause is often a stale document that retrieval correctly found.

    Exam tip

    When a scenario describes bad answers and the data is the cause, the correct fix is almost always upstream of the model: validate, deduplicate, or refresh the data. Options that change the model, raise the temperature, or add more prompt text are distractors when the root cause is a bad input.

    AWS Glue Data Quality

    AWS Glue is Amazon's serverless data integration service. It has three parts you will meet repeatedly. The Glue Data Catalog is a central metadata store that describes your datasets (table names, columns, S3 locations). A crawler scans data in S3 or a database and fills the catalog automatically. Glue ETL jobs run Spark or Python code to transform data without servers to manage. Many other AWS services, including Athena, read the same catalog, so a table you register once is queryable from several places.

    AWS Glue Data Quality adds rule-based quality checks on top of that catalog. You describe expectations in DQDL (Data Quality Definition Language), a small declarative language, and Glue evaluates them against a table or a dataset inside an ETL job. It is built on the open-source Deequ library, which Spark developers may recognize.

    Rules = [
    RowCount > 1000,
    IsComplete "doc_id",
    Uniqueness "doc_id" = 1.0,
    Completeness "body" > 0.98,
    ColumnLength "title" between 5 and 200,
    ColumnValues "status" in ["PUBLISHED", "DRAFT"],
    ColumnValues "language" = "en",
    DataFreshness "last_modified" <= 30 days
]

    Each rule is a pass or fail test, and the whole ruleset produces a quality score between 0 and 100 percent. You can write the rules by hand, or ask Glue to recommend rules. In that mode it profiles the data and proposes a starting ruleset that you then tighten. This is a reasonable first step for a corpus you have never seen.

    Where the rules run matters for the exam:

    • On a catalog table. Open the table in the Glue console, use the Data quality tab, create a ruleset, and run it. This suits periodic checks of data at rest.
    • Inside a Glue ETL job. The Evaluate Data Quality transform sits in the middle of a pipeline. It can route rows that fail to one output and clean rows to another, which is exactly the quarantine pattern described later in this chapter. This suits checks that must block bad data from moving forward.
    • On a schedule or trigger. Runs can be scheduled, and results are published as CloudWatch metrics and Amazon EventBridge events, so a failed ruleset can alarm, page someone, or start a remediation workflow.

    Glue Data Quality also offers anomaly detection, which learns the normal statistics of a dataset over time and flags runs that deviate, for example a sudden drop in row count. Use rules for expectations you can state and anomaly detection for the ones you cannot.

    Watch out

    Glue Data Quality is strongest on structured and semi-structured data that has a schema in the catalog. It can tell you that 4 percent of rows have an empty body column. It cannot tell you whether a body that is not empty contains readable prose. For that you need a custom check, covered below.

    Hands-on lab

    Run Glue Data Quality on a CSV in S3

    About 25 minutes · Cost: cents (a crawler run plus one small data quality run) · Needs: an S3 bucket in your selected Region and a small CSV of documents with columns such as doc_id, title, body, last_modified

    1. Create an S3 bucket in your selected Region and upload the CSV. Put some deliberate problems in it: two rows with the same doc_id, one empty body, one very old last_modified.
    2. Open the AWS Glue console, go to Databases, and create a database named genai_quality_lab.
    3. Go to Crawlers and choose Create crawler. Point it at the S3 folder, choose the new database as the target, and let the console create an IAM role for it. Run the crawler, then confirm a table appears under Tables.
    4. Open the table and select the Data quality tab. Choose Recommend rules, pick the IAM role, and start the recommendation run. When it finishes, review the suggested rules.
    5. Choose Create data quality rules, paste the rules you want (use the DQDL example above, adjusted to your column names), and save the ruleset.
    6. Select the ruleset and choose Run. When the run completes, open the result and see which rules failed and the overall score. Your planted duplicates, empty body, and stale row should each show up as a failed rule.

    Cleanup. Delete the data quality ruleset, the table, the database, and the crawler in Glue, then empty and delete the S3 bucket. Remove the IAM role the console created if you no longer need it. AWS Glue appears in the free-tier service list of the new AWS sign-up experience, so this lab should be available on a free plan. If a Glue call is denied anyway, skip the lab and read the DQDL examples instead.

    SageMaker Data Wrangler

    Amazon SageMaker is AWS's platform for building, training, and deploying machine learning models. Because the exam is a GenAI exam, you will mostly meet it as the place where you host custom or fine-tuned models, but it also has data preparation tools. SageMaker Data Wrangler is the visual one. You import data from S3, Athena, or other sources, apply transformations from a library of several hundred built-in operations (drop duplicates, handle missing values, split text, parse dates, encode categories), and look at an automatically generated data quality and insights report that highlights missing values, outliers, duplicate rows, and target leakage.

    Data Wrangler is aimed at an analyst or ML practitioner exploring a dataset interactively. When the flow is ready, you export it as a SageMaker Processing job, a SageMaker Pipelines step, or a notebook, so the same steps run unattended on new data. AWS now presents Data Wrangler's data preparation inside SageMaker Canvas, the no-code interface for analysts, and the older flows can be migrated there. The console entry points have moved more than once, so look under Canvas, and under SageMaker Unified Studio if your project uses it, rather than expecting one fixed location.

    Exam tip

    Match the tool to the situation. Glue Data Quality fits automated, rule-based validation of catalogued data in a pipeline. Data Wrangler fits interactive, visual exploration and preparation with a point-and-click interface and a quality report. A Lambda function fits custom logic that no managed rule can express, such as "the OCR text is mostly dictionary words". CloudWatch is where the results of any of these become metrics and alarms. If a question says "low-code" and "visual", think Data Wrangler. If it says "automatically evaluate rules as part of an ETL job", think Glue Data Quality.

    Custom validation with Lambda

    AWS Lambda runs a function in response to an event, with no server to manage and billing per millisecond of execution. It has a maximum run time of 15 minutes and memory up to 10 GB, which is enough for most per-file checks. Lambda is the natural home for validation logic that does not fit a rule language, and for files that are not tabular.

    The usual trigger is an S3 event. When a file lands in an incoming/ prefix, S3 emits an event that invokes the function (directly, or through Amazon EventBridge when you want more routing options). The function reads the object and runs its checks. Typical checks for GenAI input are:

    • Format and size. Is the extension on the allow-list? Does a PDF open? Is the file under the limit the downstream service accepts?
    • Decodability. Does a text file decode as UTF-8 without replacement characters? A high count of the U+FFFD replacement character is a sign of the wrong encoding.
    • Readability. After OCR, what fraction of tokens are real words? What is the ratio of letters to symbols? Output that is mostly punctuation means the scan was unusable.
    • Language. Is the document in a language the chosen model and embedding model handle well? Comprehend's language detection answers this in one call.
    • Sensitive data. Does the text contain PII that should not be indexed? Comprehend's PII detection can flag it, and Amazon Macie can scan S3 buckets for sensitive data at rest.
    • Size in tokens. Is the document small enough to process whole, or does it need to be split first?
    import json, os, unicodedata
import boto3

s3 = boto3.client("s3")
cw = boto3.client("cloudwatch")

ALLOWED = {".txt", ".md", ".pdf", ".docx", ".html"}
MAX_BYTES = 50 * 1024 * 1024

def handler(event, context):
    rec = event["Records"][0]["s3"]
    bucket, key = rec["bucket"]["name"], rec["object"]["key"]
    head = s3.head_object(Bucket=bucket, Key=key)

    problems = []
    ext = os.path.splitext(key)[1].lower()
    if ext not in ALLOWED:
        problems.append(f"unsupported_type:{ext}")
    if head["ContentLength"] > MAX_BYTES:
        problems.append("too_large")
    if head["ContentLength"] == 0:
        problems.append("empty")

    if ext in {".txt", ".md", ".html"} and not problems:
        text = s3.get_object(Bucket=bucket, Key=key)["Body"].read().decode("utf-8", errors="replace")
        if text.count("�") / max(len(text), 1) > 0.01:
            problems.append("bad_encoding")
        if len(unicodedata.normalize("NFKC", text).strip()) < 50:
            problems.append("too_short")

    dest_prefix = "quarantine/" if problems else "validated/"
    new_key = dest_prefix + key.split("/", 1)[-1]
    s3.copy_object(
        Bucket=bucket,
        Key=new_key,
        CopySource={"Bucket": bucket, "Key": key},
        Metadata={"validation": json.dumps(problems or ["ok"])},
        MetadataDirective="REPLACE",
    )
    s3.delete_object(Bucket=bucket, Key=key)

    cw.put_metric_data(
        Namespace="GenAI/Ingestion",
        MetricData=[{"MetricName": "Rejected" if problems else "Accepted", "Value": 1, "Unit": "Count"}],
    )
    return {"key": new_key, "problems": problems}

    This sketch is deliberately simple. A production version would add structured logging, handle the PDF cases with a real parser, and make the copy-then-delete step idempotent so that a retried invocation does not fail on a missing source object.

    Metrics, alarms, and the quarantine pattern

    A validator that only logs failures will be ignored. The pipeline needs to make quality visible, and the AWS way to do that is Amazon CloudWatch, the monitoring service for metrics, logs, and alarms. A metric is a named time series, such as the number of rejected documents per hour. An alarm watches a metric and takes an action (usually notifying an Amazon SNS topic) when it crosses a threshold.

    There are two common ways to publish a custom metric from Lambda. You can call PutMetricData, as the sketch above does. Or you can print a specially formatted JSON log line in Embedded Metric Format (EMF), and CloudWatch extracts the metrics from the logs for you. EMF avoids an extra API call and keeps the metric tied to the log line that explains it, which is why it is the recommended approach for high-volume Lambda functions. Glue Data Quality publishes its own metrics without any code.

    Useful ingestion metrics include the accepted and rejected counts, the rejection rate, the data quality score from the latest Glue run, the age of the oldest unprocessed file, and the share of documents containing PII. Alarm on the rate and not only the count, because ten rejections in a batch of ten is a different situation from ten in a batch of ten thousand.

    The quarantine pattern is what to do with failures. Do not delete them and do not pass them through. Move the failed object to a separate location (an S3 prefix or bucket), tag it with the reason, and let the rest of the batch continue. Someone can then inspect the quarantine, fix the source, and resubmit. It is the data equivalent of a dead-letter queue. In fact, for asynchronous Lambda invocations you can also configure an Amazon SQS dead-letter queue or an on-failure destination, which catches events whose processing failed with an error (as opposed to files that were processed and judged invalid). Use both: quarantine for bad data, dead-letter queue for failed processing.

    flowchart LR
  U["Upload to S3 incoming/"] --> L["Lambda validator"]
  L -->|valid| V["S3 validated/"]
  L -->|invalid| Q["S3 quarantine/ with reason tag"]
  L -->|error after retries| D["SQS dead-letter queue"]
  L --> M["CloudWatch metrics"]
  M --> A["Alarm on rejection rate"]
  A --> N["SNS topic to on-call"]
  V --> P["Next stage: conversion and enrichment"]

    Note

    Large pipelines usually put the validator inside an AWS Step Functions state machine instead of triggering Lambda directly from S3. Step Functions is AWS's workflow service, which you can compare to a durable workflow engine such as Temporal or Airflow. It gives you retries, branching on the validation result, and an execution history you can inspect. Later in this chapter the multimodal pipeline uses it for that reason.

    Exam tip

    A question that asks how to "monitor data quality over time" or "alert when quality drops" is testing the CloudWatch step: custom metrics plus an alarm. A question that asks what to do with records that fail validation is testing quarantine or dead-letter handling. Deleting bad records, or ingesting them anyway and filtering at query time, are the usual wrong answers.

    Processing text, documents, images, audio, and tables

    Once data passes validation, it has to be converted into something a model can consume. Language models read text, so most of this work is turning other things into text, or into structured fields that you later turn into text. Multimodal models blur that rule for images and sometimes audio and video, but even then a dedicated service is often cheaper, more accurate, or more predictable. Choosing between a dedicated service and a multimodal model is a recurring exam theme.

    InputPrimary AWS toolsWhat you get out
    Plain text, HTML, MarkdownLambda, Amazon ComprehendCleaned text, entities, key phrases, language, PII spans, sentiment
    Scanned documents, PDFs, forms, invoicesAmazon TextractRaw text, key-value pairs from forms, tables, layout, query answers
    Images and photosAmazon Rekognition, Bedrock multimodal modelsLabels, text in image, moderation flags, or a free-form description and reasoning
    Audio and call recordingsAmazon TranscribeTimestamped transcript, speaker labels, optional PII redaction
    Tables, logs, CSV, ParquetAWS Glue, Amazon Athena, SageMaker ProcessingCleaned, joined, aggregated data that you serialize for the model

    Documents with Amazon Textract

    Amazon Textract is a managed document-understanding service. It goes beyond ordinary OCR (optical character recognition, reading characters from an image). Its operations are worth knowing by name:

    • DetectDocumentText returns raw lines and words, the cheapest option when you only need the text.
    • AnalyzeDocument adds feature types. FORMS returns key-value pairs such as "Invoice Number: 4821". TABLES returns cells with row and column positions, so you can rebuild a table. QUERIES lets you ask natural-language questions such as "What is the total amount due?" and returns just the answer with a confidence score. LAYOUT identifies titles, headers, paragraphs, and lists, which helps with chunking later. SIGNATURES detects signature regions.
    • AnalyzeExpense and AnalyzeID are specialized for invoices and receipts, and for identity documents.

    The synchronous APIs handle a single page or image. Multi-page PDFs and TIFFs go through the asynchronous APIs (StartDocumentAnalysis and GetDocumentAnalysis), which read from S3 and can notify an Amazon SNS topic when the job finishes. Every result carries a confidence score per block, which your validation step can use: if the average confidence of a page is low, send it to a human or reject it instead of embedding noise.

    A common pattern is to run Textract with the TABLES and LAYOUT features and convert the result to Markdown, so the table structure survives in text the model can read. Flattened table text, where cells run together in reading order, is one of the most common causes of wrong answers about numbers.

    Text with Amazon Comprehend

    Amazon Comprehend is a managed natural language processing service. For this chapter its useful calls are DetectDominantLanguage, DetectEntities (people, organizations, dates, quantities, and similar), DetectKeyPhrases, DetectSentiment, and DetectPiiEntities. They return structured results with character offsets, which means you can both attach the results as metadata and redact the exact spans. Comprehend is fast and inexpensive per call, it needs no prompt, and it returns the same answer for the same input. That predictability is why exam questions prefer it over an LLM for extraction tasks with a fixed schema. Section 1.3.4 returns to it.

    Images with Rekognition or a multimodal model

    Amazon Rekognition is the dedicated computer-vision service. It detects labels (objects and scenes), text in images, faces, and unsafe content, and returns scores in a fixed schema. A multimodal foundation model on Bedrock, such as Anthropic Claude or Amazon Nova, accepts an image together with a text instruction and produces a free-form answer. It can read a chart, describe a floor plan, or answer "is the wiring in this photo consistent with the diagram?". The trade-off is the usual one. Rekognition is cheaper per image, deterministic, and good for classification and moderation at scale. A multimodal model is more flexible and can reason, but costs more per call, can hallucinate, and its output needs validation.

    A multimodal model call needs the image in the request. With the Converse API, you add an image content block next to your text block, and the image is passed as raw bytes with a format such as png or jpeg. Section 1.3.3 shows the exact structure. Bedrock also publishes limits for images per request, size per image, and pixel dimensions, and these vary by model, so check the model's documentation before designing around a number.

    Audio with Amazon Transcribe

    Amazon Transcribe is the managed speech-to-text service. In batch mode you start a transcription job against an audio file in S3, and Transcribe writes a JSON transcript with word-level timestamps and confidence. Features that matter for GenAI input:

    • Speaker identification (speaker partitioning, also called diarization) labels who said what, which you need for a call summary that says "the agent offered a refund" instead of "a refund was offered".
    • Channel identification separates the two sides of a call when the recording has one speaker per channel.
    • Custom vocabularies and language models improve accuracy on product names and jargon.
    • Automatic language identification detects the language when it is not known in advance.
    • Content redaction masks or removes PII in the transcript, so sensitive details do not reach the model or the logs.
    • Streaming transcription handles live audio over a WebSocket or HTTP/2 stream, for real-time applications.

    The transcript is raw material. Spoken language has filler words, false starts, and no paragraph breaks, so a typical pipeline sends the transcript through a cleanup step (section 1.3.4) before summarizing or embedding it.

    Tables with Glue, Athena, and SageMaker Processing

    Tabular data is the modality that language models are worst at when you paste it in whole, and the cheapest to process with conventional tools. Three services appear on the exam for this:

    • Amazon Athena runs SQL directly on files in S3 using the Glue Data Catalog for the schema. It is serverless and billed per amount of data scanned. It is the quickest way to filter, join, and aggregate.
    • AWS Glue ETL runs Spark jobs for large transformations and can write output to S3 in a columnar format such as Parquet.
    • SageMaker Processing runs your own script (pandas, scikit-learn, Spark, or a custom container) as a managed one-off job on instances that exist only for the duration of the job. Choose it when the transformation needs custom Python or a library that Glue does not provide.

    The remaining question is how to present a table to a model. Three options cover most cases. Serialize rows as Markdown or CSV text when the table is small and you want the model to read all of it. Convert each row into a short natural-language sentence ("Order 1042 shipped on 3 March to Berlin and weighs 2.4 kg") when you will embed rows for retrieval, because embedding models handle sentences better than comma-separated values. And do not put a large table into the prompt at all. Instead, let the model write a SQL query (or call a tool that runs one) against Athena, so the database does the arithmetic. LLMs make arithmetic and aggregation mistakes on long tables, and sending 50,000 rows costs far more than sending the answer.

    Exam tip

    The exam often hides the service choice inside a modality. Memorize the pairs: scanned forms and tables go to Textract, call recordings go to Transcribe, entities, PII, and sentiment in text go to Comprehend, object and moderation labels in images go to Rekognition, reasoning over an image goes to a Bedrock multimodal model, and large tables go to Athena, Glue, or SageMaker Processing. Common distractors are Comprehend used for OCR, Rekognition used to extract table structure from a scanned invoice, and Transcribe used on a PDF.

    Note

    Amazon Bedrock Data Automation is a managed Bedrock capability that extracts structured output from documents, images, audio, and video using blueprints you define. It appears in the exam guide under task 2.5, so Chapter 3 only needs you to know that it exists as a managed alternative to stitching Textract, Transcribe, and a model together yourself.

    Designing a multimodal pipeline

    Real corpora are mixed. A customer-support knowledge base might hold product manuals as PDFs, scanned warranty forms, recorded training calls, and exported spreadsheets. The standard architecture is event-driven and routes each file by type.

    flowchart TB
  S["S3 raw/ bucket"] -->|object created| EB["EventBridge rule"]
  EB --> SF["Step Functions state machine"]
  SF --> V["Validate (Lambda)"]
  V --> R{"File type?"}
  R -->|pdf or image| TX["Textract (async)"]
  R -->|audio| TR["Transcribe job"]
  R -->|csv or parquet| GL["Glue or Athena transform"]
  R -->|txt or html| TE["Lambda text cleanup"]
  TX --> CL["Clean and enrich (Comprehend, Lambda)"]
  TR --> CL
  GL --> CL
  TE --> CL
  CL --> OUT["S3 curated/ as normalized text and metadata"]
  OUT --> NEXT["Chunking and embedding (Chapters 4 and 5)"]

    The pieces fit together like this. Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus. S3 can send an "object created" event to it, and a rule matches the event and starts the state machine. The Step Functions state machine handles validation, a Choice state that routes by file type, the long-running asynchronous jobs (it can wait for a Textract or Transcribe job to finish, either by polling or by waiting for a callback token), error handling with retries and a catch path to the quarantine, and the final write of the curated output. Each branch ends in the same normalized shape: clean text plus a metadata record (source file, page or timestamp, language, entities, validation status). A uniform output is what lets the same chunking and embedding code handle every modality.

    Cost and throughput deserve a thought early. Textract and Transcribe charge by page and by audio minute, so validation should reject unreadable files before they reach them. Step Functions has a Distributed Map state for processing large numbers of S3 objects in parallel when you need to back-fill an existing archive rather than process files as they arrive.

    Backend analogy

    S3 plus EventBridge plus Step Functions is the AWS version of "object storage webhook, then a workflow engine with a router and per-type workers". If you have used Celery chains or a Temporal workflow with activity retries, the state machine plays the same role, defined in JSON (Amazon States Language) instead of code.

    Hands-on lab

    Extract text, forms, and tables with the Textract console demo

    About 10 minutes · Cost: a few cents at most, covered by the free tier for new usage · Needs: a sample PDF or image of an invoice, a form, or any page with a table, and, in the new AWS sign-up experience, a paid plan (Textract is listed only for paid plans there)

    1. Open the Amazon Textract console in your selected Region. From the landing page, choose Try Amazon Textract (or open the Analyze Document demo).
    2. Upload your sample document. Select the analysis types you want: Forms, Tables, and Queries. Add a query such as "What is the invoice total?".
    3. Review the result tabs: Raw text, Forms, Tables, and Queries. Click a detected item and see the bounding box drawn on the page.
    4. Look at the confidence scores next to each item. Find the lowest-confidence value on your page and decide whether your pipeline should accept it.
    5. Download the JSON output and open it. Find a BlockType of TABLE and follow its CELL relationships. This is the structure your Lambda code would convert to Markdown.

    Cleanup. The demo does not leave resources behind. Delete the downloaded file if it contains real data.

    Hands-on lab

    Transcribe an audio file with speaker labels

    About 15 minutes · Cost: roughly two to three cents per audio minute (use a clip under two minutes) · Needs: a short MP3 or WAV with two speakers, an S3 bucket in your selected Region, and, in the new AWS sign-up experience, a paid plan (Transcribe is listed only for paid plans there)

    1. Create an S3 bucket in your selected Region (or reuse your lab bucket) and upload the audio file.
    2. Open the Amazon Transcribe console and choose Transcription jobs, then Create job.
    3. Name the job. Set the language (or choose Automatic language identification). For Input data, browse to your S3 object. For Output data, choose Amazon Transcribe default bucket or select your own bucket.
    4. Choose Next. Under Audio settings, turn on Speaker partitioning and enter the number of speakers. Optionally enable PII identification and redaction under content removal if your language supports it.
    5. Create the job and wait until the status is Complete. Open the job and read the transcript, then download the JSON output.
    6. Find the speaker_labels section and the per-word confidence values. Note how the transcript has no paragraphs or punctuation structure beyond what Transcribe guessed. This is what the cleanup step has to fix.

    Cleanup. Delete the transcription job from the console, remove the output object (and the Transcribe default bucket contents if you used it), then empty and delete the audio bucket.

    Hands-on lab

    Send an image to a multimodal model in the Bedrock playground

    About 10 minutes · Cost: a few cents at on-demand token pricing · Needs: Bedrock model access in your selected Region and a screenshot, a receipt, or a chart

    1. Open the Amazon Bedrock console in your selected Region. In the left menu, under Test, choose Playgrounds and open the Chat playground.
    2. Choose Select model. Pick a multimodal model such as an Amazon Nova model that accepts images, or an Anthropic Claude model. If the console asks for first-time use details for a provider, complete that form and retry.
    3. Attach your image with the attachment (paperclip) control, then type an instruction: "Extract every line item as JSON with the fields description, quantity, and price. Return only JSON."
    4. Run the prompt. Compare the output with the image. Where did the model make a mistake or skip a value? Try again with a rotated or low-resolution version of the image.
    5. Open the playground's menu for View API request (the wording may differ slightly) and read the JSON. Find the image content block and the format field. You will build the same request in code in the next section.

    Cleanup. There are no resources to delete. The playground keeps nothing you need to remove, but avoid uploading images that contain real personal data.

    Formatting input for model inference

    After the data is clean, you still have to put it in the shape the API wants. This is where many first attempts fail with a ValidationException whose message says "Malformed input request". Bedrock exposes two ways to call a text model, and the difference between them is the single most testable fact in this section.

    Converse versus InvokeModel

    InvokeModel sends a raw request body to a specific model, and the body is whatever that provider defined. Converse is Bedrock's unified API. You describe the conversation in one common structure and Bedrock translates it into each provider's native format. Both come in streaming variants (ConverseStream and InvokeModelWithResponseStream), and both live on the bedrock-runtime client.

    ProviderInvokeModel request bodyParameter names to remember
    Anthropic ClaudeJSON with anthropic_version set to bedrock-2023-05-31, top-level system, and a messages arraymax_tokens is required
    Meta LlamaJSON with a single prompt string that you build with the model's special header tokensmax_gen_len, temperature, top_p
    Amazon Titan TextJSON with inputText and a textGenerationConfig objectmaxTokenCount, stopSequences
    Amazon NovaJSON that mirrors the Converse structure (messages, system, inferenceConfig)maxTokens inside inferenceConfig
    Titan Text Embeddings V2JSON with inputText, and optional dimensions and normalizeReturns an embedding array (Chapter 5)

    Notice what these have in common: nothing. The role names, the system prompt location, the limit parameter, and even whether the conversation is an array or a string all differ. That is the case for Converse, which AWS recommends for new code, and the reason a "switch models without code changes" design (Chapter 2) is practical with Converse and painful with InvokeModel. Use InvokeModel when you need a provider feature Converse does not expose, and for model types Converse does not cover, such as embedding models and image generation models.

    Exam tip

    If a scenario says a team must support several models or providers with one code path, the answer is the Converse API. If it says an application returns "Malformed input request" after the model changed, the likely cause is an InvokeModel body written for a different provider. If it says the team needs to call an embedding model, that is InvokeModel, because Converse is for conversational text generation.

    The Converse request in detail

    A Converse request has a small number of top-level fields. The ones you will use constantly are these:

    • modelId: a model ID, an inference profile ID such as us.amazon.nova-lite-v1:0, or the ARN of a managed prompt.
    • messages: a list of turns, each with a role (user or assistant) and a content list of blocks.
    • system: the system prompt, as a list of text blocks. It is separate from messages.
    • inferenceConfig: maxTokens, temperature, topP, and stopSequences.
    • toolConfig: tool definitions for function calling.
    • guardrailConfig: a guardrail identifier and version, to filter the input and the output.
    • additionalModelRequestFields: a pass-through for provider-specific parameters that Converse does not model, for example top_k on Claude.

    Each message's content is a list of content blocks. The block types you need for this chapter are text, image, and document. Others carry tool calls (toolUse and toolResult), reasoning traces, and cache markers. An image block takes a format (png, jpeg, gif, or webp) and a source, which in the Python SDK is raw bytes. A document block takes a format (one of pdf, csv, doc, docx, xls, xlsx, html, txt, or md), a name, and the bytes. The name may contain only alphanumeric characters, single spaces, hyphens, parentheses, and square brackets, up to 200 characters. Which formats a given model accepts varies, so check the model's supported features.

    import boto3
from botocore.config import Config

bedrock = boto3.client(
    "bedrock-runtime",
    config=Config(retries={"max_attempts": 5, "mode": "adaptive"}),
)

with open("invoice.png", "rb") as f:
    image_bytes = f.read()

response = bedrock.converse(
    modelId="us.amazon.nova-lite-v1:0",
    system=[{"text": "You extract invoice data. Respond with JSON only."}],
    messages=[
        {
            "role": "user",
            "content": [
                {"text": "List every line item with description, quantity, and price."},
                {"image": {"format": "png", "source": {"bytes": image_bytes}}},
            ],
        }
    ],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 800, "temperature": 0.0},
)

print(response["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"])
print(response["stopReason"], response["usage"])

    Read the response as carefully as the request. stopReason tells you why generation ended. end_turn is normal. max_tokens means the output was cut off, which for a JSON extraction task usually means invalid JSON. guardrail_intervened means a guardrail blocked or changed the response. usage reports input and output token counts, which you should log, because it is the ground truth for cost and for sizing prompts.

    The same request through InvokeModel on Claude looks different, and the differences are exactly the ones in the table above.

    import json

body = {
    "anthropic_version": "bedrock-2023-05-31",
    "max_tokens": 800,
    "temperature": 0.0,
    "system": "You extract invoice data. Respond with JSON only.",
    "messages": [
        {"role": "user", "content": [{"type": "text", "text": "List the line items."}]}
    ],
}
resp = bedrock.invoke_model(
    modelId="us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6",
    body=json.dumps(body),
    contentType="application/json",
    accept="application/json",
)
result = json.loads(resp["body"].read())
print(result["content"][0]["text"])

    Watch out

    Always set maxTokens explicitly. If you omit it, the request can reserve the model's maximum output allowance against your quota, and you will see throttling at traffic levels that should be fine. Size it to the output you actually expect. Also, the name of a document block is passed to the model along with the content, so a filename like ignore-previous-instructions.pdf becomes a small injection surface. Use a neutral, generated name such as document-1.

    Token and size limits

    Every model has a context window, the total number of tokens it can consider for input and output combined, and a separate cap on output tokens. Your request fails or is truncated when the input plus the requested output does not fit. A few practical rules follow.

    • Estimate tokens before you send. A rough rule for English is about four characters per token, and code, other languages, and numbers usually cost more. Measure real counts from the usage field in responses and keep a running average per document type.
    • Leave headroom. Budget for the system prompt, the retrieved context, the conversation history, and the answer. A common design fixes the system and answer allowance, and gives retrieval whatever remains.
    • Respect request limits besides tokens. The Converse API enforces maximum counts and sizes for images and documents per request, and the numbers differ by model and are not stated in one place, so I will not quote figures here. Look them up for your chosen model in the Bedrock documentation, and make your validation step enforce them so a request never leaves your application in a shape that will be rejected.
    • Watch for stopReason values that signal a limit was hit, such as max_tokens, and for the context-overflow error. Skill 5.2.1 in Domain 5 of the exam guide covers diagnosing these.

    Conversation formatting for dialog applications

    A model has no memory between calls. A chat application therefore sends the relevant history with every request, and formatting that history correctly is part of this task. With Converse, history is simply the messages list in order.

    • The list alternates user and assistant, and it should start with a user message. Several models reject two consecutive messages with the same role, so merge adjacent turns from the same side into one message with several content blocks.
    • The system prompt does not go in the list. Put it in system.
    • When the model calls a tool, the assistant turn contains a toolUse block, and your next user turn contains the matching toolResult block. The pair must stay together in the history.
    • History grows, and cost and latency grow with it. Trim by keeping the last N turns (a sliding window), by summarizing old turns with a cheap model and keeping the summary, or by retrieving only the past turns relevant to the new question. Store the full history outside the request, commonly in Amazon DynamoDB keyed by session ID, and build each request from it (Chapter 6 covers this pattern).
    def build_messages(history, user_text, max_turns=8):
    """history is a list of {"role": ..., "text": ...} dicts loaded from storage."""
    recent = history[-max_turns * 2:]
    while recent and recent[0]["role"] != "user":
        recent = recent[1:]          # a window must begin with a user turn
    messages = [{"role": t["role"], "content": [{"text": t["text"]}]} for t in recent]
    messages.append({"role": "user", "content": [{"text": user_text}]})
    return messages

    Formatting for SageMaker AI endpoints

    If you host a model yourself on a SageMaker AI endpoint (a managed HTTPS inference service backed by instances you choose), there is no Bedrock layer translating for you. You call the endpoint through the sagemaker-runtime client with invoke_endpoint, and the body format is defined by the container serving the model. Hugging Face text-generation containers and AWS's Large Model Inference containers typically accept a JSON body with an inputs string and a parameters object, and many newer versions also accept a chat-style messages list with max_tokens. A scikit-learn or XGBoost endpoint for tabular data usually takes text/csv. Read the container's documentation, and set ContentType to match.

    import json, boto3

smr = boto3.client("sagemaker-runtime")
payload = {
    "inputs": "Summarize the following ticket in one sentence: ...",
    "parameters": {"max_new_tokens": 128, "temperature": 0.2, "return_full_text": False},
}
resp = smr.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName="my-llm-endpoint",
    ContentType="application/json",
    Body=json.dumps(payload),
)
print(json.loads(resp["Body"].read()))

    A subtlety that bites when you serve open models yourself is the chat template. Instruction-tuned models were trained with specific special tokens that mark system, user, and assistant turns (the Llama 3 header tokens in the table above are an example). If you send the raw prompt string to a plain text-generation endpoint, you must apply that template yourself, typically with the tokenizer's apply_chat_template method. If you send a messages list to a container that supports it, the container applies the template. Sending role-labeled text without the right tokens is a frequent reason a self-hosted model seems less capable than its benchmarks suggest. This is one more reason Converse is convenient on Bedrock: it applies the template for you.

    Backend analogy

    Converse behaves like a provider-agnostic client library (LiteLLM and LangChain's chat model interface fill the same role in Python). InvokeModel behaves like calling each vendor's raw REST endpoint. The exam wants you to know when each is appropriate, even though you would rarely write the raw version by hand when a stable abstraction exists.

    Note

    The Bedrock Prompt Management feature (Chapter 6) lets you store a prompt and call it by ARN through Converse. In that mode you must not also send system, inferenceConfig, or toolConfig, because they live inside the managed prompt, and any messages you send are appended after the prompt's own messages.

    Enhancing input quality

    Validation decides whether data may proceed. Enhancement makes the data that proceeds better. The reason to invest here is mechanical. A retrieval-augmented application is only as good as the passages it retrieves, and a model summarizing a transcript is only as good as the transcript. Duplicate text, boilerplate, broken encoding, and unlabeled entities all reduce retrieval precision and waste tokens, and no prompt can fully compensate. Garbage in, confident garbage out.

    Normalize with Lambda

    The cheapest improvements are deterministic and run in a Lambda function (or a Glue job for bulk data). They do not need a model, they are free of hallucination risk, and they give identical output every time.

    • Unicode and whitespace. Apply NFKC normalization so visually identical characters become identical code points, collapse runs of whitespace, and fix common mojibake such as â€™ in place of an apostrophe.
    • Boilerplate removal. Strip navigation menus, cookie banners, page headers and footers, "Page 3 of 40" lines, and email signatures. Repeated headers on every page are the most common OCR artifact, and they pollute every chunk.
    • OCR repair. Re-join words hyphenated across line breaks, drop lines with a very low letter ratio, and use Textract's per-block confidence to discard low-confidence fragments.
    • Structure preservation. Keep headings, list markers, and tables as Markdown. Structure that survives into the text gives the chunker natural split points and gives the model context about what a passage is.
    • Deduplication. Hash the normalized text to remove exact duplicates cheaply. Near-duplicates (the same document with a changed date) need fuzzy techniques such as MinHash, which you can run in a Glue Spark job. Keep the newest version and record the others in metadata.
    import re, hashlib, unicodedata

def normalize(text: str) -> str:
    text = unicodedata.normalize("NFKC", text)
    text = re.sub(r"-\n(\w)", r"\1", text)          # rejoin hyphenated line breaks
    text = re.sub(r"(?m)^\s*Page \d+ of \d+\s*$", "", text)
    text = re.sub(r"[ \t]+", " ", text)
    text = re.sub(r"\n{3,}", "\n\n", text)
    return text.strip()

def fingerprint(text: str) -> str:
    return hashlib.sha256(normalize(text).lower().encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

    Extract entities and detect PII with Comprehend

    Comprehend turns unstructured text into labels you can attach as metadata or act on. Three calls do most of the work. DetectDominantLanguage tells you which language a document is in, so you can route it to the right pipeline or reject it. DetectEntities returns people, organizations, locations, dates, and quantities, which become filterable metadata fields in the vector store (Chapter 4). DetectPiiEntities returns the type and character offsets of sensitive values such as names, email addresses, phone numbers, and payment card numbers. Each call returns a confidence score, and the offsets let you mask exactly the right spans.

    import boto3

comprehend = boto3.client("comprehend")

def redact_pii(text: str, language: str = "en", min_score: float = 0.8) -> str:
    found = comprehend.detect_pii_entities(Text=text, LanguageCode=language)["Entities"]
    for e in sorted(found, key=lambda e: e["BeginOffset"], reverse=True):
        if e["Score"] >= min_score:
            text = text[: e["BeginOffset"]] + f"[{e['Type']}]" + text[e["EndOffset"] :]
    return text

    The loop works from the end of the string toward the start so that replacing one span does not shift the offsets of the ones before it. Synchronous Comprehend calls have a per-request size limit and PII detection supports a limited set of languages, so split long documents and check the documentation for your language. For millions of documents, use the asynchronous batch jobs, which read from S3 and write results back, and which can produce a redacted copy of the text directly.

    Comprehend is not the only PII tool in the exam scope. Amazon Macie scans S3 buckets and reports where sensitive data lives, which suits an audit of existing storage. (Macie is on the list of services not available in the new AWS sign-up experience unless you activate advanced features, so you will likely read about it rather than run it.) Bedrock Guardrails filters PII at request and response time (Domain 3). Use Comprehend when you need to change the data before it is stored or embedded.

    Use a small model to reformat noisy text

    Some problems resist rules. A transcript of a spoken conversation, a table that Textract flattened, or an email thread with nested quotes needs judgment to clean. A small, inexpensive Bedrock model is a reasonable tool here, for example an Amazon Nova Micro or Lite model, or Claude Haiku. Typical tasks are turning a transcript into labeled Q&A pairs, rewriting a flattened table as Markdown, expanding abbreviations, splitting a long document into titled sections, and generating a one-line summary to store as metadata.

    def clean_transcript(raw: str) -> str:
    resp = bedrock.converse(
        modelId="us.amazon.nova-micro-v1:0",
        system=[{"text": (
            "Rewrite the transcript as clean prose. Remove filler words and false starts. "
            "Keep speaker labels. Do not add, remove, or change any facts, names, or numbers."
        )}],
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": raw}]}],
        inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 2000, "temperature": 0.0},
    )
    return resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"]

    This is powerful but it is a transformation by a probabilistic system, so build guardrails around it. Use temperature 0 for repeatable output. Verify the result, for instance by checking that every number and proper noun in the input still appears in the output, and that the length ratio is in a sensible range. Send failures to the quarantine instead of silently trusting them. For a large backlog, use Bedrock batch inference, which processes a file of requests asynchronously at a lower price than on-demand calls. Reserve the model for text where rules fail, because a regex that fixes 95 percent of the problem for free beats a model call on every document.

    Prepare text to be chunked

    The next chapters split documents into chunks and embed them. Enhancement is the last chance to make that easy. Make sure each document has a clear title and source in its metadata, because a chunk detached from its document is often ambiguous ("the policy" of which company?). Keep headings in the text so a structure-aware chunker can split on them. Keep tables whole and in Markdown. Remove content that does not help answer questions. Attach the language, the extracted entities, the document date, and the validation status as metadata, so you can filter at query time and trace answers back to a source.

    Exam tip

    When a question needs deterministic extraction of entities, PII, or language from text at scale and at low cost, choose Comprehend. When it needs flexible rewriting, summarizing, or reformatting that varies by document, choose a Bedrock model, ideally a small one. When it needs simple cleanup such as whitespace and encoding fixes, choose Lambda. Using a large model for every normalization step is almost never the best answer on a cost-focused question.

    Hands-on lab

    Analyze text with Comprehend in the console

    About 10 minutes · Cost: effectively free for a few short requests (Comprehend bills per 100-character unit and has a free tier) · Needs: a paragraph of sample text with names, an email address, and a phone number. Amazon Comprehend is not named in either the free-plan or paid-plan service list of the new AWS sign-up experience (only Comprehend Medical is listed, as unsupported), so on such a project the console may refuse it. If so, read the steps and the code sketch earlier in this section instead of running the lab, or activate advanced features if you want to run it.

    1. Open the Amazon Comprehend console in your selected Region and choose Launch Amazon Comprehend if the landing page appears. In the left menu, choose Real-time analysis.
    2. On the Built-in tab, keep the input type as Built-in, paste your sample text (for example a short support ticket that mentions a customer name, a city, a date, jane.doe@example.com, and a phone number), then choose Analyze.
    3. Open the Insights section and visit each tab in turn: Entities, Key phrases, Language, PII, and Sentiment. For each result, note the type, the confidence score, and the highlighted span.
    4. On the PII tab, compare the detected spans with what you expect. Did the model find the name, the email, and the phone number? Did it flag anything that is not sensitive?
    5. Change the text to Spanish, or to a language mix, and analyze again. Observe how language detection changes and which tabs become unavailable for that language.
    6. Optional: copy the sample into the Python snippet above and run redact_pii with your AWS credentials, to see the same detection through the API.

    Cleanup. Nothing is created by real-time analysis, so there is nothing to delete. Do not paste real customer data into the console.

    Chapter recap

    • Data flows through four stages: validate, convert by modality, clean and enrich, then format for the model. Fix problems at the earliest stage that can catch them.
    • Quality for FM input covers completeness, duplicates, encoding, freshness, PII, schema, and size. Bedrock Knowledge Bases skip unsupported or oversized files silently, so validate before ingestion.
    • Glue Data Quality evaluates DQDL rules on catalogued data and inside ETL jobs. Data Wrangler is the visual option. Lambda holds custom checks. CloudWatch metrics and alarms make quality visible, and quarantine plus a dead-letter queue handle failures.
    • Match modality to service: Textract for scanned documents, forms, and tables; Transcribe for audio; Comprehend for text analytics and PII; Rekognition for image labels and moderation; a Bedrock multimodal model for reasoning over images; Athena, Glue, and SageMaker Processing for tables.
    • Use Converse for a uniform request shape across models. InvokeModel bodies differ by provider, and embedding models use InvokeModel. Always set maxTokens, read stopReason, and log usage.
    • Conversation history is a sliding window or summary of alternating user and assistant turns, stored outside the request. SageMaker endpoints take whatever payload the serving container defines, and open models need the right chat template.
    • Enhance with deterministic normalization first, Comprehend for entities and PII second, and a small Bedrock model only for what rules cannot do, with validation of its output.

    Knowledge check

    1. A company receives thousands of multi-page scanned supplier invoices as PDFs in S3. It needs vendor name, invoice number, and the line-item table extracted into JSON with the least development effort. Which approach is best?

      Answer

      Use Amazon Textract with the asynchronous StartDocumentAnalysis API and the FORMS and TABLES features (or AnalyzeExpense for invoices), triggered by S3 events. Textract is built for forms and tables. Rekognition DetectText returns text without structure, Comprehend works on text that already exists and does not do OCR, and sending every page image to a large multimodal model works but costs more, is less deterministic, and needs more validation.

    2. A contact center wants to summarize recorded customer calls with a foundation model. The summaries must attribute statements to the agent or the customer, and card numbers spoken on the call must never reach the model or logs. What should the pipeline do before invoking Bedrock?

      Answer

      Run an Amazon Transcribe batch job with speaker partitioning (or channel identification) and content redaction enabled, then pass the redacted, speaker-labeled transcript to Bedrock. Redacting at the transcript stage keeps the sensitive values out of every downstream system. Applying only an output guardrail would let the raw values enter the prompt and the invocation logs, and Comprehend cannot process audio.

    3. An application calls Amazon Bedrock with InvokeModel using a request body written for Anthropic Claude. The team switches the model ID to a Meta Llama model and every call fails with "Malformed input request". The team also expects to change models again. What is the best fix?

      Answer

      Move to the Converse API, which uses one request structure (messages, system, inferenceConfig) for all supported models and translates it to each provider's format. Rewriting the body for Llama would fix only this one switch. Setting temperature or maxTokens differently does not address a body that does not match the provider's schema, and a SageMaker endpoint is unnecessary for a model Bedrock already serves.

    4. A knowledge-base assistant sometimes quotes last year's policy, and the team finds that some uploaded files never appear in search results. They want to detect both problems automatically and be alerted. Which combination is most appropriate?

      Answer

      Add a validation stage before ingestion. Use Glue Data Quality rules (such as a DataFreshness rule on the modified date and uniqueness on document ID) for catalogued metadata, and a Lambda validator that checks file type and size against what the Knowledge Base supports. Publish accepted and rejected counts and the quality score to CloudWatch, alarm on them through SNS, and quarantine failures. Changing the model or raising the number of retrieved passages treats the symptom and leaves stale and missing documents in place.

    5. A team must find and mask names, email addresses, and phone numbers in two million English support tickets before embedding them. They want a consistent result for the same input, low cost per ticket, and no prompt engineering. Which service fits?

      Answer

      Amazon Comprehend PII detection, using an asynchronous batch job over the tickets in S3 (with redaction output) or DetectPiiEntities inside a Lambda function. It is deterministic, priced per unit of text, and returns offsets and scores. Asking an LLM to find PII in every ticket costs more and can miss or invent values. Macie finds sensitive data in S3 for auditing but does not rewrite the text.

    6. Analysts want to ask natural-language questions about a 40,000-row sales CSV, such as "What was total revenue in Germany last quarter?" Pasting the CSV into the prompt exceeds the context window. What is the best design?

      Answer

      Register the data in the Glue Data Catalog, query it with Amazon Athena, and have the model generate a SQL query (or call a tool that runs one) so Athena computes the answer and only the small result goes back to the model. Splitting the CSV into many prompts still leaves aggregation to the model, which is error-prone and costly. Embedding every row as text supports lookup of individual rows but not totals.

    Chapter 4 · Task 1.4

    Design and implement vector store solutions

    Retrieval Augmented Generation is only as good as the store it retrieves from. This chapter covers where embeddings live on AWS, how to choose between the half dozen options, how to attach metadata so searches are precise and safe, how to make the store fast at scale, how to connect it to the places your documents already live, and how to keep it from going stale.

    Exam skills covered

    • 1.4.1 Vector database architectures for semantic retrieval: Bedrock Knowledge Bases, OpenSearch with the neural plugin, RDS and Aurora with pgvector, DynamoDB alongside a vector store
    • 1.4.2 Metadata frameworks: S3 object metadata, custom attributes, tags, and filters
    • 1.4.3 High-performance designs: sharding, multi-index layouts, hierarchical indexing
    • 1.4.4 Integration components for document management systems, wikis, and other knowledge sources
    • 1.4.5 Data maintenance: incremental updates, change detection, synchronization, scheduled refresh

    What a vector store actually does

    You already know the idea. An embedding model turns a chunk of text into a list of numbers, a vector, so that chunks with similar meaning end up close together in that number space. At question time you embed the question, find the stored vectors nearest to it, and hand the matching chunks to the model as context. The component that answers "which stored vectors are nearest to this one?" is the vector store.

    Two details matter for everything that follows. First, an exact nearest-neighbor search compares the query against every stored vector, which is too slow once you have millions of them. Production stores use approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) indexes, usually a graph structure called HNSW, that trade a small loss in accuracy for a very large gain in speed. Second, a vector store rarely holds only vectors. Each row also carries the original text chunk, a pointer back to the source document, and metadata such as author or date. Most design decisions in this chapter come from deciding where those three things live and how they are kept in step.

    Backend analogy

    Think of a vector store as a database index you query by similarity instead of by equality. An HNSW index plays the role a B-tree plays in PostgreSQL: it is a precomputed structure that makes lookups fast, it costs memory and write time to maintain, and its settings change the speed and accuracy you get. The metadata filter is your WHERE clause.

    On AWS you will meet this component in two forms. In the first, you pick a store yourself (OpenSearch, Aurora, and so on) and either manage the pipeline around it or let a Bedrock Knowledge Base drive it. In the second, you use a Managed Knowledge Base where AWS hides the store entirely. The exam focuses on the first form, because choosing a store is a design decision with trade-offs, and the exam asks about trade-offs. The second form is worth knowing about and is covered later in this chapter.

    The AWS vector store options

    AWS does not have one vector database. It has vector capability spread across several services, and the right one depends on what you already run, how big the corpus is, how fast queries must be, and how much operating work you will accept. Here is the full map before the detail.

    StoreModelWorks with Bedrock Knowledge BasesChoose it when
    Amazon S3 VectorsServerless, vector buckets and indexesYesYou want the lowest storage cost, queries are infrequent or moderate, and you want nothing to operate
    OpenSearch Serverless (vector search collection)Serverless, billed by capacity unitsYesYou need rich filtering and hybrid search with no cluster management and can afford an always-on minimum
    OpenSearch Service (managed domain)Cluster you size and tuneYesYou need maximum control, the neural plugin, large scale, or already run OpenSearch
    Aurora PostgreSQL / RDS PostgreSQL with pgvectorRelational database with a vector typeYes (Aurora)Your data is already relational and you want vectors, metadata, and SQL joins in one place
    Neptune AnalyticsGraph database with vector searchYesRelationships between entities matter, as in GraphRAG
    Pinecone, Redis Enterprise Cloud, MongoDB AtlasThird-party, you hold the accountYesYour organization already standardized on one of them
