    MemoryDB, DocumentDB, ElastiCacheDatabases with vector search addedNot as KB storesYou are building a custom pipeline and need very low latency (MemoryDB) or are already on a document database (DocumentDB)
    DynamoDBKey-value and document storeNoIt holds metadata, conversation state, or document records next to a real vector store. It does not do vector search itself

    Amazon S3 Vectors

    S3 Vectors is the newest option and the cheapest way to hold a large number of embeddings. It is a new kind of S3 bucket, called a vector bucket, that contains vector indexes. You create an index with a fixed dimension count and a distance metric (cosine or Euclidean), write vectors with optional metadata, and query for nearest neighbors through a dedicated API under the s3vectors namespace. There are no servers, no capacity to provision, and it inherits S3 durability. The trade-off is query performance. It is tuned for infrequent to moderate query rates and does not match a dedicated search engine for very low latency or very high sustained query throughput, and it supports fewer filter operators. AWS documents sub-second latency for cold queries and as low as about 100 milliseconds for warm queries through a Knowledge Base. For a Knowledge Base that serves an internal assistant a few thousand times a day, it is often the right default. It is available in most commercial Regions, but confirm your project's Region on the S3 Vectors Regions page.

    Amazon OpenSearch Service and OpenSearch Serverless

    OpenSearch is the workhorse. It began as a text search engine, so it is strong at the thing pure vector stores are weaker at: combining keyword relevance and vector similarity in one query. It stores vectors in a knn_vector field type and supports several ANN engines, most commonly the Lucene and Faiss engines, with HNSW as the standard index structure.

    It comes in two deployment models, and the exam likes to contrast them.

    • OpenSearch Serverless gives you a collection of type vector search. You do not choose instance types or shard counts. Capacity is measured in OpenSearch Compute Units (OCUs) that scale with load. It is the easiest OpenSearch option to run, and it is one of the stores the Bedrock quick-create wizard can provision for you.
    • OpenSearch Service (managed domain) is a cluster of nodes you size. You pick instance families, node counts, shard layout, and replica counts, and you can install and configure plugins. You get control over performance tuning in exchange for owning the capacity planning.

    The managed domain is where the neural plugin matters. Neural search lets OpenSearch call an embedding model for you. You register a model connector that points at a Bedrock embedding model, define an ingest pipeline that embeds a text field when documents are indexed, and then send plain-text queries that OpenSearch embeds at query time. Without the plugin, your application code does the embedding on both sides and only sends vectors to OpenSearch. With it, indexing and querying use text, and the embedding model choice becomes a cluster configuration detail. That is the "Neural plugin for Amazon Bedrock integration" the exam guide names.

    Watch out

    Classic OpenSearch Serverless collections bill for a minimum amount of compute whenever a collection exists, whether or not anyone queries it. Compute is priced per OCU-hour (about $0.24 in US East, N. Virginia), so a redundant production-style collection that idles all month can cost several hundred dollars, and AWS also offers a dev/test mode without standby replicas that has a much smaller floor. AWS has since added NextGen collections, which its pricing page describes as having no minimum OCU and scaling to zero after about ten minutes of inactivity. This catches learners often because the Bedrock console wizard offers OpenSearch Serverless as a store option. Check which store the wizard preselects, check the current OpenSearch Serverless pricing page for the collection type you get, and delete the collection when you finish a lab.

    Aurora and RDS for PostgreSQL with pgvector

    The pgvector extension adds a vector column type and similarity operators to PostgreSQL, plus two ANN index types: HNSW and IVFFlat. Both Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL and Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL support it. The appeal for a backend developer is obvious. Your embeddings sit in the same database as your users, products, or tickets, so one SQL query can filter by a relational condition, join to another table, and order by vector distance. You get transactions, backups, and IAM-integrated access you already understand.

    The limits are real. A relational database scales vectors vertically and with read replicas, not by horizontally sharding an index, so very large corpora and very high query rates push you toward OpenSearch. Index build and memory behavior also need attention at scale. For a Knowledge Base, Aurora PostgreSQL is a supported store, and Bedrock reaches it through the RDS Data API, an HTTPS interface to the database, with credentials held in AWS Secrets Manager. It expects a table with specific columns: a primary key, the embedding vector, the text chunk, and a JSON metadata column.

    The exam guide also mentions "Amazon RDS with Amazon S3 document repositories". That is a pattern rather than a feature: the source documents stay in S3 as the system of record, and RDS or Aurora holds the embeddings, chunk text, and metadata with a pointer back to each S3 object. When a user needs the full original document, the application follows the pointer.

    Neptune Analytics

    Neptune Analytics is a graph analytics engine that also performs vector search. A Knowledge Base can use it to build a graph of entities and relationships extracted from your documents, which supports GraphRAG. Plain vector retrieval finds chunks that sound like the question. GraphRAG can also follow connections, so a question such as "which suppliers affect this product line?" can pull in related chunks that never mention the product by name. Pick it when relationships carry meaning and plain similarity keeps missing connected facts.

    Third-party stores, and other AWS databases

    Knowledge Bases can also use Pinecone, Redis Enterprise Cloud, and MongoDB Atlas. You create the index there, hold its credentials in Secrets Manager, and give Bedrock the connection details. The reason to choose one is almost always that your organization already uses it. Amazon MemoryDB offers in-memory vector search with very low latency and Amazon DocumentDB offers vector search on document data. Neither is a Knowledge Base store, but both appear as options when you build a custom retrieval pipeline.

    Where DynamoDB fits

    DynamoDB cannot answer "find the nearest vectors" on its own. The exam guide lists it because it is the natural home for the other data in a RAG system: document records, per-user access lists, conversation history, and rich metadata that would bloat the vector index. A common design keeps a lean vector entry in OpenSearch (embedding plus a document ID) and the full record in DynamoDB. After vector search returns IDs, the application fetches the records with a single BatchGetItem. DynamoDB Streams or the DynamoDB zero-ETL integration with OpenSearch can keep the two in sync when items change.

    Exam tip

    Match the store to the stated constraint. "Already running Aurora, need SQL joins with vectors" points to pgvector. "Lowest cost, infrequent queries, no servers" points to S3 Vectors. "Hybrid keyword and vector search at scale with fine control" points to OpenSearch. "Relationships between entities" points to Neptune Analytics. "Single-digit-millisecond latency" points to MemoryDB. DynamoDB is never the vector index; it is the metadata and state companion.

    Choosing a store

    Exam scenarios give you three or four constraints and ask for the best fit. Five questions settle most of them. Where does the data already live? How many vectors will you hold? How fast must a query return, and at what rate? Do you need keyword and vector search in the same query? How much operating work will the team accept?

    DimensionS3 VectorsOpenSearch ServerlessOpenSearch managedAurora pgvector
    ScaleVery large, elasticLarge, auto-scalingVery large, you shardMedium to large, vertical plus replicas
    Query latencyHigher, tuned for light to moderate useLowLowest when tunedLow to moderate
    Hybrid keyword + vectorNoYesYesYes (full-text plus vector; supported as a Knowledge Base search type when the table has a filterable text field)
    Rich metadata filteringBasicRichRichFull SQL
    Cost shapePay per use, lowest idle costHourly OCU floor on classic collections, even when idlePay for provisioned nodesPay for the database you probably already have
    OperationsNoneLowHighestMedium
     
    flowchart TD
    A["New vector store decision"] --> B{"Data already in
    a relational DB?"}
    B -- "Yes, need SQL joins" --> C["Aurora or RDS
    PostgreSQL with pgvector"]
    B -- "No" --> D{"Relationships between
    entities drive answers?"}
    D -- "Yes" --> E["Neptune Analytics

    Treat the diagram as a starting point rather than a law. Real decisions also hinge on compliance (can the data leave your VPC?), existing skills, and the cost of moving later. The cost of moving is higher than it looks. If you change embedding models you must re-embed everything anyway, which is the natural moment to change stores too, so do not agonize over lock-in at the first design.

    Exam tip

    When a question stresses "minimal operational overhead", rule out the managed OpenSearch domain and self-built pipelines first. When it stresses "cost-effective" for a rarely queried archive, think S3 Vectors. When it stresses "combine keyword matching with semantic similarity", you need a store with hybrid search, which means OpenSearch or PostgreSQL rather than S3 Vectors.

    Where Bedrock Knowledge Bases fit

    A Knowledge Base is Bedrock's managed RAG pipeline. You point it at a data source, and it handles the repetitive work: reading documents, parsing them, splitting them into chunks, calling an embedding model, and writing the vectors and metadata into a store. At question time you call the Retrieve API to get the matching chunks, or RetrieveAndGenerate to have Bedrock retrieve and then ask a model to write a grounded answer with citations.

    flowchart LR
    S["Data source
    S3, web, Confluence,
    SharePoint, Salesforce"] --> P["Parse and chunk"]
    P --> E["Embedding model
    Titan Text Embeddings V2"]
    E --> V[("Vector store
    S3 Vectors, OpenSearch,
    Aurora, Neptune, others")]
    Q["User question"] --> QE["Embed question"]
    QE --> V
    V --> R["Top chunks + metadata"]
    R --> M["Foundation model
    writes grounded answer"]

    There are two flavors, and you should know both. In a customer-managed Knowledge Base (type VECTOR), you choose the embedding model, the chunking strategy, and the vector store, and you create the store yourself. This is the model the exam guide describes, and it is where the store comparison in the previous section applies. In a Managed Knowledge Base, a newer option, AWS handles storage, parsing, chunking, and retrieval for you. You create the Knowledge Base, connect a data source, and sync. There is no vector store to provision. AWS now recommends the managed form for new work where it fits, but it hides the very decisions this exam domain tests, so expect questions to assume you are choosing the store.

    Note

    Feature availability for Managed Knowledge Bases differs by Region and changes quickly, including the list of connectors it supports. Confirm in the Bedrock documentation before you plan around it. The core ideas in this chapter (metadata, filters, sync, freshness) apply to both flavors.

    Hierarchical organization and topic-based segmentation

    Once a corpus grows past a few thousand documents, a single flat index starts to hurt. Retrieval returns near-miss chunks from unrelated areas, and every query pays to search everything. There are four ways to organize the data, and a good design often combines two.

    1. One Knowledge Base per domain. HR, legal, and engineering each get their own Knowledge Base with its own data sources and permissions. The application, or a routing prompt, decides which to query. This gives hard isolation and independent sync schedules, at the price of more resources to run.
    2. One Knowledge Base, filtered by metadata. Every document carries a domain attribute, and queries filter on it. This is cheaper and simpler, and it is the right first move. It is covered in the next section.
    3. Several indexes in one OpenSearch domain. Each topic gets its own index, possibly with its own analyzer settings, embedding model, or neural ingest pipeline. This is the "topic-based segmentation" the exam guide hints at, and it lets you tune each domain separately.
    4. Hierarchical retrieval. Search small, retrieve big. Chunks are stored at two levels: small child chunks for precise matching and larger parent chunks for context. A query matches the child, and the parent is what goes to the model. Bedrock's hierarchical chunking implements this directly, and Chapter 5 covers how it works.

    Backend analogy

    Per-domain Knowledge Bases are separate schemas or databases. A metadata filter is a partition key or a tenant column. Multiple OpenSearch indexes are separate tables with their own indexes. Pick based on the same questions you would ask for a relational design: do these datasets need different access control, different tuning, or different lifecycles?

    Hands-on lab

    Build a small Knowledge Base on the cheapest vector store

    About 25 minutes · Cost: cents if you use S3 Vectors and clean up; several dollars or more per day if the wizard creates a classic OpenSearch Serverless collection and you forget · Needs: your project's selected Region, an S3 bucket

    1. Confirm your selected Region in the Region selector at the top right of the console. Create everything in that Region only.
    2. Open the S3 console and choose Create bucket. Name it something like kb-lab-docs-yourname, keep Block Public Access on, and create it.
    3. On your computer, create four small text files and upload them to the bucket with Upload. Suggested names and contents: leave-policy.txt (annual leave rules), expenses-policy.txt (travel and meal limits), vpn-setup.txt (how to connect to the corporate VPN), incident-runbook.txt (what to do when production is down). Ten to twenty lines each is enough.
    4. Open the Amazon Bedrock console. In the left navigation under Build, choose Knowledge Bases, then Create, and choose the option to create a knowledge base with a vector store.
    5. Give it a name such as kb-lab. Let the console create a new service role. Set the data source type to Amazon S3 and choose your bucket.
    6. Leave parsing and chunking on their defaults for now. Chapter 5 returns to chunking.
    7. For the embeddings model choose Titan Text Embeddings V2. If the console asks you to enable model access first, do that and return.
    8. For the vector store choose Quick create a new vector store and look at the store options. Choose the S3 vector bucket (S3 Vectors) option, which quick create can provision for you; S3 Vectors is available in most commercial Regions, but confirm yours. Do not accept OpenSearch Serverless unless you are prepared to delete it within the hour. If S3 Vectors is not offered, a quick-create Aurora option may be cheaper than OpenSearch Serverless for a short lab, but check its pricing first.
    9. Review and choose Create knowledge base. Creation takes a few minutes while the console builds the store and role.
    10. On the Knowledge Base page, select the S3 data source and choose Sync. Wait until the status shows complete. Read the sync results: you should see four documents scanned and four indexed.
    11. In the Test knowledge base panel, turn off response generation if you want only retrieval, and ask "How do I connect to the VPN?" Expand the source details and confirm the top chunk comes from vpn-setup.txt.

    Cleanup. Keep this Knowledge Base for the metadata lab below. When you finish with it, delete the Knowledge Base, then delete the vector store it used (the S3 vector bucket, or the OpenSearch Serverless collection if one was created), then empty and delete the S3 bucket and the service role. Deleting only the Knowledge Base leaves the store and its charges behind.

    Metadata frameworks

    Semantic similarity is good at "what is this about?" and bad at "which of these is current, who wrote it, and is this person allowed to see it?" Metadata answers those questions. A well-designed metadata scheme is the difference between a demo that sometimes quotes a 2019 policy and a system you can trust with real users.

    The three places metadata can live

    AWS gives you three separate places to attach facts to a document, and mixing them up is a common source of confusion and exam traps.

    • S3 object metadata is a set of key-value headers stored with the object. System metadata (content type, last modified time) is set by S3. User-defined metadata (keys prefixed x-amz-meta-) is set by whoever uploads. It travels with the object and is immutable after upload without copying the object.
    • S3 object tags are up to ten key-value pairs per object, editable after upload. They are designed for cost allocation, lifecycle rules, and IAM conditions. A tag such as classification=confidential can drive both an S3 Lifecycle rule and an access policy.
    • Knowledge Base metadata files are what Bedrock actually indexes and filters on. For each document you add a sidecar file in the same S3 location named after the document with .metadata.json appended, such as handbook.pdf.metadata.json.

    The sidecar file is the one that matters for retrieval. A Knowledge Base does not automatically read your S3 tags or user-defined metadata as filterable attributes. If those facts already exist on your objects, a small piece of glue code has to copy them into a sidecar file. That glue is a good Lambda function, and it is the pattern the exam guide describes when it mentions timestamps from S3 metadata, authorship from custom attributes, and domain classification from tags.

    {
  "metadataAttributes": {
    "department": "hr",
    "doc_type": "policy",
    "author": "j.rivera",
    "year": 2025,
    "effective_epoch": 1735689600,
    "audience": ["all-staff", "managers"],
    "superseded": false
  }
}

    Attribute values can be strings, numbers, booleans, or lists of strings. Keep the file small, because there are limits on its size, and some stores limit how much filterable metadata a single vector can carry. S3 Vectors, for instance, separates filterable from non-filterable metadata. Bedrock stores the bulky chunk text as non-filterable so your attributes stay within the filterable allowance. With Knowledge Bases on S3 Vectors you can attach up to about 1 KB of custom metadata and 35 metadata keys per vector, and very large hierarchical chunks can overflow the limit because the parent-child relationships are stored as non-filterable metadata too. Do not put long free text in attributes.

    Bedrock also attaches its own attributes to every chunk, including the source URI, chunk ID, and data source ID. These come back with each retrieved result, so you can always trace a chunk to its document, which Chapter 5 and the governance domain both rely on.

    Designing the attribute set

    Design attributes the way you would design columns you plan to filter on. Four groups cover most needs.

    1. Provenance. Source system, author or owner, original URL. These support citations and audits.
    2. Time. Created date, effective date, last reviewed, and a superseded flag. Store dates as epoch numbers so you can use range filters. Time metadata is how you stop a retriever from preferring an old version of a policy.
    3. Classification. Domain or topic, document type, product, language, region. These narrow the search space and sharpen precision.
    4. Access. Audience, tenant ID, or clearance level. These scope what a given user may retrieve.

    Filtering at query time

    The filter runs before the similarity ranking is returned to you, narrowing the candidate set to documents that match. You pass it in the retrievalConfiguration of the Retrieve call. The available operators are equals, notEquals, greaterThan, greaterThanOrEquals, lessThan, lessThanOrEquals, in, notIn, startsWith, stringContains, and listContains, combined with andAll and orAll.

    import boto3

agent_rt = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime")

def retrieve_for_user(question: str, kb_id: str, department: str, audiences: list[str]):
    resp = agent_rt.retrieve(
        knowledgeBaseId=kb_id,
        retrievalQuery={"text": question},
        retrievalConfiguration={
            "vectorSearchConfiguration": {
                "numberOfResults": 5,
                "filter": {
                    "andAll": [
                        {"equals": {"key": "department", "value": department}},
                        {"equals": {"key": "superseded", "value": False}},
                        {"in": {"key": "audience", "value": audiences}},
                    ]
                },
            }
        },
    )
    return [
        {
            "text": r["content"]["text"],
            "score": r.get("score"),
            "source": r["location"]["s3Location"]["uri"],
            "meta": r.get("metadata", {}),
        }
        for r in resp["retrievalResults"]
    ]

    Watch out

    A filter on an attribute the document does not carry returns no results, and it does so without raising an error. Some operators are only fully supported on certain stores. startsWith and stringContains work best on OpenSearch Serverless, and on an S3 vector bucket they are silently ignored. If a filter seems to do nothing, check the operator against the store before you suspect your data.

    Metadata as an access control mechanism

    Filtering is the standard way to enforce document-level access in a shared Knowledge Base. You tag each document with an audience or tenant at ingestion, then add a filter built from the caller's identity at query time. Two rules keep this honest. First, build the filter on the server from verified identity claims, for example the groups claim in a Cognito or IAM Identity Center token. Never accept the filter from the client, because a user who can edit the request can read anything. Second, remember what a filter is: a query constraint inside a store that holds everything. If two tenants must never share infrastructure, because of contract or regulation, use separate Knowledge Bases or even separate accounts, and treat filters as a second layer rather than the only layer.

    Also keep in mind that the retrieved chunk text is returned to your application as raw text. Guardrails apply to the prompt and to the generated answer, but not to the raw retrieval results in the API response. Redact sensitive data before ingestion and avoid logging full retrieved chunks for sensitive workloads.

    Where DynamoDB and S3 metadata complement the store

    Not every fact belongs in the vector index. A common split puts fast-changing or bulky information in DynamoDB. The sidecar file carries only the attributes you filter on, such as department and year, plus a doc_id. When a chunk comes back, the application uses doc_id to fetch the full record from DynamoDB: title, owner, approval status, a signed link to the original, and the user's permission list. Changing an owner or an approval status then updates one DynamoDB item instead of forcing a re-ingest of the document.

    def build_sidecar_from_tags(s3, bucket: str, key: str) -> None:
    """Triggered after upload. Copies S3 tags and user metadata into a Knowledge Base sidecar."""
    import json
    tags = {t["Key"]: t["Value"] for t in s3.get_object_tagging(Bucket=bucket, Key=key)["TagSet"]}
    head = s3.head_object(Bucket=bucket, Key=key)
    attrs = {
        "department": tags.get("department", "unknown"),
        "classification": tags.get("classification", "internal"),
        "author": head["Metadata"].get("author", "unknown"),
        "effective_epoch": int(head["LastModified"].timestamp()),
    }
    s3.put_object(
        Bucket=bucket,
        Key=f"{key}.metadata.json",
        Body=json.dumps({"metadataAttributes": attrs}),
        ContentType="application/json",
    )

    Exam tip

    If a question asks how to restrict which documents a user can retrieve from a shared Knowledge Base, the answer is metadata attributes plus a retrieval filter built server-side from the caller's identity. If it asks how to get S3 tags or timestamps into the filter, the answer involves generating metadata sidecar files, typically with a Lambda function. Both facts come up repeatedly.

    Hands-on lab

    Add metadata files and watch a filter change the results

    About 20 minutes · Cost: a few cents of embedding and storage · Needs: the Knowledge Base and S3 bucket from the previous lab

    1. On your computer, create four small JSON files. Each file name is the document name plus .metadata.json, exactly: leave-policy.txt.metadata.json, expenses-policy.txt.metadata.json, vpn-setup.txt.metadata.json, incident-runbook.txt.metadata.json.
    2. Give each one a body of the form {"metadataAttributes": {"department": "hr", "year": 2025, "audience": "all-staff"}} and change the values per document: leave policy is hr, expenses is finance with year 2024, VPN setup is it, and the incident runbook is it with audience oncall.
    3. In the S3 console, upload the four files into the same bucket and folder as the documents. They must sit next to the documents they describe.
    4. Return to Amazon Bedrock → Knowledge Bases, open kb-lab, select the data source, and choose Sync again. In the sync results note that the count of modified documents and metadata files reflects the new files, and that unchanged documents were not re-embedded. That is the incremental behavior described later in this chapter.
    5. In the Test knowledge base panel, ask "What do I do when something is broken?" with no filter and note which documents come back.
    6. Open the test panel's configuration options and add a metadata filter: key department, operator equals, value hr. Ask the same question. The IT runbook should disappear, and you will likely see a weak or unrelated HR result. This shows that filters narrow the candidate set first and similarity ranks only what is left.
    7. Change the filter to audience equals oncall and ask again. Only the runbook should return.
    8. Add a second filter on year greater than 2024 combined with the first using AND, and predict the result before you run it.

    Cleanup. Nothing new to delete. The metadata files live in the same bucket you remove at the end of the chapter.

    Performance at scale

    A Knowledge Base over a few hundred documents works with default settings. The interesting engineering starts when the corpus reaches millions of chunks, query volume rises, or latency budgets tighten. Most of the tuning below applies to OpenSearch, because it exposes the most controls, but the underlying ideas carry over to other stores.

    How HNSW works, and the three parameters that matter

    HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World) builds a multi-layer graph in which each vector links to nearby vectors. A search enters at the top layer, hops toward the query across sparse long-range links, then descends to denser layers to refine the answer. It is fast because it visits only a small fraction of the vectors. It is approximate because the walk can miss a true neighbor. Three settings control the balance.

    ParameterSet whenRaising it gives youIt costs you
    mIndex creationBetter recall, especially on high-dimension dataMore memory per vector, slower indexing
    ef_constructionIndex creationA higher-quality graph and better recallSlower indexing, no extra memory at query time
    ef_searchQuery time or index settingBetter recallHigher latency per query

    m and ef_construction are baked in when you create the index, so changing them means re-indexing. ef_search can be adjusted without rebuilding, which makes it the first knob to turn when you are trading recall for speed. The exam will not ask for numeric values, but it does expect you to know which parameter is fixed at creation and which is a query-time dial.

    Memory sizing

    OpenSearch k-NN graphs must sit in memory for fast search, and this is what makes vector workloads expensive. A widely used estimate for float32 HNSW vectors is about 1.1 × (4 × dimensions + 8 × m) bytes per vector. With 1,024 dimensions and m of 16, that is roughly 4.6 KB per vector. Ten million chunks therefore need around 46 GB of graph memory before replicas. One replica doubles it. Instance memory is shared with the operating system and the rest of the engine, so you size the cluster well above this figure.

    You have three levers to reduce that bill.

    • Fewer dimensions. Titan Text Embeddings V2 lets you choose 1,024, 512, or 256 dimensions. Smaller vectors cut memory and speed up search at some cost in accuracy, so measure the accuracy loss on your own questions before committing.
    • Quantization. Store each number in fewer bits: half precision, 8-bit integers, or binary. Memory falls by roughly two, four, or many times, again with some recall loss that you can often win back by retrieving more candidates and re-scoring.
    • Disk-backed vector search. Newer OpenSearch versions can keep compressed vectors in memory and full-precision copies on disk for re-scoring, trading some latency for a large cost reduction on huge corpora.

    Shards, replicas, and ingest settings

    An OpenSearch index is split into primary shards, and each shard holds its own HNSW graph. A query runs against every shard in parallel and the coordinator merges the results. More shards add parallelism, but each query now searches more graphs, so shards that are too small waste work. The number of primary shards is fixed when you create the index. Aim for shards in the tens of gigabytes and avoid creating hundreds of tiny ones. Replicas are full copies of shards. They raise read throughput and give you failover, and they multiply memory use.

    For the initial load, bulk-index in large batches, temporarily set replicas to zero and relax the refresh interval, then restore them afterward. After heavy writes, merging index segments reduces the number of graphs each query must walk. On OpenSearch Serverless these knobs disappear, which is exactly the point of the service: it scales capacity for you, and you pay a premium for not tuning.

    Filters and recall

    Filtering has a performance subtlety that is easy to miss. If a store runs the vector search first and applies the filter afterward, a restrictive filter can leave you with fewer than k results, or none. Engines that apply the filter during graph traversal avoid this but spend more effort when the filter excludes most vectors. PostgreSQL with pgvector has the same issue, and recent pgvector versions add iterative index scans to continue searching until enough filtered results are found. If a question describes empty or short result lists under tight filters, this is the cause, and the usual fixes are efficient filtering, a larger candidate pool, or splitting highly selective segments into their own index.

    Multi-index and hierarchical designs

    Two structural techniques scale better than tuning a single giant index.

    Multi-index by domain. Separate indexes for legal, support, and engineering content mean each search touches only its own data, each index can use the embedding model or analyzer that suits its content, and each can be re-indexed on its own schedule. An OpenSearch alias lets one name point at several indexes when you do want to search across them.

    Two-stage hierarchical retrieval. Keep a small index of document-level summaries or titles, and a large index of chunks. The first stage finds the handful of relevant documents. The second stage searches chunks restricted to those document IDs. The search space shrinks by orders of magnitude, and the model sees chunks from the right documents rather than lookalike fragments from the wrong ones.

    flowchart LR
    Q["Question"] --> E["Embed question"]
    E --> S1["Stage 1: search
    document summary index"]
    S1 --> D["Top 5 document IDs"]
    D --> S2["Stage 2: search chunk index
    filtered to those IDs"]
    E --> S2
    S2 --> C["Top chunks + parent context"]
    C --> M["Foundation model"]

    Exam tip

    When a scenario says "latency is too high at scale" for OpenSearch, think replicas for read throughput, more or better-sized shards, and a lower ef_search. When it says "memory cost is too high", think smaller embedding dimensions and quantization. When it says "results are irrelevant across very different content", think separate indexes per domain or a two-stage design. Do not choose a larger foundation model to fix a retrieval problem.

    Connecting to the places your knowledge lives

    Documents rarely start in a tidy S3 bucket. They live in Confluence spaces, SharePoint libraries, Salesforce records, file servers, partner SFTP drops, and internal wikis with no vendor connector. The integration job is to move that content to where the Knowledge Base can read it, repeatedly, with credentials and permissions handled safely.

    Knowledge Base data source connectors

    A Knowledge Base reads from a data source. Supported types include Amazon S3, a web crawler for public or internal sites you specify, Confluence, SharePoint, and Salesforce, plus a custom data source where you push documents in through an API and no connector is involved. The supported list grows over time, so check the current documentation.

    S3 needs no stored credential because the Knowledge Base's service role is granted read access to the bucket. SaaS connectors need credentials, and you store these in AWS Secrets Manager, never in the data source configuration. The service role gets permission to read that one secret. Authentication methods change as vendors retire old ones. Microsoft, for instance, has retired some legacy SharePoint authentication approaches, so follow the current connector documentation rather than an older tutorial.

    Build it or buy it

    Two other AWS services solve overlapping problems and appear on the exam as alternatives.

    • Amazon Kendra is a managed enterprise search service with dozens of prebuilt connectors and its own index. A Knowledge Base can use a Kendra GenAI index as its retrieval layer, which is a good fit when you already invested in Kendra connectors.
    • Amazon Q Business is a finished, identity-aware enterprise assistant. It ships with many connectors, applies each source system's document permissions at answer time, and has its own chat interface. Choose it when the requirement is "give employees an assistant over our company data quickly" and not "build a custom application". Choose Knowledge Bases when you need control over chunking, models, prompts, and the surrounding application.

    Glue services for sources without a connector

    Source situationServiceWhat it does here
    SaaS applications such as Salesforce, ServiceNow, Slack, ZendeskAmazon AppFlowRuns scheduled or event-triggered flows that land records in S3 without code
    On-premises NFS or SMB file sharesAWS DataSyncTransfers files to S3 on a schedule and copies only changes
    Partners who deliver files by SFTPAWS Transfer FamilyProvides a managed SFTP endpoint that writes straight into S3
    Internal wiki or document management system with a REST API or webhookAPI Gateway + LambdaReceives change events, fetches content, converts it, writes it to S3 or pushes it to a custom data source
    Multi-step conversion (HTML to text, scanned PDFs, deduplication)Step Functions, Textract, GlueOrchestrates cleanup before the content reaches the Knowledge Base
     
    flowchart LR
    CF["Confluence"] -->|"native connector"| KB
    SP["SharePoint"] -->|"native connector"| KB
    SF["Salesforce"] -->|"AppFlow"| S3["S3 bucket"]
    FS["On-prem file server"] -->|"DataSync"| S3
    PT["Partner files"] -->|"Transfer Family SFTP"| S3
    WK["Internal wiki"] -->|"webhook, API Gateway, Lambda"| S3
    S3 --> KB["Bedrock Knowledge Base"]
    KB --> VS[("Vector store")]

    Note

    Production traffic between your application and Bedrock can stay off the public internet. A VPC interface endpoint for Bedrock runtime and agent runtime, and a gateway endpoint for S3, keep retrieval calls and document transfers on the AWS network. Domain 3 covers this in depth, but plan for it when you design the integration layer, because retrofitting private connectivity is harder than starting with it.

    Keeping the store current

    A vector store that is wrong is worse than one that is empty, because the model will state outdated facts with confidence and cite a real document while doing it. Freshness is a correctness property. Plan for it from the first design, because retrofitting change detection onto a system built around a one-time load is painful.

    Content goes stale in five ways. New documents appear and are not yet indexed. Existing documents change, leaving old chunks that contradict the new text. Documents are deleted but their chunks remain, a problem sometimes called ghost chunks. Permissions change, so a user can still retrieve what they should no longer see. And the model or chunking configuration changes, which invalidates every vector already stored. The first three are handled by synchronization, the fourth by metadata and fast propagation, and the fifth by versioned re-indexing.

    How a Knowledge Base sync works

    You refresh a Knowledge Base by starting an ingestion job against a data source, with StartIngestionJob in the API or the Sync button in the console. The job is incremental. It compares what is in the source with what is already indexed, then embeds and writes only new and modified documents and removes the chunks of documents that no longer exist. Unchanged documents are not re-embedded, so you are not paying embedding costs again for the whole corpus on every run. The job reports statistics you can read through GetIngestionJob: documents scanned, new documents indexed, modified documents indexed, documents deleted, and documents failed.

    Only one ingestion job can run at a time for a given data source, and a new request while one is running returns a conflict error. Your automation must handle that. Querying before the first job completes returns nothing, and unsupported file types or oversized files are skipped, so check the failed count rather than assuming a clean run.

    Triggering syncs

    There are three triggers, and most production systems use more than one.

    • Event-driven. For S3 sources, turn on EventBridge notifications for the bucket. An EventBridge rule matches Object Created and Object Deleted events and starts the sync pipeline. This gives you near-real-time freshness for content that lands in S3.
    • Scheduled. Connectors such as the web crawler, Confluence, and SharePoint have no event to hook, so you poll. An EventBridge Scheduler rule runs a Lambda function on a cron expression, hourly or nightly depending on how fresh the content must be, and the function starts an ingestion job.
    • Source-driven. When a system can notify you of changes, use that. A wiki webhook calls API Gateway and Lambda. A DynamoDB table that tracks your documents emits changes through DynamoDB Streams to a Lambda function. A database can publish change events through change data capture. These let you update only the affected document.

    A raw S3 trigger has a flaw. Uploading a hundred files fires a hundred events, and a hundred concurrent StartIngestionJob calls would collide. Put a queue between the events and the sync. EventBridge sends events to an SQS queue, a Lambda consumer reads batches with a batching window of up to five minutes, and the function starts a single job per batch.

    flowchart LR
    U["Document uploaded or deleted"] --> S3["S3 bucket
    EventBridge notifications on"]
    S3 --> EB["EventBridge rule"]
    EB --> Q["SQS queue
    batching window"]
    Q --> L["Lambda: start sync"]
    L -->|"StartIngestionJob"| KB["Knowledge Base"]
    SCH["EventBridge Scheduler
    cron, for connectors"] --> L
    KB --> VS[("Vector store")]
    L -.->|"failures and sync age"| CW["CloudWatch metrics and alarms"]
    import os
import boto3
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError

agent = boto3.client("bedrock-agent")
KB_ID, DS_ID = os.environ["KB_ID"], os.environ["DS_ID"]

def handler(event, context):
    """SQS-triggered. A burst of S3 events arrives as one batch, so one job covers all of them."""
    try:
        job = agent.start_ingestion_job(knowledgeBaseId=KB_ID, dataSourceId=DS_ID)["ingestionJob"]
    except ClientError as err:
        if err.response["Error"]["Code"] == "ConflictException":
            # A job is already running and may have started before the latest upload.
            # Failing the invocation returns the batch to the queue for a retry.
            raise RuntimeError("Ingestion already running; retry after visibility timeout") from err
        raise
    return {"ingestionJobId": job["ingestionJobId"], "status": job["status"]}

    For sources where scanning a bucket is slow or you need an edit to appear in seconds, a custom data source avoids the scan. You send a document straight to the Knowledge Base with the IngestKnowledgeBaseDocuments API, and you remove one with DeleteKnowledgeBaseDocuments, each identified by an ID you choose. A single ingest call accepts up to 10 documents. The wiki webhook pattern uses this: the page-updated event carries the page ID, Lambda fetches and cleans the content, and one API call replaces the old chunks.

    def upsert_wiki_page(agent, kb_id: str, ds_id: str, page_id: str, text: str, space: str):
    agent.ingest_knowledge_base_documents(
        knowledgeBaseId=kb_id,
        dataSourceId=ds_id,
        documents=[{
            "content": {
                "dataSourceType": "CUSTOM",
                "custom": {
                    "customDocumentIdentifier": {"id": page_id},
                    "sourceType": "IN_LINE",
                    "inlineContent": {"type": "TEXT", "textContent": {"data": text}},
                },
            },
            "metadata": {
                "type": "IN_LINE_ATTRIBUTE",
                "inlineAttributes": [
                    {"key": "space", "value": {"type": "STRING", "stringValue": space}},
                ],
            },
        }],
    )

    Deletes, supersession, and stale chunks

    Deletion is the case teams forget. When someone removes a document from S3, the next sync removes its chunks, but only if a sync runs. A system triggered only by object creation will keep answering from deleted files indefinitely, so subscribe to delete events too. Where a document is replaced by a new version rather than removed, give both versions a superseded or effective_to attribute and filter on it, or overwrite the same document ID so the sync replaces the old chunks. Guard against accidental mass deletion: if a source is emptied by a misconfigured upload job, the next sync will faithfully empty the index. Some configurations offer a deletion safeguard, and in any case alarm on a sudden drop in indexed document count.

    Changing the embedding model or chunking

    This is the change that cannot be done incrementally. A vector store index has a fixed number of dimensions, and vectors from different embedding models are not comparable even when the dimensions happen to match. Moving from one model to another, or from one dimension setting to another, means building a new index and re-embedding everything. A Knowledge Base's chunking configuration is likewise fixed when the data source is created, so changing it means a new data source.

    Treat it as a blue-green deployment. Build the new index alongside the old one, point a copy of your evaluation questions at it, compare retrieval quality, and then switch traffic by flipping an alias or a configuration value. Keep the old index for a short rollback window, and then delete it. Skipping the comparison step is the usual mistake, because a model that looks better on a benchmark can retrieve worse on your content.

    Watching freshness

    Do not trust a sync you never check. Have the sync function publish custom CloudWatch metrics: the age of the last successful sync, documents failed in the last job, and indexed document count. Alarm on failures and on a sync age beyond your freshness target. Bedrock records CreateKnowledgeBase, CreateDataSource, and StartIngestionJob as CloudTrail management events, which gives you an audit trail of who triggered what. Add a small set of canary questions with known answers that you run on a schedule. If a known fact stops being retrievable, you find out from a test instead of from a user. Domain 4 builds this into a full monitoring design.

    Exam tip

    Remember the shape of the standard answer: S3 event to EventBridge to Lambda to StartIngestionJob, with a queue to absorb bursts for large uploads, and EventBridge Scheduler for sources with no events. Remember also that syncs are incremental, that deletes need handling, and that changing the embedding model forces a full rebuild into a new index. A distractor will often suggest "re-run the whole pipeline nightly", which is wasteful when incremental sync exists.

    Hands-on lab

    Watch incremental sync, a delete, and then clean up

    About 20 minutes · Cost: cents · Needs: the Knowledge Base from the first lab

    1. In the S3 console, download vpn-setup.txt, change a sentence (for example, change the VPN client name), and upload it again with the same name.
    2. In Amazon Bedrock → Knowledge Bases → kb-lab, start a Sync on the data source. When it completes, open the sync history and read the statistics. You should see one modified document and the other documents unchanged. That is the incremental behavior.
    3. Ask the test panel about the VPN client and confirm the answer reflects your edit.
    4. In S3, delete incident-runbook.txt and its .metadata.json file. Before syncing, ask a question about a production outage and note the runbook still comes back. This is the ghost chunk problem.
    5. Run Sync again. The statistics should show a deleted document. Ask the same question and confirm the runbook is gone.

    Cleanup, in this order. (1) In Bedrock, delete the Knowledge Base kb-lab. (2) Delete the vector store it used: if it was an S3 vector bucket, delete its index and then the vector bucket in the S3 console; if the wizard created an OpenSearch Serverless collection, delete it in the OpenSearch Service console under Serverless collections, which is the step that stops hourly charges. (3) Empty and delete your document bucket. (4) In IAM, delete the service role the wizard created if you do not plan to reuse it. Afterward, check the Billing console the next day to confirm no vector store charges continue.

    Chapter recap

    • A vector store holds embeddings, chunk text, and metadata, and answers nearest-neighbor queries using an approximate index such as HNSW.
    • AWS options: S3 Vectors for lowest cost with light query load, OpenSearch Serverless for low-ops hybrid search with an always-on minimum, OpenSearch managed for maximum control and the neural plugin, Aurora or RDS with pgvector when data is relational, Neptune Analytics for GraphRAG. DynamoDB holds metadata and state, not vectors.
    • Bedrock Knowledge Bases automate parse, chunk, embed, and store. Customer-managed ones let you choose the store. Managed ones hide it.
    • Metadata lives in sidecar .metadata.json files that Bedrock indexes. S3 tags and object metadata are not filterable until you copy them across. Filters narrow results and can enforce access, if built server-side from verified identity.
    • At scale, tune ef_search at query time, size shard count and replicas deliberately, estimate memory from dimensions and m, and reduce cost with fewer dimensions or quantization. Use separate indexes per domain and two-stage retrieval for large mixed corpora.
    • Connect sources through KB connectors, or through AppFlow, DataSync, Transfer Family, and Lambda behind API Gateway. Kendra and Q Business are buy-not-build alternatives.
    • Syncs are incremental, one job runs at a time per data source, and deletes need an explicit trigger. Use events, schedules, and queues, monitor freshness, and treat embedding model changes as a full re-index into a new index.

    Knowledge check

    1. A retailer keeps product, order, and customer data in Aurora PostgreSQL. They want a support assistant that retrieves product manuals and can restrict results by product category and by whether the customer owns the product. They want to avoid introducing and operating a new database. Which design is best?

      Answer

      Use the existing Aurora PostgreSQL cluster with the pgvector extension to hold manual embeddings alongside the relational tables, and query by vector distance with SQL conditions on category and ownership. Bedrock Knowledge Bases can also use Aurora through the RDS Data API. OpenSearch or a third-party store would add a new system to operate, and DynamoDB cannot perform vector search.

    2. A media company must hold 200 million embeddings of archived transcripts. Queries are infrequent, a few hundred per day, and latency of a second or so is acceptable. The team has no capacity to manage clusters and wants the lowest cost. Which store fits best?

      Answer

      Amazon S3 Vectors. It is serverless, scales to large vector counts, and has a pay-per-use cost shape suited to infrequent queries. OpenSearch Serverless has an always-on minimum cost and is overbuilt for this query rate. A managed OpenSearch domain would need memory for the whole graph and cluster operations. MemoryDB is built for very low latency, which is not required here and would be costly at this size.

    3. Documents in an S3 bucket are tagged with department and classification object tags. A shared Knowledge Base must return only documents from the caller's department, and the filter must not be bypassable by a user editing requests. What should the team do?

      Answer

      Generate .metadata.json sidecar files that copy the S3 tags into metadata attributes, for example with a Lambda function triggered on upload, then sync. At query time, build the retrieval filter on the server from the caller's verified identity claims and pass it in the Retrieve call. Relying on S3 tags alone fails because the Knowledge Base does not filter on them. A client-supplied filter is bypassable. A prompt instruction or a guardrail does not enforce document access.

    4. An OpenSearch managed domain serves 10 million 1,024-dimension vectors. The cluster is memory-bound and cost is too high, but recall is acceptable and the team wants to cut cost with minimal application change. Which actions help most?

      Answer

      Reduce the memory per vector: choose a smaller embedding dimension where accuracy allows, or apply quantization or disk-backed vector search, accepting a small recall loss. These reduce the in-memory graph size directly. Adding shards or replicas increases total memory rather than lowering it. Raising ef_search increases latency and does nothing for memory. Switching to a larger foundation model is unrelated to retrieval memory.

    5. Employees upload policy files to S3 throughout the day, sometimes hundreds at once during a quarterly review. Answers must reflect new files within about ten minutes. Which architecture meets this with the least waste?

      Answer

      Enable EventBridge notifications on the bucket, route object created and deleted events through an EventBridge rule to an SQS queue, and have a Lambda function consume batches with a batching window and call StartIngestionJob once per batch. The queue prevents hundreds of concurrent job requests, and the incremental sync embeds only changed files. A nightly full rebuild misses the ten-minute target and wastes embedding cost, and starting one job per event causes conflict errors.

    6. A team wants to move from Titan Text Embeddings V1 to V2 because a benchmark shows better results. They plan to switch the model setting on the existing index and let new documents use V2. What is wrong with this plan, and what should they do?

      Answer

      Vectors from different models are not comparable, and the index has a fixed dimension count, so mixing them breaks retrieval or fails at write time. They must build a new index, or a new Knowledge Base, with the new model, re-embed the whole corpus, compare retrieval quality against the old index using their own evaluation questions, then switch traffic with an alias or configuration change and keep the old index briefly for rollback.

    Chapter 5 · Task 1.5

    Retrieval Mechanisms for FM Augmentation

    A foundation model only knows what was in its training data and what you put in its prompt. Retrieval is the machinery that decides which of your documents make it into that prompt. This chapter walks through every stage of that machinery on AWS: how documents are cut up, turned into vectors, searched, re-ordered, and finally handed to a model.

    Exam skills covered

    • 1.5.1 Document segmentation approaches for retrieval (Bedrock chunking, Lambda fixed-size chunking, hierarchical chunking)
    • 1.5.2 Selecting and configuring embedding solutions (Titan embeddings, dimensionality, batch generation)
    • 1.5.3 Deploying vector search (OpenSearch Service, Aurora pgvector, Bedrock Knowledge Bases)
    • 1.5.4 Advanced search architectures (hybrid search, reranker models)
    • 1.5.5 Query handling (expansion, decomposition, transformation)
    • 1.5.6 Consistent access mechanisms (function calling, MCP, standardized retrieval APIs)

    The retrieval pipeline in one picture

    You already know the shape of retrieval augmented generation: embed documents, store the vectors, embed the question, find the nearest vectors, and paste the matching text into the prompt. If you have built this with LangChain and Chroma, nothing about the idea changes on AWS. What changes is that every box in the pipeline maps to a named AWS service or a named Bedrock setting, and the exam asks you to choose among them under constraints like cost, latency, operational effort, and compliance.

    It helps to see the pipeline as two separate paths that meet in the vector store. The ingestion path runs offline, whenever documents change. The query path runs online, once per user question, and is the one users feel as latency.

    flowchart LR
  subgraph Ingestion["Ingestion path (offline)"]
    S["Source documents: S3, wiki, web"] --> P["Parse"]
    P --> C["Chunk"]
    C --> E["Embed with an embedding model"]
    E --> V[("Vector store")]
  end
  subgraph Query["Query path (online)"]
    Q["User question"] --> R["Rewrite or expand"]
    R --> QE["Embed the query"]
    QE --> SR["Search: vector plus keyword"]
    SR --> RR["Rerank"]
    RR --> G["Generate answer with a foundation model"]
  end
  V --> SR

    The six skills in this task line up with the boxes. Skill 1.5.1 is the chunk box, 1.5.2 is the embed box, 1.5.3 is the vector store and the search that runs against it, 1.5.4 is the search and rerank boxes taken further, 1.5.5 is everything that happens to the question before it is embedded, and 1.5.6 is the interface that lets an application or an agent trigger all of this without knowing the details. Chapter 4 covered where vectors live and how metadata and freshness are managed. This chapter covers what goes in, how it is found, and how it is exposed.

    Backend analogy

    Think of the ingestion path as building a search index in a nightly ETL job, and the query path as the read endpoint that serves it. Chunking is the same decision you make when you choose the row granularity of a table: too coarse and every query returns a lot of irrelevant data, too fine and you lose the context that makes a row meaningful.

    Two flavors of Bedrock Knowledge Base

    Bedrock Knowledge Bases now come in two forms. A customer-managed knowledge base is the original design: you pick the vector store, the chunking strategy, and the embedding model, and Bedrock runs the ingestion and query plumbing for you. A managed knowledge base is a newer option where AWS also owns storage, parsing, chunking, and retrieval, and you provision nothing. This chapter teaches the underlying components, which apply to both and to hand-built pipelines. The exam guide's skill list (chunking strategies, choosing a vector store, OpenSearch, pgvector) is written in terms of the customer-managed model, so that is the one the examples follow. Where the two differ in an API, the text says so.

    Document segmentation: choosing how to chunk (1.5.1)

    An embedding model turns a piece of text into a single vector. That vector is a summary of everything in the piece, so the size of the piece determines what the vector can say. Chunking is the act of choosing those pieces, and it is the single decision that most affects retrieval quality, which is why the exam spends a skill on it.

    Why chunking exists at all

    There are three practical reasons. First, embedding models have an input limit, so a 200-page PDF cannot be embedded as one unit. Second, a vector for a whole chapter averages many topics together and ends up close to nothing in particular, so searches for a specific fact miss it. Third, whatever you retrieve you also pay to put into the model prompt, in tokens and in latency, so retrieving a whole chapter to answer a one-line question wastes money on every request.

    That leads to the central tension. Small chunks give precise matches and cheap prompts but strip away surrounding context, so a retrieved sentence like "It must be renewed annually" no longer says what "it" is. Large chunks keep context but dilute the vector and inflate the prompt. Every chunking strategy on Bedrock is a different way of managing this tension.

    Chunk size and overlap

    Chunk size is measured in tokens. A token is roughly three quarters of an English word, so a 300-token chunk is about 225 words, or half a printed page. Overlap repeats the tail of one chunk at the start of the next so that an idea straddling a boundary appears whole in at least one chunk. Bedrock expresses overlap as a percentage of the chunk size for fixed-size chunking.

    Overlap has a cost that is easy to underestimate. With a chunk size of s and an overlap fraction of o, each new chunk advances by s × (1 − o) tokens. A 20 percent overlap therefore produces about 25 percent more chunks than no overlap, which means about 25 percent more embedding calls, 25 percent more vectors to store, and more near-duplicate results competing for your top-k slots. A common starting point is 200 to 400 tokens with 10 to 20 percent overlap for prose, and you tune from there using retrieval tests rather than intuition.

    ChoiceRecall of relevant factsPrecision of retrieved textCost and latency
    Smaller chunksHigher for narrow facts, lower for ideas that span paragraphsHigh, little noise per chunkMore vectors to store and search, cheaper prompts
    Larger chunksHigher for broad questions, vector is a blurrier summaryLower, each chunk carries unrelated textFewer vectors, larger prompts
    More overlapFewer ideas lost at boundariesLower, near-duplicate resultsMore embeddings and storage
    No overlapBoundary cuts can split a factHigherCheapest to build

    The built-in strategies in Bedrock Knowledge Bases

    When you create a data source for a customer-managed knowledge base, you choose one chunking strategy. Bedrock offers five options, and you should be able to say for each one what it does and what it is best at.

    Default chunking. If you choose nothing, Bedrock splits each document into chunks of roughly 300 tokens and tries to respect sentence boundaries so that it never cuts a sentence in half. It is a sensible baseline and a good way to get a first answer quickly, but it knows nothing about your document structure.

    Fixed-size chunking. You set the maximum tokens per chunk and the overlap percentage. In the API this is FIXED_SIZE with maxTokens and overlapPercentage. It is predictable and cheap, and it suits uniform content such as FAQs, short articles, and support tickets where one chunk naturally holds one idea.

    Hierarchical chunking. Bedrock creates two levels of chunks from each document. Small child chunks are what gets embedded and searched, and larger parent chunks hold the surrounding text. At query time the search matches on the precise child, and Bedrock then returns the parent, so the model receives the matched sentence together with its context. You configure the maximum tokens for each level and an overlap in tokens between chunks. Commonly cited starting values are 1,500 tokens for the parent, 300 for the child, and 60 tokens of overlap. This is the strategy to reach for with structured, long documents such as manuals, contracts, and policies, because it resolves the precision versus context tension directly. Two side effects matter on the exam and in practice. Several child hits can map to the same parent, so you may get fewer distinct results than the number you asked for. And because parents are bigger, the text you pass to the model is bigger than a child-only setup.

    flowchart TB
  D["Document"] --> P1["Parent chunk 1 (about 1500 tokens)"]
  D --> P2["Parent chunk 2 (about 1500 tokens)"]
  P1 --> C1["Child A (300)"]
  P1 --> C2["Child B (300)"]
  P2 --> C3["Child C (300)"]
  P2 --> C4["Child D (300)"]
  Q["Query matches Child B"] -.->|"search is on children"| C2
  C2 -.->|"Bedrock returns the parent"| P1

    Semantic chunking. Instead of cutting at a fixed length, Bedrock embeds the sentences and starts a new chunk where the meaning shifts. You configure a maximum token size, a buffer size (how many neighboring sentences are considered together when judging similarity, 0 or 1), and a breakpoint percentile threshold (how large a drop in similarity counts as a topic change, with 95 as a typical default). The chunks follow the ideas in the text, which helps with long-form prose that wanders between topics. The price is slower ingestion and an extra charge, because a foundation model is invoked during chunking.

    No chunking. With NONE, each file is treated as one chunk. This is the right choice only when your files are already the unit you want to retrieve, for example one short product description per file, or when you plan to do all the splitting yourself in a custom step.

    Watch out

    The chunking strategy is fixed when you create the data source. To change it you create a new data source (or a new knowledge base) and re-ingest everything, which means paying for parsing and embedding again. Test your strategy on a small sample of documents before you ingest the full corpus.

    StrategyHow it splitsBest forMain trade-off
    DefaultAbout 300 tokens, sentence-awareFirst prototypeIgnores document structure
    Fixed-sizeMax tokens plus overlap percentageFAQs, short and uniform documentsMay cut through a semantic unit
    HierarchicalChild chunks searched, parent chunks returnedManuals, contracts, long structured documentsLarger prompts, possibly fewer distinct results
    SemanticSplits where meaning changesLong-form prose with shifting topicsSlower, extra model cost during ingestion
    NoneOne chunk per filePre-chunked or very short filesYou own all splitting decisions

    Exam tip

    Match the document to the strategy. Structured, long documents where an answer needs surrounding context point to hierarchical. Uniform short records point to fixed-size. Prose that changes topic mid-document points to semantic, and the catch in the answer is the added ingestion cost. If a question says the content is already split into the right units, the answer is no chunking. A distractor that appears often is "increase the chunk size to improve precision", which is backwards, since larger chunks lower precision.

    Custom chunking with a Lambda transformation

    The built-in strategies cover most documents, but sometimes you need logic they cannot express. You might split legal text on clause numbers, keep every table row with its header, strip boilerplate headers and footers, or attach extra metadata to each chunk. Knowledge Bases supports this through a custom transformation: you give the data source an AWS Lambda function and an intermediate S3 location, and Bedrock calls your function during ingestion.

    The flow works like this. Bedrock reads the source file, parses it to text, and writes the result to the intermediate S3 location. It then invokes your Lambda function with a description of where the files are. Your function reads them, splits them however it likes, writes the new chunks back to S3, and returns pointers to the output. Bedrock then embeds your chunks and stores them. You can run your function after Bedrock's own chunking (to post-process chunks, for example to add metadata) or you can set the chunking strategy to NONE and let your function do all the splitting. In the API this is a customTransformationConfiguration with an intermediateStorage S3 location and a transformation whose stepToApply is POST_CHUNKING. Two details are easy to miss: chunk-level metadata that your function returns overrides file-level metadata when the keys collide, and web connectors hand your function markdown rather than HTML.

    flowchart LR
  S3A["Source file in S3"] --> PARSE["Bedrock parses to text"]
  PARSE --> INT1["Intermediate S3 location"]
  INT1 --> LAM["Your Lambda: custom chunker"]
  LAM --> INT2["Chunks written back to S3"]
  INT2 --> EMB["Bedrock embeds chunks"]
  EMB --> VS[("Vector store")]

    Here is a sketch of a fixed-size chunker with overlap. It splits on characters as a stand-in for tokens, at roughly four characters per token for English, which is fine for a sketch and is the kind of approximation you would replace with a real tokenizer in production. The event and output field names below follow the documented Knowledge Bases custom transformation contract: the function receives bucketName and inputFiles, each file carries contentBatches that point to intermediate S3 objects, those objects hold a fileContents list, and the function returns outputFiles in the same shape.

    import json
import boto3

s3 = boto3.client("s3")

CHUNK_CHARS = 1200   # about 300 tokens
OVERLAP_CHARS = 200  # about 50 tokens


def split(text: str) -> list[str]:
    step = CHUNK_CHARS - OVERLAP_CHARS
    pieces = (text[i:i + CHUNK_CHARS] for i in range(0, len(text), step))
    return [p for p in pieces if p.strip()]


def lambda_handler(event, context):
    bucket = event["bucketName"]
    output_files = []

    for input_file in event["inputFiles"]:
        out_batches = []
        for batch in input_file["contentBatches"]:
            body = json.loads(
                s3.get_object(Bucket=bucket, Key=batch["key"])["Body"].read()
            )
            chunks = []
            for doc in body["fileContents"]:
                for piece in split(doc["contentBody"]):
                    chunks.append({
                        "contentType": "TEXT",
                        "contentBody": piece,
                        "contentMetadata": doc.get("contentMetadata", {}),
                    })

            out_key = batch["key"] + ".chunks"
            s3.put_object(
                Bucket=bucket,
                Key=out_key,
                Body=json.dumps({"fileContents": chunks}).encode("utf-8"),
            )
            out_batches.append({"key": out_key})

        output_files.append({
            "originalFileLocation": input_file["originalFileLocation"],
            "fileMetadata": input_file.get("fileMetadata", {}),
            "contentBatches": out_batches,
        })

    return {"outputFiles": output_files}

    The function needs permission to read and write the intermediate bucket, and the knowledge base service role needs permission to invoke the function. Because the function runs once per ingestion batch rather than once per query, its performance rarely matters, but its correctness does: a bug here silently degrades every chunk in the index.

    Exam tip

    The wording "Lambda functions to implement fixed-size chunking" or "custom processing for hierarchical chunking based on content structure" in the exam guide points at this custom transformation feature and at Lambda-based chunking in a hand-built pipeline. If a question needs chunk boundaries that follow something Bedrock cannot see, such as clause numbers or HTML sections, or needs per-chunk metadata computed during ingestion, the answer is a Lambda custom transformation, not a different built-in strategy.

    Structure-aware chunking

    Real documents are not flat streams of text. They have headings, lists, tables, code blocks, and page furniture, and a chunker that ignores them produces chunks that mislead the embedding model and the language model alike. Three cases come up repeatedly.

    Headings. Text under a heading is only meaningful together with the heading. A paragraph that says "The limit is 25 per account" is useless without the heading "Concurrent export jobs". Good structure-aware chunking splits at heading boundaries and prepends the heading path (for example "Billing > Exports > Concurrent export jobs") to each chunk, so the vector and the model both see where the text came from. Hierarchical chunking gets some of this benefit through parent chunks, but a custom splitter can use real headings rather than token counts.

    Tables. A table cut in the middle is worse than no table, because the rows lose their column headers and numbers become meaningless. Either keep a table in one chunk, or repeat the header row in every chunk that contains part of it, or convert rows into sentences ("The Pro plan costs 40 dollars per month and includes 5 seats"). Standard text extraction flattens tables badly, so for table-heavy PDFs you should turn on advanced parsing, described next.

    Code and structured text. Splitting source code mid-function or JSON mid-object destroys meaning. Split on function, class, or object boundaries, and keep the file path in each chunk's text or metadata.

    Parsing comes before chunking

    Chunking operates on text, so something has to turn a PDF, a scanned page, or a slide deck into text first. In a customer-managed knowledge base you choose a parsing strategy on the data source. The default parser extracts text only and is fast and free of model charges. Two alternatives hand parsing to a model for richer output. The foundation model parser uses a multimodal model you select to read each page, which preserves tables, figures, and layout and produces markdown-like text, at the cost of model invocations per page. Bedrock Data Automation is a managed document-processing option for complex documents. Use the default parser for clean text, and an FM-based parser when documents contain tables, charts, or scanned content where the default parser would drop information.

    Watch out

    FM-based parsing and semantic chunking both call a model for every document, so a large corpus can turn a cheap ingestion into an expensive one. Estimate the cost on a sample first, and note that Task 1.3 covers the alternative of preprocessing documents yourself with services such as Amazon Textract before they reach the knowledge base.

    Choosing a strategy in practice

    A reliable order of operations is to start with the default or fixed-size chunking at about 300 tokens, build a small set of real questions with known answers, and measure whether the right chunk comes back. If answers are correct but missing context, move to hierarchical. If answers are split across chunk boundaries, add overlap or switch to semantic. If tables are garbled, fix parsing before touching chunking, because no chunking strategy repairs text that was extracted badly. Chunking is an empirical choice, and the exam rewards the candidate who proposes measuring it, which Domain 5 develops in detail.

    Embedding models: turning chunks into vectors (1.5.2)

    An embedding model maps text to a list of numbers, a vector, positioned so that texts with similar meaning land close together. You know this part. What the exam asks is which embedding model fits a workload, what the dimensionality setting costs and buys, and how to generate embeddings at volume.

    The embedding models on Bedrock

    Embedding models are listed in Bedrock like any other model. You can see them with aws bedrock list-foundation-models --by-output-modality EMBEDDING, and as with generation models you must have access to the model in your Region before you can call it. The families you are expected to know are these.

    Model familyModel ID (typical)Output dimensionsNotes
    Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:01024 (default), 512, or 256The default for Knowledge Bases. Roughly 8K tokens of input, 100+ languages, optional output normalization, optional binary vectors.
    Amazon Titan Embeddings G1 Textamazon.titan-embed-text-v11536The earlier generation. Fixed dimension, no configurable size.
    Amazon Titan Multimodal Embeddings G1amazon.titan-embed-image-v11024, 384, or 256Embeds text and images into the same vector space, so a text query can find images and the reverse.
    Cohere Embed v3 (English, Multilingual)cohere.embed-english-v3, cohere.embed-multilingual-v31024Takes an input_type that distinguishes documents from queries. Strong multilingual retrieval. Accepts batches of texts per call.
    Newer multimodal embedding modelsList them with aws bedrock list-foundation-models --by-output-modality EMBEDDINGVariesNewer Cohere and Amazon models add multimodal and longer-context options. Confirm availability in your Region.

    The details most likely to matter in a question are these. Titan V2 is the default and the safe answer when a question gives no reason to choose otherwise. Cohere is the better answer when the corpus is heavily multilingual or when you need the document versus query distinction. A multimodal embedding model is the answer when a single search must span text and images.

    Dimensionality: what you trade

    Titan V2 lets you choose 1024, 512, or 256 dimensions for the vectors it returns. Lower-dimension vectors are less expressive, so retrieval accuracy drops slightly, but they are much cheaper to run at scale. The arithmetic is simple and worth memorizing. Each dimension of a float vector takes 4 bytes, so a 1024-dimension vector is 4 KB. One million chunks at 1024 dimensions is about 4 GB of raw vector data, and the same corpus at 256 dimensions is about 1 GB. The search index adds its own overhead on top, since approximate nearest neighbor structures such as HNSW graphs keep extra links in memory. Fewer dimensions also mean faster distance calculations and lower query latency.

    Titan V2 can also return binary vectors, which store one bit per dimension. A 1024-dimension binary vector is 128 bytes, a 32x reduction from float. This trades more accuracy for much lower storage and is only worth it when the vector store supports binary types and your evaluation shows acceptable quality. S3 Vectors, for example, accepts floating-point vectors only.

    The right way to decide is empirical. Embed a sample at two or three dimension settings, run your test questions, and look at recall. A frequent finding is that 512 or even 256 dimensions are nearly as good as 1024 for a domain-specific corpus, and the saving compounds across millions of vectors.

    Watch out

    The vector field in your index has a fixed dimension, and it must equal the embedding model's output dimension exactly. A mismatch makes ingestion fail. If you create an OpenSearch index mapping with 1024 dimensions and later switch Titan to 512, every write is rejected.

    Normalization and similarity

    Titan V2 has a normalize option, on by default, that scales each vector to length 1. This matters because of how similarity is computed. For unit-length vectors, cosine similarity and the dot product give identical rankings, so you can use the cheaper dot product. If you leave vectors unnormalized, a long or repetitive chunk can end up with a larger magnitude and win dot-product comparisons unfairly. The safe rule is to normalize, or to use cosine similarity in the index. Section 1.5.3 returns to the similarity metrics.

    Documents and queries are not always symmetric

    A question and the passage that answers it rarely look alike. "How do I rotate my API key?" shares few words with the paragraph describing key rotation. Some embedding models are trained to handle this asymmetry. Cohere embedding models take an input_type of search_document when you embed chunks and search_query when you embed the user's question, and they produce slightly different vectors for each so that questions land near their answers. Titan embeds both the same way. The rule that follows: whichever model you pick, embed documents and queries with the same model, and with Cohere always set the correct input_type on each side.

    Calling an embedding model with Boto3

    Embedding models use the InvokeModel API, not Converse, because there is no conversation. Each provider has its own request body, which is the one place where you must read the model's documentation rather than assume.

    import json
import boto3

bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime")  # use your project's selected Region


def embed_titan(text: str, dims: int = 512) -> list[float]:
    resp = bedrock.invoke_model(
        modelId="amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0",
        body=json.dumps({
            "inputText": text,
            "dimensions": dims,      # 1024, 512, or 256
            "normalize": True,
        }),
        contentType="application/json",
        accept="application/json",
    )
    return json.loads(resp["body"].read())["embedding"]


def embed_cohere(texts: list[str], is_query: bool) -> list[list[float]]:
    resp = bedrock.invoke_model(
        modelId="cohere.embed-english-v3",
        body=json.dumps({
            "texts": texts,                      # a batch per call
            "input_type": "search_query" if is_query else "search_document",
            "truncate": "END",
        }),
    )
    return json.loads(resp["body"].read())["embeddings"]

    Notice that Titan embeds one string per call, while Cohere accepts a list of texts. For a large corpus, that batching difference changes how you build the loader.

    Generating embeddings at scale

    When you use a Knowledge Base, ingestion embeds the chunks for you and you never write the loop above. When you build your own pipeline, for example because you store vectors in a system the Knowledge Base does not support or you need custom preprocessing, you have to generate embeddings yourself.
