Prompt it to return structured JSON: the detected intent, the slots it found (for a refund: order ID, reason), and the list of missing slots. A Choice state then branches on the missing list. Bound the loop with a counter in the state machine so a confused user cannot cycle forever, and after two rounds hand the conversation to a human or a fallback answer. That bound is a recurring exam theme: any agentic or iterative design needs a stopping condition.

    Note

    Step Functions has two workflow types. Standard workflows run up to a year, support the callback pattern, and are billed per state transition. Express workflows are short (up to five minutes), high-volume, and cheaper, but do not support the wait-for-callback pattern. A human-in-the-loop clarification needs Standard.

    Intent recognition with Amazon Comprehend

    The exam guide names Amazon Comprehend for intent recognition. Comprehend is AWS's managed natural language processing service. It detects entities, key phrases, sentiment, language, and PII out of the box, and it supports custom classification, where you train a classifier on your own labeled examples and then call it to label new text. For intent recognition you would train a custom classifier with labels like refund, order_status, cancel, and route on the label. Compared with asking a foundation model to classify, a Comprehend classifier is cheaper per call, faster, deterministic, and predictable in cost, but it needs labeled training data and retraining when intents change. A foundation model needs no training data and handles new intents immediately, but costs more per request and can vary.

    A third service often appears next to these in scenarios: Amazon Lex, the managed conversational interface service (the engine behind Alexa-style bots) that has first-class intents, slots, and multi-turn slot-filling prompts. When a question describes a structured, form-like dialogue with defined intents and slots, Lex is a valid answer. When it describes free-form natural language that needs generative answers, a foundation model with Step Functions is the stronger one.

    Session design checklist

    • Generate a session_id server-side and tie it to the authenticated user, so one user cannot read another's history by guessing an ID.
    • Store one turn per item with a TTL, and decide up front how long conversations may be retained for compliance.
    • Cap the history you send, and log the token usage returned in each Converse response so you can see the cost curve.
    • Keep the system prompt identical across turns so it stays a cacheable prefix.
    • Define a fallback when clarification does not converge: a human handoff or a safe default answer.

    Exam tip

    Pick by the sentence in the question. "Preserve conversation context across requests" is DynamoDB with a session key. "Automatically delete old conversations" is DynamoDB TTL. "Pause until the user responds" is a Step Functions callback with a task token. "Classify user intent cheaply at high volume" is a Comprehend custom classifier. "Reduce tokens in a very long chat" is summarization or a sliding window, not a larger model.

    Prompt management and governance (skill 1.6.3)

    Governance means being able to answer four questions about any prompt in production. What exactly is it? Who changed it, and when? Who approved the change? Which applications used it? If your prompts live as string constants in Git, you can answer the first three from Git history, but only for developers, and the fourth not at all. AWS gives you purpose-built pieces for this, and the exam wants you to know which piece answers which question.

    Amazon Bedrock Prompt Management

    Prompt Management is a Bedrock feature that stores prompts as first-class resources with an ARN, outside your application code. Its core ideas are these.

    • Prompt. A named resource holding one or more variants. It is created and edited through the Bedrock console or the bedrock-agent API (control plane). A variant has a template type of TEXT (one block of text) or CHAT (system prompt plus user and assistant messages). Choose CHAT when the model supports Converse and you want a system prompt, tools, or prompt caching in the stored prompt.
    • Variables. Placeholders written with double curly braces, such as {{customer_question}}. The values arrive at invocation time. This is the "parameterized template" the exam guide mentions.
    • Variant. A complete alternative configuration of the prompt: its own template text, its own model, and its own inference parameters such as temperature and max tokens. Variants exist so you can compare approaches side by side, for example the same instructions on two different models, or two different wordings on one model. The console has a Compare variants mode that lets you add up to three variants and run them against the same test values, which is the systematic A/B testing referred to in Task 3.4. Be aware that leaving compare mode with Save as draft keeps only the variant you save and deletes the others. Through the API, a single prompt can store several variants, with one named as the default.
    • Draft. The editable working copy. Every prompt always has one.
    • Version. An immutable snapshot of the draft. Once you create version 1 it can never change. Edits go into the draft, and you cut version 2 when ready. Versions are what make rollback and audit meaningful, because "production uses version 3" is a fixed, reviewable statement.

    The console also offers a prompt optimization action that rewrites your prompt for a chosen target model. Treat its output as a starting point to review, not as something to promote blindly.

    Invoking a managed prompt

    The payoff is at runtime. Instead of embedding the template, your application passes the prompt's ARN to Converse in place of a model ID, plus the variable values. The model, system text, and inference settings come from the stored prompt. The ARN with a version suffix, such as ...:prompt/ABCDEFGHIJ:3, pins to that exact immutable version. The ARN without a suffix resolves to the draft, which is convenient in development and dangerous in production.

    import boto3

runtime = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime")

PROMPT_ARN = "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:111122223333:prompt/ABCDEFGHIJ:3"  # pinned to version 3

resp = runtime.converse(
    modelId=PROMPT_ARN,                    # the prompt ARN replaces the model ID
    promptVariables={
        "customer_question": {"text": "Why was I charged twice this month?"},
        "policy_excerpts":   {"text": policy_text},
    },
    # Do not pass system, inferenceConfig, or toolConfig here.
    # They are defined inside the managed prompt.
)
print(resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"])

    Two details trip people up. When you use a managed prompt, you must not also send system, inferenceConfig, toolConfig, or additionalModelRequestFields, because they are already part of the prompt. And any messages you send are appended after the messages stored in the prompt, which is how a chat prompt continues with a live user turn. A guardrailConfig is still allowed and applies to the whole prompt plus the appended messages, so a guardrail stays under the caller's control even when the prompt is managed. The variable values go in promptVariables, each wrapped as {"text": "..."}.

    Creating and versioning the prompt through code is symmetrical, using the bedrock-agent client. This sketch matches the current Boto3 API model. create_prompt creates the editable DRAFT and returns the prompt's id and arn. create_prompt_version returns a new arn that ends in the version number.

    import boto3

agent = boto3.client("bedrock-agent")

created = agent.create_prompt(
    name="billing-answer",
    description="Answers billing questions from policy excerpts",
    defaultVariant="concise",
    variants=[{
        "name": "concise",
        "templateType": "TEXT",
        "modelId": "us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6",
        "templateConfiguration": {"text": {
            "text": "Answer using only the policy.\n<policy>{{policy_excerpts}}</policy>\n"
                    "Question: {{customer_question}}",
            "inputVariables": [{"name": "policy_excerpts"}, {"name": "customer_question"}],
        }},
        "inferenceConfiguration": {"text": {"temperature": 0.2, "maxTokens": 512}},
    }],
)

version = agent.create_prompt_version(promptIdentifier=created["id"],
                                      description="Reviewed by support lead")
print(version["arn"])        # ARN ending in :1, immutable

    The lifecycle: draft, review, version, promote

    Prompt Management gives you the versioning primitive. The approval workflow around it is yours to build, and the exam guide lists the usual ingredients: parameterized templates and approval workflows, an S3 template repository, CloudTrail, and CloudWatch Logs. A common design looks like this.

    flowchart LR
    A["Author edits DRAFT in console or via IaC"] --> B["Automated tests (Lambda and Step Functions)"]
    B --> C{"Tests pass?"}
    C -- "No" --> A
    C -- "Yes" --> D["Human approval (Step Functions callback or pipeline gate)"]
    D --> E["create_prompt_version (immutable)"]
    E --> F["Update app config to pin the new version ARN"]
    F --> G["Production traffic"]
    G --> H["CloudTrail and invocation logs feed audit and monitoring"]

    The approval step can be a manual approval action in CodePipeline, or a Step Functions state that sends an email through SNS and waits on a task token until a reviewer approves. The pinning step is where this connects to AWS AppConfig from Chapter 2: store the production prompt ARN in AppConfig so you can roll forward or back by changing a configuration value, with no code deployment.

    S3 and infrastructure as code as the template repository

    Not every organization uses Prompt Management for everything. Some keep templates as files in an Amazon S3 bucket or in a Git repository that deploys through infrastructure as code, and load them at runtime. S3 gives you cheap, durable storage, object versioning (every overwrite keeps the old version, so you can restore), encryption with KMS, and bucket policies for access control. The trade-off against Prompt Management is that you build the variable substitution, the side-by-side comparison, and the console editing experience yourself. A sensible rule: choose Prompt Management when non-engineers edit prompts or you want variants and managed versions; choose S3 or Git when prompts must travel with code through a strict CI/CD pipeline or when you need formats the managed feature does not model.

    Exam tip

    Prompt Management versions are immutable, and production should reference a version ARN, not the draft. If an answer option says "reference the DRAFT in production so changes go live immediately," it is the distractor. If the requirement is "roll back a bad prompt change quickly," the answer is re-pointing to the previous version.

    Access control: who may edit, who may use

    IAM (Identity and Access Management) is AWS's permission system. Every API call is allowed or denied by policies attached to the caller's role. Prompt Management actions are separate IAM actions, so you can split duties cleanly. The management actions are bedrock:CreatePrompt, UpdatePrompt, GetPrompt, ListPrompts, DeletePrompt, CreatePromptVersion, and OptimizePrompt. Authors get the create and update actions. The approver role is the only one allowed to call CreatePromptVersion. The application's runtime role gets none of them. To invoke a managed prompt through Converse, the runtime role needs bedrock:RenderPrompt, which is a permission-only action that authorizes using a stored prompt for inference, plus bedrock:InvokeModel (and InvokeModelWithResponseStream for ConverseStream) on the model. If the prompt uses an inference profile, add bedrock:GetInferenceProfile. Scope each statement to specific ARNs rather than bedrock:*. This is the least-privilege principle applied to prompts, and it means the service that answers customers cannot edit the prompt that controls it.

    Audit: CloudTrail and CloudWatch Logs

    Two logging services answer different questions, and the exam likes to swap them in distractors.

    • AWS CloudTrail records API activity: which principal called which API, from where, and when. It answers "who created version 4 of the billing prompt?" and "who changed this IAM policy?" Prompt creation, update, and version actions are management events, so they appear in the CloudTrail event history by default. Calls to InvokeModel, Converse, and their streaming variants are also logged as management events by default, which tells you who called which model and when, but not what text was sent. Using a stored prompt is different: RenderPrompt is recorded only as a data event, so to track which principals used which approved template you must configure advanced event selectors for the AWS::Bedrock::Prompt resource type. Data events are off by default and carry an extra charge. For a durable, queryable record beyond the default 90-day event history, create a trail that delivers events to S3.
    • Amazon CloudWatch Logs holds application and service logs. For Bedrock, model invocation logging is an account-level setting that writes the full request and response of model calls to CloudWatch Logs, S3, or both. It answers "what text did we actually send and receive for this request?" Both destinations are opt-in, so enabling it is part of your governance setup. It also captures whatever is in the prompts, including personal data, so encrypt the log group with KMS, restrict access, and set a retention period.

    Together, CloudTrail covers changes to the prompt assets and CloudWatch Logs covers the use of them. A compliance scenario that asks to "track usage of approved templates" and "log access" is asking for both: CloudTrail for the asset lifecycle and CloudWatch Logs for runtime access.

    Exam tip

    Know which Bedrock calls are data events, because those need advanced event selectors and are not logged by default: RenderPrompt (AWS::Bedrock::Prompt), InvokeFlow (AWS::Bedrock::FlowAlias), Retrieve and RetrieveAndGenerate (AWS::Bedrock::KnowledgeBase), InvokeAgent (AWS::Bedrock::AgentAlias), and ApplyGuardrail (AWS::Bedrock::Guardrail). Plain InvokeModel and Converse are management events. For the actual prompt and response text, CloudTrail never helps. That is model invocation logging.

    Hands-on lab

    Create, compare, and version a parameterized prompt

    About 20 minutes · Cost: a few cents of model tokens, Prompt Management itself has no charge · Needs: access to at least one text model in your selected Region

    1. Confirm you are in your project's assigned Region (top-right Region selector). Open the Amazon Bedrock console, select Prompt management in the left navigation pane, then choose Create prompt.
    2. Enter the name billing-answer, leave encryption at the default AWS managed key, and choose Create prompt. You land in the Prompt builder.
    3. In the Prompt pane, enter the template below in the last User message box. Each double-brace name you type appears in the Test variables section. 
      You are a billing support assistant. Answer only from the policy below.
If the policy does not cover the question, say "I don't have that information."

<policy>
{{policy_excerpts}}
</policy>

Question: {{customer_question}}
    4. In the Configurations pane, choose a small, inexpensive text model as the Generative AI resource. Under Inference parameters, set Temperature to 0.2 and Maximum tokens to 300.
    5. In Test variables, enter a short policy (for example, "Refunds are available within 14 days of purchase.") and the question "Can I get a refund after 30 days?". Choose Run and read the answer.
    6. Choose Compare variants, then the plus sign to add a second variant. Change its wording to demand a one-sentence answer, optionally pick a different model, then run both variants against the same test values and compare the outputs side by side. When you have a winner, choose Save as draft on it. The other variants are deleted, so this is an experiment tool, not storage.
    7. Choose Save draft, then Create version. Note the version number (1) and copy the prompt ARN. The version is now immutable.
    8. Edit the draft again (tighten a rule), choose Save draft, and create version 2. Open the prompt's detail page and confirm both versions are listed. Try to find a way to edit version 1: you cannot, which is the point.
    9. Optional, from your machine: run the Converse snippet from this chapter with modelId set to the version 1 ARN and then the version 2 ARN, and compare results. Your role needs bedrock:RenderPrompt and bedrock:InvokeModel. Without RenderPrompt you will get an access denied error even though the model itself is allowed.
    10. Open CloudTrail in the console, choose Event history, and filter by event name CreatePromptVersion. Find your own action, and note the user identity, time, and source IP. That is the audit trail for a prompt change.

    Cleanup. Keep the prompt if you plan to do the Prompt Flows lab later in this chapter, because it will reuse it. Otherwise, return to Prompt management, select billing-answer, and delete it. A prompt with no traffic costs nothing to keep.

    Quality assurance for prompts (skill 1.6.4)

    Traditional software has unit tests because functions are deterministic. Prompts are not. The same prompt can return different text on two runs, and a change that improves one input can quietly break another. A prompt that passed review in March can fail in June because the provider updated the model or because a teammate edited a shared template. Quality assurance for prompts therefore means building a repeatable harness that runs a fixed set of inputs through the prompt and checks the outputs against rules, every time anything changes.

    What you can check automatically

    Not every property of a good answer can be tested by code, but many can, and those are the cheap, fast, high-value checks. They fall into a few groups.

    • Structure. Is the output valid JSON? Does it match the schema, with the required keys and allowed enum values? This is the most common production failure, because one stray sentence before the JSON breaks the parser.
    • Constraints. Is it under the length limit? Does it avoid forbidden phrases? Does it contain a required disclaimer, found with a regular expression?
    • Grounding hints. Do the cited IDs exist in the retrieved context? Is every number in the answer present in the source?
    • Behavior on edge cases. Does the model refuse correctly, return the exact fallback sentence when the answer is missing, and resist an injected instruction?
    • Judged quality. For tone and helpfulness, use a second model as a grader (LLM-as-a-judge). Chapter-level detail on this belongs to Domain 5, but you can include a judge step in the same harness.

    Lambda as the output verifier

    AWS Lambda runs a short function on demand without servers to manage. It is the natural home for verification logic because it is small, stateless, cheap, and callable from Step Functions, API Gateway, or your own code. A verifier receives the model output and returns pass or fail with reasons.

    import json, re
from jsonschema import validate, ValidationError   # package this with the function

SCHEMA = {
    "type": "object",
    "required": ["answer", "confidence"],
    "properties": {
        "answer": {"type": "string", "maxLength": 600},
        "confidence": {"enum": ["high", "low"]},
    },
    "additionalProperties": False,
}
BANNED = re.compile(r"\b(guarantee|legal advice)\b", re.I)

def handler(event, context):
    raw = event["model_output"]
    try:
        data = json.loads(raw)
        validate(data, SCHEMA)
    except (json.JSONDecodeError, ValidationError) as exc:
        return {"passed": False, "reason": f"schema: {exc}"}
    if BANNED.search(data["answer"]):
        return {"passed": False, "reason": "banned phrase"}
    return {"passed": True, "reason": "ok"}

    Use the same verifier in two places. In the test harness it scores the prompt before release. In the live request path it checks each response, and on failure the application retries once with a repair instruction ("your last reply was not valid JSON, reply again with only the JSON") or falls back to a safe default. That live use is the "post-processing validation" layer you will see again in the defense-in-depth design of Domain 3.

    Step Functions as the test harness

    For a suite of tests, Step Functions orchestrates the run. A Map state iterates over a list of test cases, and by setting a maximum concurrency you run many cases in parallel without writing a thread pool. For each case the state machine calls Bedrock (directly through the optimized integration, or through a Lambda that uses the managed prompt ARN), passes the output to the verifier Lambda, and records the result. At the end, an aggregation state computes the pass rate and fails the workflow if it is below a threshold, which turns the whole thing into a quality gate you can place in a pipeline.

    The test cases themselves are your golden dataset: a curated set of inputs with the expected properties of the output (not necessarily exact expected text, since wording varies). Store it in S3 as JSON Lines. Good golden sets are built from real production questions, include the awkward cases (empty input, very long input, other languages, prompt-injection attempts, questions that must be refused), and grow every time a production bug is found. Each bug becomes a permanent regression test.

    Regression tracking with CloudWatch

    Amazon CloudWatch is AWS's metrics, logs, and alarms service. A test run should publish its results as custom metrics so you can see quality over time rather than a pass or fail in one log. Publish the pass rate, the schema-failure rate, the average output length, and the average token count, with a dimension for the prompt name and version. Then build a CloudWatch alarm on the pass rate: if it drops below your threshold after a prompt or model change, the alarm fires and the pipeline blocks the release.

    import boto3
cw = boto3.client("cloudwatch")

def publish(prompt_name: str, version: str, passed: int, total: int) -> None:
    cw.put_metric_data(
        Namespace="PromptQA",
        MetricData=[{
            "MetricName": "PassRate",
            "Dimensions": [{"Name": "Prompt", "Value": prompt_name},
                           {"Name": "Version", "Value": version}],
            "Value": 100.0 * passed / total,
            "Unit": "Percent",
        }],
    )

    Run this suite on three triggers: when a prompt version is proposed, when you change the model or the inference parameters, and on a schedule against production (a nightly run catches silent changes in model behavior). CloudWatch Synthetics, which runs scripted canaries on a schedule, is another way to execute a recurring synthetic request against your live endpoint.

    Exam tip

    Map the tool to the job. Lambda verifies a single output against rules. Step Functions runs many cases, in parallel, with a gate at the end. A golden dataset in S3 supplies the cases. CloudWatch custom metrics and alarms track regression over time. If a question asks how to "detect prompt regressions after a model update," the answer is a stored golden dataset replayed automatically with results compared to a baseline, not manual spot-checks.

    Iterative refinement beyond basic prompting (skill 1.6.5)

    You know the basics: be specific, give context, give examples. This section is about the techniques that move a prompt from "usually fine" to "reliable enough to ship," and about the loop that keeps improving it after launch. Treat it as a toolbox. The exam does not ask you to recite the techniques so much as to pick the one that fits a described failure.

    Structured input with tags

    When a prompt mixes instructions, retrieved documents, and user text, the model has to work out which is which. Wrapping each part in descriptive XML-style tags removes the guesswork. Claude models in particular respond well to this, and it works across model families. It also gives your instructions something to refer to ("summarize only the text in the <document> tags"), and it makes prompts easier to review and diff.

    Explicit output specifications

    Say what the output looks like, and show it. The most reliable pattern pairs a schema description with one complete example. If you need exactly one of a fixed set of values, list them. If the answer must be absent when information is missing, specify the exact fallback string, so your code can detect it. Compare these two versions of a support-ticket classifier.

    Before
    Look at this support ticket and tell me what category it is and how urgent it is.

{{ticket_text}}

    This produces friendly prose that varies per run ("This looks like a billing issue and seems fairly urgent!"), which your code cannot reliably parse.

    After
    You classify support tickets for a SaaS company.

<ticket>
{{ticket_text}}
</ticket>

Classify the ticket in the tags above.
- category: one of "billing", "outage", "access", "feature_request", "other"
- urgency: one of "low", "normal", "high". Use "high" only if the customer cannot work at all.

Reply with only JSON and nothing else, in this shape:
{"category": "billing", "urgency": "normal", "reason": "one short sentence"}

Example input: "I was charged twice and need it fixed before payroll runs tomorrow."
Example output: {"category": "billing", "urgency": "high", "reason": "Duplicate charge with a stated deadline."}

    The second prompt defines the role, delimits the data, enumerates allowed values, defines what "high" means, fixes the shape, and demonstrates it once. Failures now become detectable: anything that does not parse or has an out-of-set value is a verifier failure from the previous section.

    Few-shot examples

    Examples teach format and judgment faster than rules. Two to five varied examples usually beat a long paragraph of rules, and they should include a hard case or a refusal case, not only easy ones. Two cautions. Examples cost input tokens on every call, so for a prompt called millions of times they are a real expense, and they are an ideal candidate for prompt caching since they are a stable prefix. And the model will copy the examples' surface patterns, so if every example answer is three sentences you will get three sentences.

    Chain-of-thought, and when it costs more than it is worth

    Asking the model to reason step by step before answering improves accuracy on multi-step problems such as arithmetic, policy eligibility rules, and multi-document comparison. The way to use it in a pipeline is to ask for the reasoning in one tag and the final answer in another, then extract only the answer.

    Think through the eligibility rules step by step inside <reasoning> tags.
Then give your final decision inside <answer> tags as "approve" or "deny".

    The costs are real. Reasoning tokens are output tokens, which are priced higher than input tokens, and they add latency. For simple classification or extraction, chain-of-thought adds cost with no accuracy gain. Some newer models can also reason internally with a thinking or adaptive-reasoning setting, in which case you configure the budget through the API instead of prompting for it. As a rule of thumb: use it for tasks where a human would need scratch paper, skip it for lookups and formatting.

    Prefill, and what replaced it

    An older technique was prefill: you end the messages list with the start of the assistant's reply, such as an opening brace, and the model continues from there, which forces JSON. It worked well on earlier Claude models. On Claude 4.6 and later on Bedrock, prefill is not supported and returns a hard 400 error, so any prompt library that relies on it breaks when you upgrade the model. The replacement is to enforce the format through the API: a tool definition with a JSON schema, or the structured output setting on the Converse request, plus clear instructions in the system prompt. This is a good example of why prompts need tests: a model migration can fail loudly or silently, and your regression suite is how you find out before users do.

    Step-back and self-critique

    Two more patterns show up in advanced prompting. Step-back prompting asks the model to first state the general principle or the relevant policy, and only then apply it to the specific case, which reduces errors on questions where the model jumps to a surface answer. Self-critique adds a second pass: generate a draft, then ask the model (or a cheaper one) to check the draft against a checklist and revise. Self-critique doubles your calls, so reserve it for high-stakes outputs. It pairs naturally with a Prompt Flow, where the draft node feeds a critique node.

    Feedback loops

    Refinement does not end at launch. Real users generate the best evidence of where a prompt fails. Add a thumbs-up and thumbs-down control to the interface, and store each rating with the request ID, the prompt version, and the model ID, for example in DynamoDB or in a log record. Review the low-rated conversations weekly, find the pattern, change the prompt in a new draft, add the failing cases to the golden dataset, run the regression suite, and ship the new version through the approval path from the governance section. Because every response is tagged with the prompt version, you can compare satisfaction between version 3 and version 4 directly, which is real A/B evidence rather than anecdote.

    Watch out

    Change one thing at a time. If you reword the prompt, switch the model, and change the temperature in the same release, you cannot tell which change helped or hurt. Prompt Management variants make this discipline easy: vary one setting per variant and compare on the same test inputs.

    Exam tip

    Match the technique to the symptom. Inconsistent format: explicit schema, example, and API-enforced structure. Wrong answers on multi-step logic: chain-of-thought. Model confuses data with instructions: tags and delimiters. Answers invent facts: ground with retrieved context and an explicit "say you do not know" rule, then verify. Costs too high: shorter prompt, caching, fewer examples, or skip chain-of-thought for simple tasks.

    Complex prompt systems with Prompt Flows (skill 1.6.6)

    Some tasks do not fit in one prompt. Summarizing a support ticket, classifying it, looking up the right policy, and drafting a reply is four steps with different instructions, possibly different models, and a decision in the middle. You can write that as a Python function that calls Converse four times, and for many teams that is the right answer. Amazon Bedrock Prompt Flows is the managed alternative: a visual, node-based workflow builder where each step is a node, the connections between nodes carry data, and the whole graph is a versioned, deployable resource you can invoke through an API.

    Note

    The exam guide says "Amazon Bedrock Prompt Flows". The AWS documentation and the console now call the same feature Amazon Bedrock Flows, and the left navigation item uses that name. If you see either name in a question, it is the same service.

    Backend analogy

    A flow is a small DAG executor, comparable to a LangGraph graph or an Airflow DAG, but with a drag-and-drop editor and AWS-managed hosting. You give up arbitrary Python in exchange for no orchestration code to run, and a builder that non-developers can read.

    Nodes and connections

    A flow is made of nodes. Every flow has exactly one Flow input node, where the invocation payload enters, and one or more Flow output nodes, where results leave (a flow with several branches can have several output nodes). Between them you place the nodes that do work. The ones you should recognize for the exam are these.

    NodeWhat it doesTypical use
    PromptRuns a prompt against a model. The template can be defined in the node or pulled from Prompt Management.Classify, summarize, rewrite, draft
    Knowledge baseQueries a Bedrock Knowledge Base and returns retrieved results or a generated answer.The retrieval step of a RAG flow
    Lambda functionRuns your Lambda code with the node's inputs.Pre-processing, post-processing, validation, calls to your own systems
    ConditionEvaluates named conditions in order on its inputs and routes execution down the first one that is true, with a default branch for when none match.Route by category, confidence, or length
    Iterator and CollectorIterator splits an array into items and processes them one by one, not in parallel; Collector gathers the results back into an array.Summarize each document in a list
    DoWhile loopRepeats a sequence of nodes while a condition stays true. It always runs once, and stops at maxIterations (default 10).Retry or refine until a check passes
    Inline codeRuns Python 3 code inside the flow in an isolated environment with no internet access. Preview feature, and not supported in asynchronous flow execution.Light data transformation without a separate Lambda
    AgentInvokes a Bedrock Agent as a step.A step that needs tool use inside a larger flow
    LexSends input to an Amazon Lex bot.Structured intent and slot handling
    S3 storage and retrievalWrites data to, or reads an object from, an S3 bucket.Persist results, load a template or document

    The node list changes over time, so treat this table as the core rather than a permanent complete list. Nodes connect in two ways. A data connection passes an output value from one node into a named input of another. A conditional connection leaves a Condition node along a specific branch. Each node input has a name, a type (String, Number, Boolean, Object, or Array), and an expression that extracts the part of the incoming value to use, such as $.data.retailPrice. Bedrock validates the extracted value against the declared type at runtime.

    The Condition node deserves a closer look because it is where most flow bugs live. It has one or more named inputs, and each condition is a boolean expression that refers to those input names. You can compare with ==, !=, >, >=, <, and <= (the ordering operators work on numbers, equality also works on strings and booleans), and combine tests with and, or, and not. A typical condition reads (retailPrice > 10) and (type == "produce"). Conditions are checked in the order you list them, and when several are true the earlier one wins. The Condition node has no outputs of its own: you attach each condition, and the default condition, to the downstream node that should receive the data.

    flowchart LR
    I["Input node (ticket text)"] --> P1["Prompt: classify ticket"]
    P1 --> C{"Condition node"}
    C -- "category is billing" --> KB["Knowledge base: billing policy"]
    KB --> P2["Prompt: draft billing reply"]
    C -- "default" --> P3["Prompt: draft general reply"]
    P2 --> L["Lambda: validate and redact"]
    P3 --> L
    L --> O["Output node (reply)"]

    Sequential chains, branching, and reuse

    Three design patterns cover most flows.

    • Sequential chain. Output of one prompt becomes input of the next: extract facts, then summarize the facts, then translate the summary. Splitting a big prompt into a chain makes each step simpler and testable, and lets you use a cheaper model for the easy steps.
    • Conditional branching on model output. A first prompt classifies, a Condition node inspects the result, and different branches handle different cases. The weak point is the string comparison: if the classifier replies "Billing." with a period and your condition expects billing, the flow silently takes the default branch. Instruct the classifier to reply with a single word from a fixed list, and put a Lambda node in front of the condition to normalize the text when you cannot trust the format.
    • Reusable components. A Prompt node can reference a managed prompt from Prompt Management by version. The same "classify ticket" prompt can then appear in several flows and be governed in one place. This is the "reusable prompt components" item in the exam guide.

    Pre-processing and post-processing are what Lambda nodes are for. Before the model, a Lambda can clean the input, strip markup, truncate to a token budget, or fetch customer records. After the model, a Lambda can validate the JSON, redact sensitive values, or reformat the text for the channel. These are the same verification patterns from the quality assurance section, placed inside the graph.

    Versions and aliases

    Flows follow the same draft-and-version discipline as prompts, with one more concept. A new flow starts with a working DRAFT and a test alias named TSTALIASID that points at it, which is what the console test pane and your early experiments use. When you are ready, you create an immutable version (numbered from 1) and point an alias at it. Applications invoke the alias, not a version number. To roll out a new flow you create a version and move the alias to it. To roll back you move the alias back. The application code and its configuration never change. This is the same decoupling that Lambda aliases provide, and it is a common exam signal for "release without redeploying the client."

    Invoking a flow from code

    Execution uses the bedrock-agent-runtime client (the data plane for agents, knowledge bases, and flows), and the result is a stream of events. The inputs list names the Flow input node (FlowInputNode by default) and its output, which is always called document. Each flowOutputEvent carries the output node's name and content, and the final flowCompletionEvent has a completionReason of SUCCESS, or INPUT_REQUIRED when an Agent node paused to ask the user for more information. Pass enableTrace=True to receive trace events that show which node ran and what it returned, which is the first tool to reach for when debugging.

    import boto3

runtime = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime")

resp = runtime.invoke_flow(
    flowIdentifier="FLOWID12345",
    flowAliasIdentifier="ALIASID123",
    inputs=[{
        "content": {"document": "I was charged twice and need it fixed today."},
        "nodeName": "FlowInputNode",
        "nodeOutputName": "document",
    }],
)

for event in resp["responseStream"]:
    if "flowOutputEvent" in event:
        print(event["flowOutputEvent"]["content"]["document"])
    elif "flowCompletionEvent" in event:
        print("done:", event["flowCompletionEvent"]["completionReason"])

    Flows, Step Functions, and Agents

    Three services orchestrate multi-step work, and scenario questions are written to make you choose. The distinctions are worth learning as a table.

    ChooseWhenKey trait
    Prompt FlowsA mostly fixed pipeline of prompts, retrieval, and light logic that you want to build visually and invoke by APIPrompt-centric, no-code builder, versions and aliases
    Step FunctionsWorkflows with durable waits, human approval, retries, error handling, parallel fan-out, or many non-AI servicesGeneral-purpose orchestration, runs up to a year, full audit of each state
    Bedrock Agents or AgentCoreThe model should decide at runtime which tools to call and in what orderModel-driven control flow rather than designed control flow

    Exam tip

    "Sequential prompt chain", "conditional branching based on model response", "no-code", and "reusable prompt components" together describe Prompt Flows. "Wait for a human approval for days" or "retry with backoff and catch errors across many services" describes Step Functions. If the path through the steps is decided by the model rather than by you, that is an agent, not a flow.

    Hands-on lab

    Build a classify-then-route Prompt Flow and test it

    About 30 minutes · Cost: cents of model tokens, plus any Flows usage charge, which the Bedrock pricing page should list but which could not be confirmed when this guide was written · Needs: a text model enabled in your selected Region

    1. In the Amazon Bedrock console, select Amazon Bedrock Flows in the left navigation pane, then choose Create flow. Name it ticket-router. For Service role name, choose Create and use a new service role. This is the IAM role that lets the flow call models on your behalf. Choose Create.
    2. The console builds a starter flow for you: a Flow input node, a Prompt node, and a Flow output node, already connected. Select the Prompt node and rename it classify. Select the Flow input node and set its Type to String in the Configure tab.
    3. With classify selected, open the Configurations section, keep Define in node selected, and use Select a model to pick a small, inexpensive model. Replace the prompt text with this template, where the double-brace name becomes the node's input: 
      Classify the support ticket below as exactly one word from this list:
billing, outage, other.
Reply with the single word only. No punctuation.

<ticket>
{{ticket}}
</ticket>
    4. Make sure the Flow input node's document output connects to the ticket input of classify, and that the classify output connects to the Flow output node's document input. Choose Save.
    5. In the Test flow pane on the right, enter "I was charged twice this month" and choose Run. You should see a single word. That is your input to prompt to output chain.
    6. Now add branching. In the flow builder's Nodes tab, drag a Condition node onto the canvas. In its Configure tab, add one input named category with type String, and connect the classify output to it. Under Conditions, name the first condition isBilling and enter the expression category == "billing". The default condition already exists and catches everything else.
    7. Add two more Prompt nodes, billingReply ("Write a two-sentence reply to a billing complaint about: {{ticket}}") and generalReply ("Write a two-sentence acknowledgement of this message: {{ticket}}"). Connect the isBilling condition to billingReply and the default condition to generalReply. Connect the Flow input's document to the ticket input of both reply nodes. Add a second Flow output node, connect each reply node to its own output node, and delete the direct classify to Flow output connection.
    8. Choose Save and test again with a billing ticket, then with "The site has been down for an hour". Compare the two replies to see which branch ran. If every input goes to the default branch, the classifier output probably does not exactly match "billing", which is the string-comparison pitfall described earlier.
    9. Create a version of the flow from the working draft, then create an alias named prod that points at that version. Note the flow ID and alias ID. Optionally run the invoke_flow snippet from this chapter with them. Your role needs permission to invoke the flow, and note that the flow's service role, not your role, is what calls the models.

    Cleanup. Delete the alias first, then the flow version, then the flow itself from Amazon Bedrock Flows. Delete the billing-answer prompt from the earlier lab if you no longer need it. In IAM, delete the service role the console created for the flow if nothing else uses it. These resources have no standing cost, but removing them keeps the project tidy.

    Chapter recap

    • Put durable instructions in the system field and per-request data in the user message. Use Converse so the same structure works across models, and always set maxTokens.
    • Prompts guide behavior, Guardrails enforce policy. "Must never" wants a guardrail, "should format as" wants a schema, a tool definition, or structured output.
    • Converse is stateless. Keep history in DynamoDB with a session key and a TTL attribute, and control cost with a sliding window, summarization, or retrieval over past turns.
    • Step Functions Standard workflows with a callback task token pause a clarification loop for the user without running compute. Bound the loop. Comprehend custom classification or Lex handles intent when you want a cheap, deterministic classifier.
    • Prompt Management stores parameterized prompts with variants and immutable versions. Production should pin a version ARN, and a managed prompt ARN replaces the model ID in Converse.
    • Governance is layered: IAM scopes who edits (CreatePromptVersion) and who invokes (RenderPrompt plus InvokeModel), CloudTrail records changes by default and prompt use only with AWS::Bedrock::Prompt data events, model invocation logging in CloudWatch Logs or S3 records the actual text, and S3 or Git holds templates when the pipeline demands it.
    • Prompt QA is a golden dataset plus automated checks: Lambda verifies outputs, a Step Functions Map state runs cases in parallel, and CloudWatch metrics and alarms catch regressions after any prompt or model change.
    • Refinement tools: tags for structure, explicit schemas and examples for format, chain-of-thought for multi-step reasoning at a token cost, and feedback loops tied to prompt version. Prefill is unsupported on newer Claude models.
    • Prompt Flows chain prompts, retrieval, Lambda, and conditions into a versioned graph with aliases, invoked through invoke_flow. Choose Step Functions for durable, human-in-the-loop, multi-service work and agents when the model should choose the path.

    Knowledge check

    1. A company wants marketing staff to adjust the tone of a customer-facing assistant without involving developers. Every change must be reviewed before release, production must always run a reviewed version, and the team must be able to roll back in minutes. Which approach meets these requirements with the least custom build effort?

      Answer

      Store the prompt in Amazon Bedrock Prompt Management with variables, let editors work on the draft, create an immutable version after review, and have the application invoke the version ARN (kept in AppConfig or application configuration). Rolling back means pointing at the previous version. Hard-coding the template in the application forces a deployment for every change. Referencing the draft in production bypasses review. A custom S3 and DynamoDB solution works but is far more to build, and it does not give you versioned variants and comparison for free.

    2. A chatbot runs on Lambda and answers each request independently, so users complain that it forgets what they said one message earlier. Compliance requires that transcripts be deleted after 30 days. Which design fixes both problems?

      Answer

      Store each turn in DynamoDB keyed by a server-generated session ID with a sort key for turn order, load recent turns and send them in the Converse messages array on each call, and set a TTL attribute 30 days out so DynamoDB deletes old items. Remember that TTL deletion is not instant, so filter on the expiry when reading if exactness matters. Increasing Lambda memory does nothing for statelessness, a larger model does not remember anything between calls, and storing history in Lambda's local storage is lost between invocations.

    3. A workflow must ask the user a clarifying question when a request is missing information, then continue when the user replies. The reply may take hours. Which implementation is best?

      Answer

      A Step Functions Standard workflow that uses the wait-for-callback pattern with a task token. The workflow pauses with no compute running, and the application calls SendTaskSuccess with the token when the user responds. A Lambda function that sleeps is limited to 15 minutes and wastes money. Express workflows do not support the callback pattern and are meant for short runs. Polling a database from a Lambda cron adds complexity that Step Functions already solves.

    4. After a model upgrade, a JSON-returning assistant occasionally emits a sentence before the JSON, which breaks the parser. The team wants to catch this kind of regression before release in future. What should they build?

      Answer

      A golden dataset of representative and edge-case inputs stored in S3, replayed automatically by a Step Functions workflow with a Map state, with a Lambda verifier that validates each output against the JSON schema, and results published as CloudWatch custom metrics with an alarm that blocks release when the pass rate drops. For a lasting fix, enforce structure through the API (a tool schema or structured output) rather than relying on instructions alone. Manual spot-checking does not scale or repeat, and asking the model to "please only return JSON" more forcefully is the fragile approach that failed here.

    5. An auditor asks two things: who changed the approved refund prompt last month, and what text was actually sent to and received from the model for a disputed customer request. Which combination answers both?

      Answer

      AWS CloudTrail for the first question, because prompt create, update, and version actions are API calls that record the principal, time, and source. Bedrock model invocation logging to CloudWatch Logs or S3 for the second, because it captures request and response content. Secure the logs with KMS encryption, restricted access, and retention settings because they can contain personal data. CloudTrail alone does not show full prompt and response text, and invocation logs alone do not tell you who edited a prompt.

    6. A team needs a pipeline that classifies an incoming ticket, queries a knowledge base only for billing tickets, drafts a reply, and then validates the reply with custom code. They want a visual builder, versioned releases, and the ability to move production to a new release without changing client code. What should they use?

      Answer

      Amazon Bedrock Prompt Flows: a Prompt node to classify, a Condition node to branch, a Knowledge base node on the billing branch, a Prompt node to draft, and a Lambda node to validate. Publish a version, point an alias at it, and have clients invoke the alias, so a release is a change of alias target. A single large prompt cannot branch or call a knowledge base conditionally. Step Functions could do it but is not prompt-centric or no-code, and a Bedrock Agent is for cases where the model chooses the steps, which is not required here because the path is fixed by design.

    Project workshop 3 · Build after Chapter 6

    Project 3: A governed prompt registry and triage flow

    Chapter 6 treated prompts as production assets. This workshop builds the machinery around that idea: prompts stored and versioned outside the code, tested automatically before release, approved by a person, audited afterwards, and chained into a small multi-step flow with a clarification loop.

    Exam skills practiced

    • 1.6.1 instruction frameworks and templates
    • 1.6.2 conversation context and clarification workflows
    • 1.6.3 prompt management and governance
    • 1.6.4 quality assurance and regression testing
    • 1.6.5 and 1.6.6 iterative refinement and complex prompt systems

    Problem statement

    The scenario

    A support organization runs an AI assistant that triages incoming customer messages. Last month an engineer edited the system prompt string inside the application to make replies "friendlier". The assistant then began promising refunds it was not allowed to give. Nobody could say who changed the prompt, when it went live, or which conversations were affected, and rolling back meant a redeploy. Leadership now requires the following.

    • Prompts live outside application code, with versions and parameters.
    • No prompt version goes live without passing an automated test suite and a named human approval.
    • Rolling back is a pointer change that takes seconds.
    • Every conversation record shows which prompt version produced each reply.
    • The triage logic runs as a controlled multi-step flow: classify the message, route it, draft a reply, and check the reply. If key details are missing, the assistant asks a clarifying question first.

    Governance questions are a recurring exam theme. The typical stem says a company needs "consistency", "oversight", "an audit trail", or "approval before changes". The best answer combines Bedrock Prompt Management for versions, Step Functions or Lambda for testing, CloudTrail and CloudWatch Logs for audit, and a guardrail for safety. This project makes you wire those pieces together once so the answer feels obvious.

    Solution architecture

    The first diagram is the release path. It runs whenever someone proposes a prompt change.

    flowchart LR
  A["Git repo: prompt specs + test cases"] --> B["CI job"]
  B --> C["Bedrock Prompt Management: create prompt version"]
  C --> D["Step Functions: regression test run"]
  D --> E["Lambda: invoke model on each test case"]
  E --> F["Lambda: validators (schema, forbidden phrases, judge)"]
  F --> G["Results: S3 report + CloudWatch metrics"]
  G -->|"pass"| H["Approval step: SNS email, wait for human decision"]
  G -->|"fail"| X["Reject and report"]
  H -->|"approved"| I["DynamoDB: active version pointer + audit record"]
  H -->|"rejected"| X

    The second is the runtime path. It reads only the approved pointer, so a version nobody approved cannot reach a customer.

    flowchart LR
  U["Customer message"] --> API["FastAPI triage service"]
  API --> P["DynamoDB: active prompt versions"]
  API --> H["DynamoDB: conversation history"]
  API --> F["Bedrock Flow: classify, route, draft, check"]
  F --> G["Guardrail on input and output"]
  F --> Q{"Missing details?"}
  Q -->|"yes"| C["Ask clarifying question"]
  Q -->|"no"| R["Final reply"]
  API --> L["CloudWatch Logs: prompt id + version per reply"]
  F --> T["CloudTrail: data events for prompt and flow use"]

    The triage flow has four stages. A classifier prompt labels the message (billing, technical, account, complaint, unclear). A condition then routes it. Each route uses its own reply prompt, and a final checker prompt reviews the draft against policy and returns a pass or a reason. In Bedrock Flows these are prompt nodes joined by a condition node. You can also build the same graph in Step Functions, and doing both is a good way to feel the trade-off between a visual prompt chain and a general workflow engine.

    Backend analogy

    The release path is a deployment pipeline for text. The active-version pointer is a feature flag. The regression suite is your unit and integration tests. The approval step is a protected-branch review. You are applying ordinary release discipline to an artifact most teams still edit in production.

    Technology and services

    PieceWhat you useWhy this choiceRead first
    Prompt storageBedrock Prompt Management with variables and numbered versions; Git for the source specsVersioned, parameterized templates; Git gives review history.Prompt management
    Instruction designRole, rules, output format, and examples in one standard templateConsistent behavior across all prompts.Instruction frameworks
    ChainingBedrock Flows with prompt and condition nodesSequential prompts and branching without custom orchestration code.Prompt flows
    Quality gateStep Functions Map state, Lambda validators, optional judge modelParallel test runs with auditable results.Prompt QA
    ApprovalStep Functions task token with SNS notificationPauses a release until a person responds, with a record.Prompt QA
    Active pointerDynamoDB table keyed by prompt name, or an AppConfig profileRollback is a one-item write.Prompt management
    Conversation stateDynamoDB, with prompt version stored on each assistant turnTraceability and memory in one place.Interactive systems
    SafetyBedrock Guardrails attached to the flow and the direct callsPolicy enforced outside the prompt text.Chapter overview
    AuditCloudTrail data events, CloudWatch Logs, structured log line per replyAnswers who changed what and which version served which user.Prompt management
    Service layerFastAPI in a container, infrastructure as codeYour standard stack.Deployment strategies

    Build path

    Suggested folder name: prompt-governance-service. Keep the project small enough that the governance machinery is the focus, not the triage domain.

    1. Standard template and four promptsWrite one template skeleton (role, rules, refusal conditions, output format) and four prompts from it: classifier, billing reply, technical reply, checker. Keep them as files in Git with a small header for name, owner, and variables.
    2. Register and versionWrite a script that creates or updates each prompt in Prompt Management and creates a numbered version. Call each version through converse with variables filled in. Confirm that editing the draft does not change a published version.
    3. Test cases and validatorsCreate 40 or more cases: normal messages, ambiguous ones, hostile ones, and ones that try to extract policy text. Write validators for JSON schema, forbidden phrases (for example "guarantee a refund"), length, and language. Add an optional judge-model check for tone.
    4. Regression pipelineBuild the Step Functions workflow that runs every case through a candidate version in parallel, aggregates a pass rate, and writes a report to S3 and a metric to CloudWatch. Set a threshold such as 95% and compare each run with the currently active version.
    5. Approval and pointerAdd the human approval step with an SNS email. On approval, write the active-version pointer and an audit item that records who approved, which version, the test report location, and the time. On rejection, record the reason.
    6. Runtime serviceBuild the FastAPI service. It reads the pointer, renders the prompt, applies the guardrail, calls the model, and writes each turn to DynamoDB along with the prompt name and version. Prove that rolling back means writing one item.
    7. Flow and clarification loopBuild the classify, route, draft, check flow in Bedrock Flows and invoke it from the service. Add the clarification path: when the classifier returns "unclear" or a required detail such as an order number is missing, the service returns a question and stores the pending state so the next message continues the same case.
    8. Audit reviewTurn on CloudTrail data events for the prompt and flow actions. Then answer these from logs alone: who approved the current billing prompt, which conversations used the previous version, and which user ran the last release.
    9. Planted regressionEdit a prompt to add "be as friendly as possible and agree with the customer". Push it through the pipeline and confirm that your tests catch the refund promise and block the release. Keep that run as your evidence.

    Definition of done

    • No prompt text appears in application source files, only prompt identifiers and version pointers.
    • A version that fails the suite cannot become active, and a passing version still waits for a recorded human decision.
    • Rollback takes one write and the next request uses the older version.
    • For any past reply you can name the prompt version, the guardrail, and the approver of that version.
    • The planted-regression run is blocked, and you can explain which test caught it.

    Cost, Region, and account notes

    Watch out

    Model invocation calls such as Converse and InvokeModel appear in CloudTrail by default without the prompt text, while prompt rendering and flow invocation are data events that you must enable and that carry their own CloudTrail charge. Enable them for the test period only, then review the cost. Do not turn on invocation logging of full prompts for real customer data without a retention and access plan, because the log store then holds the sensitive text. Also check the supported-services list for Step Functions, SNS, and CloudTrail data events on your plan before you start.

    Model cost stays low if you use a small model for the regression suite and cap output tokens. Bedrock Flows and Prompt Management have their own pricing and quotas, so read the current pages before running large test batches, and prefer a smaller suite while you iterate.

    Stretch ideas

    Add prompt variants for an A/B test, with a metric for escalation rate. Add a nightly job that replays recent real conversations against the active prompt and alerts when the pass rate drops, which is the exam's "prompt regression" idea in production form. Connect the clarification state to a handoff queue for a human agent, which leads into Domain 2.

    Project workshop 4 · After the whole domain

    Choosing your projects and covering every skill

    The three workshops are independent on purpose. Each is small enough to finish, and together they exercise almost every skill in Domain 1. This closing section shows the coverage, the order I suggest, the gaps to fill with short labs, and how to combine the three into one larger application if you want a single showpiece.

    Skill coverage map

    P1 is the model gateway, P2 the document knowledge platform, and P3 the prompt registry. Two skills are better learned another way, and the next section covers them.

    1.3.4Improving input qualityP2 enrichment step
    1.4.1Vector store architectureP2 milestones 2, 7, and 9
    1.4.2Metadata frameworksP2 metadata schema and filters

    1.6.5Iterative refinementP3 milestone 9 and stretch
    1.6.6Complex prompt systemsP3 milestone 7

    What the projects leave out

    Skill 1.2.4, deploying and versioning customized models, is the one real gap. Building a fine-tuning pipeline for practice is expensive and only weakly useful, because the exam tests knowing which service does what and the order of the lifecycle. Cover it with the customization section in Chapter 2 and with a table that you write yourself: for each customization method, how the model is hosted, how it is versioned, how a rollback works, and what it costs while idle. The scale techniques in 1.4.3 are also hard to show with a small corpus, so read the sharding and multi-index sections of Chapter 4 and sketch how your Project 2 design would change at ten million chunks. Those two items, plus rereading the knowledge checks, close the domain.

    Suggested order and effort

    1. Project 1, after Chapter 2The smallest project, and its gateway pattern is the most reusable. Allow roughly a week of evenings. It teaches the account setup and deployment habits that the other two reuse.
    2. Project 2, after Chapter 5The largest and the most exam-relevant. Allow two to three weeks. Do milestones 1 and 2 first to get a text-only system running, and treat every later milestone as an increment.
    3. Project 3, after Chapter 6Allow about a week. It is mostly workflow and discipline, and it makes a good final exercise because it ties prompts to testing and audit.

    These are rough planning figures for part-time work. Adjust them to your own pace, and stop a project at any milestone if it already taught you what you needed.

    Optional capstone: one governed knowledge assistant

    The idea

    A fourth, separate project that combines the three into one product: employees ask questions in a chat endpoint. The service routes each model call through the gateway logic from Project 1, retrieves cited, permission-filtered passages with the Project 2 retrieval service, and uses governed, versioned prompts from Project 3 with a guardrail and an audit trail.

    flowchart LR
  U["Employee"] --> API["FastAPI assistant service"]
  API --> PR["Prompt registry: active version"]
  API --> RT["Retrieval service: filter, hybrid, rerank"]
  RT --> VS["Vector store + metadata"]
  API --> GW["Model gateway: routing, fallback, metering"]
  GW --> BR["Bedrock models + guardrail"]
  API --> DB["DynamoDB: conversations + prompt version per turn"]
  ING["Ingestion pipeline"] --> VS
  REL["Prompt release pipeline"] --> PR
  OBS["CloudWatch + CloudTrail"] --- API

    The repository's rule is that each project is independent and has its own dependencies and infrastructure. For the capstone, copy the few modules you need into the new folder and keep them as local code. Do not import across project folders. If you notice that you copied the same module a third time, that is the moment to consider extracting a shared package, and not before.

    Deploy the capstone as a container behind a load balancer, with secrets in Secrets Manager and an IAM role scoped to the models, tables, and buckets it uses. That step belongs to Domain 2 and beyond, so Domain 1 only needs the design, the retrieval, and the governance to work locally and in a small deployment. When you finish the three workshops you will have built nearly everything this domain tests, and the capstone shows you can combine it.

    Exam tip

    After each project, write a three-line summary of the decisions you made and the reason for each, for example "chose S3 Vectors first for zero idle cost, moved to Aurora pgvector for hybrid search". Exam scenarios reward exactly this kind of constraint-driven reasoning, and your own build history gives you real examples to recall under pressure.
